 Sharp noncommutative Hilbert and Riesz inequalities:
 classification and spectral corrections Thanks:  ∗ Department of Mathematical Sciences, Tsinghua University,
Beijing 100084, China.
 E-mail:  longzy25@mails.tsinghua.edu.cn 
 
 
 Zhouyu Long ∗ 
 

 Abstract. 
    
 For every  1 < p < ∞ 1<p<\infty , with  p ∗ = max ⁡ { p , p / ( p − 1 ) } p^{*}=\max\{p,p/(p-1)\} ,
we determine the sharp Hilbert norm  C p = cot ⁡ ( π / ( 2 ​ p ∗ ) ) C_{p}=\cot(\pi/(2p^{*})) 
on  L p ​ ( ℝ , 𝒮 p ) L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{S}_{p})  and the sharp uniform bound  D p = csc ⁡ ( π / p ) D_{p}=\csc(\pi/p) 
for retained and strict Riesz projections of maximal subdiagonal
algebras in finite tracial von Neumann algebras.
The Hilbert value is also the common Fourier–Schur completely
bounded norm.
For finite matrices, a nonnegative logarithmic determinant defect
connects Heinävaara’s tracial joint spectral measure to eigenvalues.
The resulting transfer identity classifies finite signed real ridge
inequalities on nilpotents and identifies their least additive
eigenvalue corrections. A dual theorem characterizes weak-* limits
of weighted angular measures of finite nilpotents.
In general finite tracial algebras, separate positive-kernel and
sharp determinant-covariance comparisons yield the Riesz bounds.
Consequences include the sharp Volterra constant, Riesz–Titchmarsh
and shifted-transform formulas, joint real-pencil norms, and exact
triangular and operator-valued Fourier Riesz norms. 
  
 
 
 Key words and phrases:  Hilbert transform, Riesz projection, Schatten class, subdiagonal algebra, spectral correction
 
 2020 Mathematics Subject Classification Primary 47B10; Secondary 42A50, 47A30, 46L52
 
 
 1.  Introduction 

 
 Throughout,  1 < p < ∞ 1<p<\infty ,  p ′ = p / ( p − 1 ) p^{\prime}=p/(p-1) , and
 p ∗ = max ⁡ { p , p ′ } p^{*}=\max\{p,p^{\prime}\} . We determine the noncommutative sharp constants 
 

 
 
 C p = cot ⁡ π 2 ​ p ∗ , D p = csc ⁡ π p . C_{p}=\cot\frac{\pi}{2p^{*}},\qquad D_{p}=\csc\frac{\pi}{p}. 
 
 
 The Hilbert transform on  L p ​ ( ℝ , 𝒮 p ) L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{S}_{p})  and the ordered Hilbert
Schur multiplier have norm  C p C_{p} . Every maximal subdiagonal algebra
in a finite tracial von Neumann algebra has retained and strict
Riesz projections bounded by  D p D_{p} , optimally over this class.
The first three theorems state the analytic-distance bound, the
determinant-covariance estimate, and the classification with least
spectral correction. 
 
 
 The scalar Hilbert value is due to Pichorides  [ 14 ] ,
and the real-pencil values to Hollenbeck–Kalton–Verbitsky
 [ 9 ] . Neuwirth–Ricard identified the Hilbert Fourier–Schur
and completely bounded norms for every exponent
 [ 18 , Theorem 6.2] , and computed their value for powers of two
and their conjugate exponents. We determine that value throughout
the exponent range. The shifted-transform formula includes the
Riesz–Titchmarsh conjecture at the half shift and Laeng’s full
Conjecture 5.7  [ 12 ] ; it extends the even-integer and
conjugate-exponent cases of Bañuelos–Kwaśnicki  [ 1 ] .
The Volterra consequence gives the conjectured sharp comparison
of the self-adjoint parts of compact quasinilpotent operators,
recorded in  [ 24 ] ; see also the classical work
 [ 21 ,  22 ,  23 ] . 
 
 
 Hollenbeck–Verbitsky determined the scalar Riesz value  D p D_{p} 
 [ 34 ] . Randrianantoanina proved boundedness for finite maximal
subdiagonal algebras  [ 33 ] . Neuwirth–Ricard identified the
triangular Schatten norm with the completely bounded Fourier Riesz
norm and computed its value at  p = 4 p=4   [ 18 , Proposition 6.3] ;
duality gives  p = 4 / 3 p=4/3 , besides the elementary case  p = 2 p=2 .
Here the sharp uniform upper bound is proved directly in arbitrary
finite tracial algebras. 
 
 
 The proofs use two spectral comparisons with exact nonnegative
remainders. For finite matrices, our logarithmic determinant defect
compares eigenvalues with Heinävaara’s positive tracial joint
spectral measure  [ 2 ] . The ridge transform and its
one-variable differential inverse are already present in
 [ 3 , Remark 2, p. 40] ; the additional ingredient here
is the nonnegative eigenvalue defect and its optimal spectral
correction. For finite tracial algebras, logarithmic rearrangement
and determinant covariance give the analytic witnesses needed for
the Riesz theorem. Remark  1.8  specifies the
distinct scopes of these comparisons. 
 
 
 1.1.  Sharp Riesz bounds in finite tracial algebras 

 
 For the subdiagonal results,  ( ℳ , τ ) (\mathcal{M},\tau)  is a finite von Neumann algebra with a faithful normal tracial state and  τ ⁡ ( I ) = 1 \tau(I)=1 . For  1 ≤ p < ∞ 1\leq p<\infty ,  L p ​ ( ℳ ) L^{p}(\mathcal{M})  is the completion of  ℳ \mathcal{M}  for
 ‖ x ‖ p = ( τ ​ | x | p ) 1 / p \|x\|_{p}=(\tau|x|^{p})^{1/p} , identified with the corresponding affiliated measurable operators. We use the trace pairing  ⟨ x , y ⟩ = τ ⁡ ( y ∗ ​ x ) \langle x,y\rangle=\tau(y^{*}x) , Hölder’s inequality, cyclicity for integrable products, and  L p L^{p} – L p ′ L^{p^{\prime}}  duality, where  p ′ = p / ( p − 1 ) p^{\prime}=p/(p-1) . Since the trace is normalized,  ‖ x ‖ q ≤ ‖ x ‖ p \|x\|_{q}\leq\|x\|_{p}  when  q ≤ p q\leq p . 
 
 
 A  subdiagonal algebra  is a weak-star closed unital algebra  ℬ ⊂ ℳ \mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{M}  for which  𝒟 = ℬ ∩ ℬ ∗ \mathcal{D}=\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{B}^{*}  admits a trace-preserving normal conditional expectation  Φ : ℳ → 𝒟 \Phi:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{D} , multiplicative on  ℬ \mathcal{B} , and  ℬ + ℬ ∗ \mathcal{B}+\mathcal{B}^{*}  is weak-star dense in  ℳ \mathcal{M} . It is  maximal  if it is maximal among the subdiagonal algebras for the same expectation. Write  ℬ 0 = ℬ ∩ ker ⁡ Φ \mathcal{B}_{0}=\mathcal{B}\cap\ker\Phi  and 
 

 
 
 H p = ℬ ¯ L p , H 0 p = ℬ 0 ¯ L p , 𝒞 = ℬ + ℬ 0 ∗ . H^{p}=\overline{\mathcal{B}}^{\,L^{p}},\qquad H_{0}^{p}=\overline{\mathcal{B}_{0}}^{\,L^{p}},\qquad\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{B}+\mathcal{B}_{0}^{*}. 
 
 
 On this bounded core the retained-diagonal projection is  P ⁡ ( U + Y ) = U P(U+Y)=U  and the strict projection is  P 0 = P − Φ P_{0}=P-\Phi . Their well-definedness and core density are proved below. 
 
 
 Theorem 1.1 . 
 
 For every maximal subdiagonal algebra in  ( ℳ , τ ) (\mathcal{M},\tau)  and every
 1 < p < ∞ 1<p<\infty , 
 

 
 
 ‖ U + Y ‖ p \displaystyle\|U+Y\|_{p} 
 ≥ sin ⁡ π p ​ ‖ U ‖ p , \displaystyle\geq\sin\frac{\pi}{p}\,\|U\|_{p}, 
 U \displaystyle U 
 ∈ H p , Y ∈ ( H 0 p ) ∗ , \displaystyle\in H^{p},\quad Y\in(H_{0}^{p})^{*}, 
 
 
 (1.1) 
 
 ‖ U 0 + Y 0 ‖ p \displaystyle\|U_{0}+Y_{0}\|_{p} 
 ≥ sin ⁡ π p ​ ‖ U 0 ‖ p , \displaystyle\geq\sin\frac{\pi}{p}\,\|U_{0}\|_{p}, 
 U 0 \displaystyle U_{0} 
 ∈ H 0 p , Y 0 ∈ ( H p ) ∗ . \displaystyle\in H_{0}^{p},\quad Y_{0}\in(H^{p})^{*}. 
 
 
 Here  ( H p ) ∗ = { x ∗ : x ∈ H p } (H^{p})^{*}=\{x^{*}:x\in H^{p}\}  and similarly for  H 0 p H_{0}^{p} .
Consequently  P P  and  P 0 P_{0}  extend to bounded operators on  L p ​ ( ℳ ) L^{p}(\mathcal{M}) ,
and 
 

 
 (1.2) 
 
 ‖ P ‖ L p → L p ≤ csc ⁡ π p , ‖ P 0 ‖ L p → L p ≤ csc ⁡ π p . \quad\|P\|_{L^{p}\to L^{p}}\leq\csc\frac{\pi}{p},\qquad\|P_{0}\|_{L^{p}\to L^{p}}\leq\csc\frac{\pi}{p}.\quad 
 
 
 The distance coefficient and both projection bounds are optimal
uniformly over this class. Equality is not asserted for every
individual algebra. 
 
 
 
 The new upper bound. 

 
 The scalar sharp value supplies a lower bound; the known
Fourier–Schur transference relations do not supply the missing
all-exponent upper bound. We prove that upper bound in the finite
algebra itself. Determinant covariance handles  2 < p < 4 2<p<4 ;
positive-kernel comparison and independently established analytic
regularization handle  p > 4 p>4 . Continuity, duality, and a subdiagonal
matrix construction complete the retained and strict bounds.
Section  8.6  then gives the exact uniform
triangular norm and the exact Fourier Riesz norm with any nonzero
finite coefficient algebra
(Corollaries  8.11  and  8.13 ). 
 
 
 The trace inequality below supplies the middle-exponent estimate.
Here  Det τ \operatorname{Det}_{\tau}  is the Fuglede–Kadison determinant,
with its definition and structural properties recalled in
Appendix  C . 
 
 
 Theorem 1.2 . 
 
 Let  ( ℳ , τ ) (\mathcal{M},\tau)  be any finite von Neumann algebra with a faithful
normal trace satisfying  τ ⁡ ( I ) = 1 \tau(I)=1 , and let  N ∈ ℳ N\in\mathcal{M}  satisfy
 Det τ ⁡ ( I + ζ ​ N ) ≥ 1 \operatorname{Det}_{\tau}(I+\zeta N)\geq 1  for every  ζ ∈ ℂ \zeta\in\mathbb{C} .
Then  τ ​ N = 0 \tau N=0 , and for every bounded invertible  D > 0 D>0  and  0 < β < 1 0<\beta<1 , 
 

 
 (1.3) 
 
 | τ ​ D β ​ N | 2 ≤ sin 2 ⁡ π ​ β 2 ​ ( 1 + β ) ​ τ ​ D 1 + β ​ τ ​ ( N ∗ ​ D β − 1 ​ N ) . |\tau D^{\beta}N|^{2}\leq\sin^{2}\frac{\pi\beta}{2(1+\beta)}\,\tau D^{1+\beta}\,\tau(N^{*}D^{\beta-1}N). 
 
 
 When  v = τ ⁡ ( N ∗ ​ D β − 1 ​ N ) > 0 v=\tau(N^{*}D^{\beta-1}N)>0 , the deficit has the nonnegative
entropy and determinant residual ( 5.26 ).
For each fixed  β \beta , the squared
coefficient is optimal uniformly over normalized finite tracial algebras;
this is proved after the circle construction in Section  6.3 . 
 
 
 
 For  N ∈ ℬ 0 N\in\mathcal{B}_{0}  or  N ∈ ℬ 0 ∗ N\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{*} , the determinant hypothesis follows
from the general Jensen inequality
(Corollary  5.10 ). With  β = ( p − 2 ) / 2 \beta=(p-2)/2 , the squared
coefficient in ( 1.3 ) is  cos 2 ⁡ ( π / p ) \cos^{2}(\pi/p) .
Analytic factorization turns this covariance estimate into the
 L 2 L^{2}  angle bound used for  2 < p < 4 2<p<4 . The proof and residual are given
in Section  5 ; sharpness follows from the scalar
circle construction in Section  6.3 . 
 
 
 
 
 1.2.  Matrix classification and optimal spectral correction 

 
 The underlying questions are: which finite signed real ridge
inequalities hold for all nilpotent matrices, and what is their
least additive eigenvalue correction for arbitrary matrices?
Theorem  1.3  answers both through the least
homogeneous superharmonic majorant, extending the scalar
analytic-test/majorant principle of  [ 9 , Theorem 2.1] . 
 
 
 All Hilbert spaces are complex; traces are unnormalized unless
specified otherwise. Let  m 2 m_{2}  denote planar Lebesgue measure and
set  Δ = ∂ x 2 + ∂ y 2 \Delta=\partial_{x}^{2}+\partial_{y}^{2} .
We write  ∥ ⋅ ∥ p \|\cdot\|_{p}  for the Schatten  p p -norm; unadorned
matrix norms are operator norms. A compact quasinilpotent operator
is called a Volterra operator. For  T = A + i ​ B T=A+iB , set
 A = Re ⁡ T = ( T + T ∗ ) / 2 A=\operatorname{Re}T=(T+T^{*})/2  and  B = Im ⁡ T = ( T − T ∗ ) / ( 2 ​ i ) B=\operatorname{Im}T=(T-T^{*})/(2i) .
For real coefficients  c j , a j , b j c_{j},a_{j},b_{j} , define the finite signed
real ridge sum and its trace functional by 
 
 
 

 
 (1.4) 
 
 F ⁡ ( x + i ​ y ) \displaystyle F(x+iy) 
 = ∑ j = 1 m c j ​ | a j ​ x + b j ​ y | p , \displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}c_{j}|a_{j}x+b_{j}y|^{p}, 
 
 
 (1.5) 
 
 ℱ p ​ ( A , B ) \displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{p}(A,B) 
 = ∑ j = 1 m c j ​ tr ⁡ | a j ​ A + b j ​ B | p , A = A ∗ , B = B ∗ . \displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}c_{j}\operatorname{tr}|a_{j}A+b_{j}B|^{p},\qquad A=A^{*},\quad B=B^{*}. 
 
 
 Let  𝒰 p \mathcal{U}_{p}  be the class of real  p p -homogeneous functions
 U ∈ C 1 ​ ( ℂ ) ∩ W loc 2 , q ​ ( ℂ ) U\in C^{1}(\mathbb{C})\cap W^{2,q}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{C})  for some  q > 1 q>1  such
that  Δ ​ U ≤ 0 \Delta U\leq 0  distributionally. Thus  U ⁡ ( r ​ z ) = r p ​ U ​ ( z ) U(rz)=r^{p}U(z)  for
 r ≥ 0 r\geq 0 . 
 
 
 Theorem 1.3 . 
 
 For  F F  in ( 1.4 ), the following are equivalent. 
 
 (N)  
 
 ℱ p ​ ( Re ⁡ N , Im ⁡ N ) ≤ 0 \mathcal{F}_{p}(\operatorname{Re}N,\operatorname{Im}N)\leq 0  for every finite
nilpotent complex matrix  N N .

 For every complex polynomial  q q  with  q ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 q(0)=0 ,

 When these conditions hold there is a least superharmonic majorant

 (1.7)

 It is enough in (N) to test  N = q ⁡ ( S n ) N=q(S_{n}) , where  S n S_{n}  is the nilpotent
shift and  q ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 q(0)=0 . 
 
 
 Moreover, for every  z ∈ ℂ z\in\mathbb{C} ,

 = sup n ≥ 1 , T ∈ M n ​ ( ℂ ) spec ⁡ T = { z } 1 n ​ ℱ p ​ ( Re ⁡ T , Im ⁡ T ) \displaystyle=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geq 1,\ T\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\\

q(0)=0\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{n}\mathcal{F}_{p}\bigl(\operatorname{Re}(zI_{n}+q(S_{n})),\operatorname{Im}(zI_{n}+q(S_{n}))\bigr). 
 
 
 Consequently a real-valued function  G G  satisfies 
 

 
 (1.9)

 if and only if  G ≥ U ∗ G\geq U_{*} . Eigenvalues are counted with algebraic
multiplicity. The comparison holds for arbitrary real-valued  G G . 
 
 
 
 The spectral mechanism. 

 
 Heinävaara’s positive tracial joint spectral measure  μ A , B \mu_{A,B} 
 [ 2 ]  represents real directional trace
functionals. Corollary  2.6  connects it to the
eigenvalues of  T = A + i ​ B T=A+iB  through a nonnegative potential  Ψ T \Psi_{T} :
for every  U ∈ 𝒰 p U\in\mathcal{U}_{p}  with  U ≥ F U\geq F , 
 

 
 
 ∑ k U ⁡ ( λ k ​ ( T ) ) − ℱ p ​ ( A , B ) \displaystyle\sum_{k}U(\lambda_{k}(T))-\mathcal{F}_{p}(A,B) 
 = p ⁡ ( p + 1 ) ​ ∫ ℂ ( U − F ) ​ d ​ μ A , B \displaystyle=p(p+1)\int_{\mathbb{C}}(U-F)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{A,B} 
 
 
 
 
 + 1 2 ​ π ∫ ℂ Ψ T ( − Δ U ) d m 2 . \displaystyle+\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\Psi_{T}(-\Delta U)\,\mathrm{d}m_{2}. 
 
 
 Both remainders are finite and nonnegative. This gives the matrix
inequality, while translated Toeplitz tests establish the pointwise
optimal correction ( 1.8 ).
The mass of  Ψ T \Psi_{T}  is  π 2 ​ ( ‖ T ‖ 2 2 − ∑ k | λ k ​ ( T ) | 2 ) \frac{\pi}{2}(\|T\|_{2}^{2}-\sum_{k}|\lambda_{k}(T)|^{2}) ,
linking Henrici’s departure from normality  [ 8 ]  to the
sharp quantitative Weyl comparison in Corollary  B.3 . 
 
 
 
 Angular measures and inverse realization. 

 
 Theorem  7.2  gives a converse realization theorem:
a finite positive measure  ν \nu  on  𝕋 \mathbb{T}  is a weak-* limit of the
weighted angular measures of single finite nilpotent matrices if
and only if 
 

 
 
 ν = ( ∂ θ 2 + p 2 ) ω , ω ∈ C ( 𝕋 ) , ω ≥ 0 . \nu=(\partial_{\theta}^{2}+p^{2})\omega,\qquad\omega\in C(\mathbb{T}),\quad\omega\geq 0. 
 
 
 The matrix dimensions may grow. Separately,
Corollary  3.7  derives the finite directional moment
cones and their optimal quotients directly from the classification
by finite-dimensional separation. 
 
 
 At  p = 4 p=4 , Theorem  8.5  identifies the closed disk
of normalized directional moments. Corollary  8.7 
gives the optimal quotient in closed form for every signed numerator
and every signed denominator strictly positive on this disk.
The signed classification and joint norm formulas follow by substitution. 
 
 
 
 
 1.3.  Operator models and the common constant 

 
 Use
 H ​ f ​ ( x ) = π − 1 ​ p . v . ​ ∫ f ⁡ ( t ) / ( x − t ) ​ 𝑑 t Hf(x)=\pi^{-1}\operatorname{p.v.}\int f(t)/(x-t)\,\mathrm{d}t  and 
 

 
 (1.10) 
 
 ( R α ​ V ) ℓ = sin ⁡ ( π ​ α ) π ​ ∑ m ∈ ℤ V m ℓ + α − m , 0 < α < 1 . (R_{\alpha}V)_{\ell}=\frac{\sin(\pi\alpha)}{\pi}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{V_{m}}{\ell+\alpha-m},\qquad 0<\alpha<1. 
 
 
 Initially defined on finitely supported sequences,  R α R_{\alpha} 
extends boundedly to  ℓ p ​ ( ℤ , 𝒮 p ​ ( 𝒦 ) ) \ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z};\mathcal{S}_{p}(\mathcal{K}))  by
Lemma  4.3 ;  𝒦 = ℂ \mathcal{K}=\mathbb{C}  gives the scalar case. 
 
 
 For a finite family  ℰ = ( E j ) \mathcal{E}=(E_{j})  of pairwise orthogonal
projections with  ∑ j E j = I \sum_{j}E_{j}=I  on a finite-dimensional Hilbert

 (1.11)

 Here  D ℰ D_{\mathcal{E}}  is the block-diagonal conditional expectation,
and  ( E j ) (E_{j})  are the successive difference projections of the flag.
We use  sgn ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 \operatorname{sgn}(0)=0 . Suprema over  ℰ \mathcal{E}  allow all
finite ambient dimensions and block sizes, independently of
 𝒦 \mathcal{K} . Write  𝒮 p d = 𝒮 p ​ ( ℂ d ) \mathcal{S}_{p}^{d}=\mathcal{S}_{p}(\mathbb{C}^{d})  and define, initially on
finitely supported matrices on  ℓ 2 ​ ( ℕ ) \ell^{2}(\mathbb{N}) , 
 
 
 

 
 (1.12) 
 
 ℋ 0 ​ ( e j ​ k ) = i ​ sgn ⁡ ( j − k ) ​ e j ​ k . \mathscr{H}_{0}(e_{jk})=i\operatorname{sgn}(j-k)e_{jk}. 
 
 
 The spaces  L p ​ ( ℝ ) L^{p}(\mathbb{R})  and  𝒮 p = 𝒮 p ​ ( ℓ 2 ​ ( ℕ ) ) \mathcal{S}_{p}=\mathcal{S}_{p}(\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N}))  carry their
canonical noncommutative  L p L^{p}  operator-space structures. For a linear
map  T : E → F T:E\to F  its completely bounded norm means 
 

 
 (1.13)

 Section  4.5  recalls the characterization of this
norm by Schatten  p p -amplifications. 
 
 
 For real pairs  ( a j , b j ) j = 1 m (a_{j},b_{j})_{j=1}^{m}  and weights  w j ≥ 0 w_{j}\geq 0 , set 
 

 
 
 P ⁡ ( x , y ) \displaystyle P(x,y) 
 = ∑ j = 1 m w j ​ | a j ​ x + b j ​ y | p , \displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}w_{j}|a_{j}x+b_{j}y|^{p}, 
 
 
 (1.14) 
 
 Λ p ​ ( P ) p \displaystyle\Lambda_{p}(P)^{p} 
 = sup N ≠ 0 ​ finite ​ nilpotent ∑ j w j ​ tr ⁡ | a j ​ Re ⁡ N + b j ​ Im ⁡ N | p tr ⁡ | Re ⁡ N | p . \displaystyle=\sup_{N\neq 0\ {\rm finite\ nilpotent}}\frac{\sum_{j}w_{j}\operatorname{tr}|a_{j}\operatorname{Re}N+b_{j}\operatorname{Im}N|^{p}}{\operatorname{tr}|\operatorname{Re}N|^{p}}. 
 
 
 We reserve  C p C_{p}  for the scalar Hilbert constant and use  Λ p ​ ( P ) \Lambda_{p}(P) 
for the joint constant. All joint output spaces below use the  ℓ p \ell^{p} 
direct-sum norm. Write 
 

 
 
 𝐇 ​ f = ( w j 1 / p ​ ( a j ​ f + b j ​ H ​ f ) ) j = 1 m , 𝐓 ℰ ​ X = ( w j 1 / p ​ ( a j ​ X + b j ​ H ℰ ​ X ) ) j = 1 m . \mathbf{H}f=(w_{j}^{1/p}(a_{j}f+b_{j}Hf))_{j=1}^{m},\qquad\mathbf{T}_{\mathcal{E}}X=(w_{j}^{1/p}(a_{j}X+b_{j}H_{\mathcal{E}}X))_{j=1}^{m}. 
 
 
 
 
 Theorem 1.4 . 
 
 The constant ( 1.14 ) is finite and equals the least
 C ≥ 0 C\geq 0  for which  P − C p ​ | x | p P-C^{p}|x|^{p}  has a majorant in  𝒰 p \mathcal{U}_{p} .
Such a majorant exists at this optimal value of  C C .
It also satisfies 
 

 
 (1.15) 
 
 Λ p ​ ( P ) p = sup q ≠ 0 ​ polynomial q ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 ∫ 0 2 ​ π P ⁡ ( Re ⁡ q ⁡ ( e i ​ t ) , Im ⁡ q ⁡ ( e i ​ t ) ) ​ 𝑑 t ∫ 0 2 ​ π | Re ⁡ q ⁡ ( e i ​ t ) | p ​ 𝑑 t . \Lambda_{p}(P)^{p}=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\neq 0\ {\rm polynomial}\\
q(0)=0\end{subarray}}\frac{\int_{0}^{2\pi}P(\operatorname{Re}q(e^{it}),\operatorname{Im}q(e^{it}))\,\mathrm{d}t}{\int_{0}^{2\pi}|\operatorname{Re}q(e^{it})|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t}. 
 
 
 For every nonzero Hilbert space  𝒦 \mathcal{K} , 
 

 
 (1.16) 
 
 Λ p ​ ( P ) = ‖ 𝐇 ‖ L p ​ ( ℝ ) → ℓ p m ​ ( L p ​ ( ℝ ) ) = ‖ 𝐇 ‖ L p ​ ( ℝ , 𝒮 p ​ ( 𝒦 ) ) → ℓ p m ​ ( L p ​ ( ℝ , 𝒮 p ​ ( 𝒦 ) ) ) = sup ℰ ‖ 𝐓 ℰ ‖ . \Lambda_{p}(P)=\|\mathbf{H}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R})\to\ell_{p}^{m}(L^{p}(\mathbb{R}))}=\|\mathbf{H}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{S}_{p}(\mathcal{K}))\to\ell_{p}^{m}(L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{S}_{p}(\mathcal{K})))}=\sup_{\mathcal{E}}\|\mathbf{T}_{\mathcal{E}}\|. 
 
 
 If  a j = cos ⁡ ( π ​ α j ) a_{j}=\cos(\pi\alpha_{j})  and  b j = sin ⁡ ( π ​ α j ) b_{j}=\sin(\pi\alpha_{j}) ,
 0 < α j < 1 0<\alpha_{j}<1 , the simultaneous transform
 𝐑 ​ V = ( w j 1 / p ​ R α j ​ V ) j = 1 m \mathbf{R}V=(w_{j}^{1/p}R_{\alpha_{j}}V)_{j=1}^{m}  satisfies 
 

 
 (1.17) 
 
 ‖ 𝐑 ‖ ℓ p ​ ( ℤ ) → ℓ p m ​ ( ℓ p ​ ( ℤ ) ) = ‖ 𝐑 ‖ ℓ p ​ ( ℤ , 𝒮 p ​ ( 𝒦 ) ) → ℓ p m ​ ( ℓ p ​ ( ℤ , 𝒮 p ​ ( 𝒦 ) ) ) = Λ p ​ ( P ) . \|\mathbf{R}\|_{\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z})\to\ell_{p}^{m}(\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z}))}=\|\mathbf{R}\|_{\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z};\mathcal{S}_{p}(\mathcal{K}))\to\ell_{p}^{m}(\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z};\mathcal{S}_{p}(\mathcal{K})))}=\Lambda_{p}(P). 
 
 
 The scalar Hilbert and discrete norms and the uniform Schur norm
are unchanged on replacing them by completely bounded norms for
the canonical noncommutative  L p L^{p}  direct sums.
At  p = 2 p=2 ,  Λ 2 ​ ( P ) 2 = ∑ j w j ​ ( a j 2 + b j 2 ) \Lambda_{2}(P)^{2}=\sum_{j}w_{j}(a_{j}^{2}+b_{j}^{2}) . 
 
 
 
 The simultaneous realization uses one finite nilpotent matrix for
all shifts. At  p = 4 p=4 , the joint norm and the criterion for a common
extremizing sequence are explicit (Corollary  8.8 );
the same moment disk classifies all signed fourth-power inequalities
(Corollary  8.6 ). For a bounded real sequence
 𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} , let  D 𝜿 ​ X D_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}X  have diagonal
entries  κ j ​ X j ​ j \kappa_{j}X_{jj}  and zero off-diagonal entries. Then
Corollary  8.4  gives the exact perturbation formula 
 

 
 
 ‖ ℋ 0 + D 𝜿 ‖ = ‖ ℋ 0 + D 𝜿 ‖ cb = max ⁡ { C p , ‖ 𝜿 ‖ ∞ } . \|\mathscr{H}_{0}+D_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\|=\|\mathscr{H}_{0}+D_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\|_{\rm cb}=\max\{C_{p},\|\boldsymbol{\kappa}\|_{\infty}\}. 
 
 
 
 
 Corollary 1.5 . 
 
 Let  ( a , b ) (a,b)  be a real pair and let  𝒦 ≠ { 0 } \mathcal{K}\neq\{0\} . 
 
 
 (i) Scalar norm and optimal majorant constant. 
For a single pencil, the common norm and the least admissible
constant in Theorem  1.4  are given by 
 

 
 (1.18) 
 
 K p ​ ( a , b ) p = max 0 ≤ t ≤ π ⁡ | a ​ cos ⁡ t + b ​ sin ⁡ t | p + | a ​ cos ⁡ ( t + π / p ) + b ​ sin ⁡ ( t + π / p ) | p | cos ⁡ t | p + | cos ⁡ ( t + π / p ) | p . K_{p}(a,b)^{p}=\max_{0\leq t\leq\pi}\frac{|a\cos t+b\sin t|^{p}+|a\cos(t+\pi/p)+b\sin(t+\pi/p)|^{p}}{|\cos t|^{p}+|\cos(t+\pi/p)|^{p}}. 
 
 
 Its symmetries and coordinate values are 
 

 
 (1.19) 
 
 K p ​ ( a , − b ) = K p ​ ( a , b ) , K p ​ ( c ​ a , c ​ b ) = | c | ​ K p ​ ( a , b ) , K p ′ ​ ( a , b ) = K p ​ ( a , b ) , \displaystyle K_{p}(a,-b)=K_{p}(a,b),\qquad K_{p}(ca,cb)=|c|K_{p}(a,b),\qquad K_{p^{\prime}}(a,b)=K_{p}(a,b), 
 
 
 (1.20) 
 
 K p ​ ( 0 , 1 ) = C p = cot ⁡ π 2 ​ p ∗ , K 2 ​ ( a , b ) = a 2 + b 2 , K p ​ ( a , 0 ) = | a | . \displaystyle K_{p}(0,1)=C_{p}=\cot\frac{\pi}{2p^{*}},\qquad K_{2}(a,b)=\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}},\qquad K_{p}(a,0)=|a|. 
 
 
 
 
 (ii) Schur and completely bounded norms. 
For the standard infinite Hilbert Schur multiplier, 
 

 
 (1.21) 
 
 ‖ a ​ I + b ​ ℋ 0 ‖ 𝒮 p → 𝒮 p = ‖ a ​ I + b ​ ℋ 0 ‖ cb , 𝒮 p = ‖ a ​ I + b ​ H ‖ cb , L p ​ ( ℝ ) = K p ​ ( a , b ) . \|aI+b\mathscr{H}_{0}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{p}\to\mathcal{S}_{p}}=\|aI+b\mathscr{H}_{0}\|_{\mathrm{cb},\mathcal{S}_{p}}=\|aI+bH\|_{\mathrm{cb},L^{p}(\mathbb{R})}=K_{p}(a,b). 
 
 
 In particular, the Hilbert-transform norms on  L p ​ ( ℝ ) L^{p}(\mathbb{R})  and
 L p ​ ( ℝ , 𝒮 p ) L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{S}_{p}) , and the completely bounded norm on  L p ​ ( ℝ ) L^{p}(\mathbb{R}) ,
all equal  C p C_{p} . The infinite signed Schur
conventions with diagonal value  + 1 +1  or  − 1 -1  have the same operator norm
and completely bounded norm, by Lemma  4.8 .
For the Hilbert transform, the identification of the Schur multiplier
norm with the completely bounded norm is due to  [ 18 , Theorem 6.2] ;
the value for every  1 < p < ∞ 1<p<\infty  and the extension to finite
families of real pencils follow here. 
 
 
 (iii) Shifted discrete transforms. 
For  0 < α < 1 0<\alpha<1 , 
 

 
 (1.22) 
 
 ‖ R α ‖ ℓ p ​ ( ℤ , 𝒮 p ​ ( 𝒦 ) ) = ‖ R α ‖ ℓ p ​ ( ℤ ) = K p ​ ( cos ⁡ ( π ​ α ) , sin ⁡ ( π ​ α ) ) . \|R_{\alpha}\|_{\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z};\mathcal{S}_{p}(\mathcal{K}))}=\|R_{\alpha}\|_{\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z})}=K_{p}(\cos(\pi\alpha),\sin(\pi\alpha)). 
 
 
 For  t = k + α t=k+\alpha  with  k ∈ ℤ k\in\mathbb{Z}  and  0 < α < 1 0<\alpha<1 , define
 ( R t ​ V ) ℓ = ( − 1 ) k ​ ( R α ​ V ) ℓ + k (R_{t}V)_{\ell}=(-1)^{k}(R_{\alpha}V)_{\ell+k} ; at integers set
 ( R k ​ V ) ℓ = ( − 1 ) k ​ V ℓ + k (R_{k}V)_{\ell}=(-1)^{k}V_{\ell+k} . Thus the same norm formula holds
with  ( cos ⁡ ( π ​ t ) , sin ⁡ ( π ​ t ) ) (\cos(\pi t),\sin(\pi t))  for every real  t t .
The completely bounded value is the same
by Theorem  1.4 . Formula ( 1.22 ) resolves
Laeng’s Conjecture 5.7 and, at  α = 1 / 2 \alpha=1/2 , the Riesz–Titchmarsh
conjecture. 
 
 
 The uniform Schur bounds are sharp as suprema over finite dimensions. 
 
 
 
 
 1.4.  Sharp Volterra bounds 

 
 Corollary 1.6 . 
 
 Let  T = A + i ​ B T=A+iB  be compact and quasinilpotent. If  A ∈ 𝒮 p A\in\mathcal{S}_{p} , then
 B ∈ 𝒮 p B\in\mathcal{S}_{p}  and, for every real  a , b a,b , 
 

 
 (1.23) 
 
 ‖ a ​ A + b ​ B ‖ p ≤ K p ​ ( a , b ) ​ ‖ A ‖ p . \|aA+bB\|_{p}\leq K_{p}(a,b)\|A\|_{p}. 
 
 
 In particular, if  B ∈ 𝒮 p B\in\mathcal{S}_{p} , then  A ∈ 𝒮 p A\in\mathcal{S}_{p}  and 
 

 
 (1.24) 
 
 ‖ A ‖ p ≤ C p ​ ‖ B ‖ p . \|A\|_{p}\leq C_{p}\|B\|_{p}. 
 
 
 The constants are optimal already over finite nilpotent matrices. In
particular ( 1.24 ) resolves, for every  1 < p < ∞ 1<p<\infty , the
conjectured sharp constant recorded by Matsaev–Sodin
 [ 24 ] ; see also  [ 22 ] . 
 
 
 
 The spectral correction determines the optimal comparison constant
for every nonempty prescribed spectral set. 
 
 
 Corollary 1.7 . 
 
 Let  1 < p < ∞ 1<p<\infty  and  ∅ ≠ Ω ⊂ ℂ \varnothing\neq\Omega\subset\mathbb{C} , and put 
 

 
 
 r ⁡ ( z ) = | Re ⁡ z | | Im ⁡ z | ( Im ⁡ z ≠ 0 ) , r ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 , r ⁡ ( z ) = + ∞ ( z ∈ ℝ ∖ { 0 } ) . r(z)=\frac{|\operatorname{Re}z|}{|\operatorname{Im}z|}\quad(\operatorname{Im}z\neq 0),\qquad r(0)=0,\qquad r(z)=+\infty\quad(z\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}). 
 
 
 The optimal constant in
 ‖ Re ⁡ T ‖ p ≤ L ​ ‖ Im ⁡ T ‖ p \|\operatorname{Re}T\|_{p}\leq L\|\operatorname{Im}T\|_{p}  over finite matrices with
 spec ⁡ T ⊂ Ω \operatorname{spec}T\subset\Omega  is 
 

 
 (1.25) 
 
 L Ω = max ⁡ { C p , sup z ∈ Ω r ⁡ ( z ) } , L_{\Omega}=\max\{C_{p},\sup_{z\in\Omega}r(z)\}, 
 
 
 where  L Ω = + ∞ L_{\Omega}=+\infty  means that no finite bound exists.
If  L Ω < ∞ L_{\Omega}<\infty , every compact  T = A + i ​ B ∈ 𝒮 p T=A+iB\in\mathcal{S}_{p}  with
 spec ⁡ T ⊂ Ω \operatorname{spec}T\subset\Omega  satisfies 
 

 
 (1.26) 
 
 ‖ A ‖ p ≤ L Ω ​ ‖ B ‖ p . \|A\|_{p}\leq L_{\Omega}\|B\|_{p}. 
 
 
 In particular, for  K ≥ C p K\geq C_{p} , the bound with constant  K K  holds
for all finite matrices with spectrum in  Ω \Omega  exactly when
 Ω ⊂ Σ p , K \Omega\subset\Sigma_{p,K} , where 
 

 
 (1.27) 
 
 Σ p , K = { z : | Re ⁡ z | ≤ K ​ | Im ⁡ z | } , Σ p = Σ p , C p . \Sigma_{p,K}=\{z:|\operatorname{Re}z|\leq K|\operatorname{Im}z|\},\qquad\Sigma_{p}=\Sigma_{p,C_{p}}. 
 
 
 For  Ω = Σ p \Omega=\Sigma_{p} , sharpness holds already over finite nilpotents. 
 
 
 
 The compact assertion assumes  T ∈ 𝒮 p T\in\mathcal{S}_{p}  and allows nonnormal operators.
The one-component membership assumption of Corollary  1.6 
is used only in the quasinilpotent setting. 
 
 
 Remark 1.8 . 
 
 The real ridge classification concerns finite matrices and its
Schatten-class consequences. Its extension to arbitrary finite
von Neumann algebras would require a compatible positive ridge
representation and the corresponding integrability and comparison
identities. The Riesz argument establishes its stated bounds in
arbitrary finite tracial algebras by a different spectral comparison;
it does not supply such a representation. In particular, traces of
real directional powers cannot in general be combined as scalar
radial functions to recover  τ ​ | A + i ​ B | p \tau|A+iB|^{p} .
The real-pencil norm identities and the Riesz projection identities
therefore remain complementary results. They do not assert a sharp
lifting theorem for arbitrary complex pencils  a ​ I + b ​ H aI+bH .
For the subdiagonal projections, sharpness means optimality uniformly
over algebras; for triangular truncation it means a supremum over
matrix sizes. The Fourier Riesz norm equals  D p D_{p}  for every nonzero
finite coefficient algebra. 
 
 
 
 The proofs follow two complementary routes. For finite matrices,
Heinävaara’s tracial joint spectral measure and a nonnegative
logarithmic determinant defect give the exact spectral transfer
identity in Section  2 . Analytic-disc envelopes and
Toeplitz tests then yield the classification and least spectral
correction in Section  3 . The transfer identity and
classification, together with the finite-dimensional realizations
of Section  4 , give the Hilbert, Volterra, and
shifted-transform bounds. Section  7  establishes the
angular inverse realization. 
 
 
 For general finite tracial algebras, the Riesz argument uses the
separate comparisons in Section  5 .
Determinant covariance gives the analytic-distance estimate for
 2 < p < 4 2<p<4 , while positive-kernel comparison and independently proved
analytic regularization supply the witnesses for  p > 4 p>4 .
Section  6  completes the projection theorem by
continuity, duality, and a subdiagonal corner construction.
Section  8  collects the compact spectral, moment, and
projection consequences; Section  9  explains the
limits of the two-point criterion. The appendices contain the
coordinate majorant, quantitative comparisons, Hardy-space
structural inputs, analytic regularization, and rotation and
independent-coordinate consequences. 
 
 
 
 
 2.  A spectral transfer theorem and its homogeneous consequences 

 
 We use the following form of Heinävaara’s representation
 [ 2 ] . For Hermitian  A , B ∈ M d ​ ( ℂ ) A,B\in M_{d}(\mathbb{C})  there is
a positive Borel measure  μ A , B \mu_{A,B} , normalized by
 μ A , B ​ ( { 0 } ) = 0 \mu_{A,B}(\{0\})=0 , such that the following identity holds
simultaneously for every  s , t ∈ ℝ s,t\in\mathbb{R}  and every measurable real  f f 
with  f ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 f(0)=0  and  ∫ − M M | f ⁡ ( u ) / u | ​ 𝑑 u < ∞ \int_{-M}^{M}|f(u)/u|\,\mathrm{d}u<\infty  for each  M > 0 M>0 : 
 

 
 (2.1) 
 
 ∫ ℝ 2 f ⁡ ( s ​ x + t ​ y ) ​ d ​ μ A , B ​ ( x , y ) = tr ⁡ ℋ ⁡ ( f ) ​ ( s ​ A + t ​ B ) , ℋ ⁡ ( f ) ​ ( u ) = ∫ 0 1 1 − r r ​ f ​ ( r ​ u ) ​ 𝑑 r . \int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(sx+ty)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{A,B}(x,y)=\operatorname{tr}\mathcal{H}(f)(sA+tB),\qquad\mathcal{H}(f)(u)=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1-r}{r}f(ru)\,\mathrm{d}r. 
 
 
 The measure is unique away from the origin
 [ 2 ] . Complex tests are handled by real and
imaginary parts. The normalization at zero fixes the otherwise
irrelevant mass there.
In particular, for every  q > 0 q>0 , 
 

 
 (2.2) 
 
 q ⁡ ( q + 1 ) ​ ∫ | s ​ x + t ​ y | q ​ d ​ μ A , B = tr ⁡ | s ​ A + t ​ B | q . q(q+1)\int|sx+ty|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{A,B}=\operatorname{tr}|sA+tB|^{q}. 
 
 
 For integer  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1  the same tracial formula gives 
 

 
 (2.3) 
 
 ∫ ( s ​ x + t ​ y ) k ​ d ​ μ A , B = tr ⁡ ( s ​ A + t ​ B ) k k ⁡ ( k + 1 ) . \int(sx+ty)^{k}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{A,B}=\frac{\operatorname{tr}(sA+tB)^{k}}{k(k+1)}. 
 
 
 Nonnegative one-variable tests show 
 

 
 (2.4) 
 
 supp ⁡ μ A , B ⊂ [ − ‖ A ‖ , ‖ A ‖ ] × [ − ‖ B ‖ , ‖ B ‖ ] . \operatorname{supp}\mu_{A,B}\subset[-\left\lVert A\right\rVert,\left\lVert A\right\rVert]\times[-\left\lVert B\right\rVert,\left\lVert B\right\rVert]. 
 
 
 For example, choose a nonnegative  f f  vanishing on  [ − ‖ A ‖ , ‖ A ‖ ] [-\left\lVert A\right\rVert,\left\lVert A\right\rVert]  and strictly positive off this interval, then use  ( s , t ) = ( 1 , 0 ) (s,t)=(1,0)  in ( 2.1 ). The other coordinate is identical.
For every  q > 0 q>0 , the elementary bound
 | ( x , y ) | q ≤ C q coord ​ ( | x | q + | y | q ) |(x,y)|^{q}\leq C_{q}^{\mathrm{coord}}(|x|^{q}+|y|^{q}) , where
 C q coord = max ⁡ { 1 , 2 q / 2 − 1 } C_{q}^{\mathrm{coord}}=\max\{1,2^{q/2-1}\} , and ( 2.2 ) give 
 

 
 (2.5) 
 
 ∫ | ( x , y ) | q ​ d ​ μ A , B < ∞ . \int|(x,y)|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{A,B}<\infty. 
 
 
 Thus the tracial measure has compact support and every positive radial
moment is finite, although its total mass can be infinite. 
 
 
 There is also an exact support-radius identity. Put  T = A + i ​ B T=A+iB ,
 ℓ θ ​ ( z ) = Re ⁡ ( e − i ​ θ ​ z ) \ell_{\theta}(z)=\operatorname{Re}(e^{-i\theta}z)  and
 R θ = sup z ∈ supp ⁡ μ A , B ∪ { 0 } | ℓ θ ​ ( z ) | R_{\theta}=\sup_{z\in\operatorname{supp}\mu_{A,B}\cup\{0\}}|\ell_{\theta}(z)| .
For  0 < a < R θ 0<a<R_{\theta} , continuity and the definition of support give
 μ A , B ​ ( E a ) > 0 \mu_{A,B}(E_{a})>0  for  E a = { | ℓ θ | > a } E_{a}=\{|\ell_{\theta}|>a\} , while
 a ​ μ A , B ​ ( E a ) ≤ ∫ | ℓ θ | ​ d ​ μ A , B < ∞ a\mu_{A,B}(E_{a})\leq\int|\ell_{\theta}|\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{A,B}<\infty .
For  q ≥ 1 q\geq 1 , 
 

 
 
 a q ​ μ A , B ​ ( E a ) ≤ ∫ | ℓ θ | q ​ d ​ μ A , B ≤ R θ q − 1 ​ ∫ | ℓ θ | ​ d ​ μ A , B . a^{q}\mu_{A,B}(E_{a})\leq\int|\ell_{\theta}|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{A,B}\leq R_{\theta}^{q-1}\int|\ell_{\theta}|\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{A,B}. 
 
 
 Taking  q q th roots, then  q → ∞ q\to\infty  and  a ↑ R θ a\uparrow R_{\theta} ,
gives convergence to  R θ R_{\theta}  despite the possibly infinite total
mass. The case  R θ = 0 R_{\theta}=0  is immediate. Equation ( 2.2 )
therefore gives  R θ = ‖ Re ⁡ ( e − i ​ θ ​ T ) ‖ R_{\theta}=\|\operatorname{Re}(e^{-i\theta}T)\| , and hence 
 

 
 (2.6) 
 
 sup z ∈ supp ⁡ μ A , B ∪ { 0 } | z | = sup θ ‖ Re ⁡ ( e − i ​ θ ​ T ) ‖ = max ‖ v ‖ = 1 ⁡ | v ∗ ​ T ​ v | . \sup_{z\in\operatorname{supp}\mu_{A,B}\cup\{0\}}|z|=\sup_{\theta}\|\operatorname{Re}(e^{-i\theta}T)\|=\max_{\|v\|=1}|v^{*}Tv|. 
 
 
 The union with  { 0 } \{0\}  includes the zero matrix. 
 
 
 The full ridge identity ( 2.1 ), rather than only its absolute
power moments, is used in the Green decomposition below; see also
Example  B.1 . 
 
 
 2.1.  A nonnegative logarithmic determinant defect 

 
 Logarithmic determinants and subharmonicity have long been used to
compare real and imaginary parts; see  [ 24 ]  and the Hilbert-transform
formulation in  [ 25 , Section 2] . Here the determinant Jensen
formula gives the explicit nonnegative potential used in the exact
spectral-defect identity.
Fix a finite matrix  T = A + i ​ B T=A+iB . For  w ∉ spec ⁡ T w\notin\operatorname{spec}T  set  E = T − w ​ I E=T-wI  and define 
 

 
 (2.7) 
 
 L T ​ ( w ) \displaystyle L_{T}(w) 
 = d ​ log ⁡ 2 + 1 2 ​ π ​ ∫ 0 2 ​ π log ⁡ | det Re ⁡ ( e − i ​ θ ​ E ) | ​ 𝑑 θ , \displaystyle=d\log 2+\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\log\left|\det\operatorname{Re}(e^{-i\theta}E)\right|\,\mathrm{d}\theta, 
 
 
 (2.8) 
 
 Ψ T ​ ( w ) \displaystyle\Psi_{T}(w) 
 = L T ​ ( w ) − log ⁡ | det ( T − w ​ I ) | . \displaystyle=L_{T}(w)-\log|\det(T-wI)|. 
 
 
 Values at the finitely many eigenvalues will be irrelevant to area integration. 
 
 
 Lemma 2.1 . 
 
 For  w ∉ spec ⁡ T w\notin\operatorname{spec}T , 
 

 
 (2.9) 
 
 Ψ T ​ ( w ) = ∑ j = 1 d log + ⁡ | λ j ​ ( E − 1 ​ E ∗ ) | ≥ 0 . \Psi_{T}(w)=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\log^{+}\left|\lambda_{j}(E^{-1}E^{*})\right|\geq 0. 
 
 
 The function  Ψ T \Psi_{T}  is continuous off  spec ⁡ T \operatorname{spec}T  and belongs to
 L q ​ ( ℂ ) L^{q}(\mathbb{C})  for every finite  q ≥ 1 q\geq 1 . Its essential support is contained
in the numerical range: 
 

 
 (2.10) 
 
 supp Ψ T ⊂ W ⁡ ( T ) ¯ , W ( T ) = { v ∗ T v : v ∈ ℂ d , ∥ v ∥ = 1 } . \operatorname{supp}\Psi_{T}\subset\overline{W(T)},\qquad W(T)=\{v^{*}Tv:\ v\in\mathbb{C}^{d},\ \|v\|=1\}. 
 
 
 In particular it vanishes for  | w | > ‖ T ‖ |w|>\|T\| . On each bounded set it is bounded above by a constant plus
 ∑ j log − ⁡ | w − λ j ​ ( T ) | \sum_{j}\log^{-}|w-\lambda_{j}(T)| . Moreover, 
 

 
 (2.11) 
 
 Ψ r ​ T ​ ( w ) = Ψ T ​ ( w / r ) , r > 0 . \Psi_{rT}(w)=\Psi_{T}(w/r),\qquad r>0. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The determinant factorization is 
 

 
 
 det Re ⁡ ( e − i ​ θ ​ E ) = 2 − d ​ e − i ​ d ​ θ ​ det E ​ det ( I + e 2 ​ i ​ θ ​ E − 1 ​ E ∗ ) . \det\operatorname{Re}(e^{-i\theta}E)=2^{-d}e^{-id\theta}\det E\,\det(I+e^{2i\theta}E^{-1}E^{*}). 
 
 
 For every  ζ ∈ ℂ \zeta\in\mathbb{C} , 
 

 
 
 1 2 ​ π ​ ∫ 0 2 ​ π log ⁡ | 1 + ζ ​ e i ​ θ | ​ 𝑑 θ = log + ⁡ | ζ | . \frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\log|1+\zeta e^{i\theta}|\,\mathrm{d}\theta=\log^{+}|\zeta|. 
 
 
 Factoring the characteristic polynomial, rather than diagonalizing the matrix, proves ( 2.9 ). Roots on the unit circle cause only integrable logarithmic zeros. Since  E E  is invertible, the determinant polynomial in  e 2 ​ i ​ θ e^{2i\theta}  is not identically zero. 
 
 
 When  w ∉ W ⁡ ( T ) ¯ w\notin\overline{W(T)} ,  E E  is invertible because
 spec ⁡ T ⊂ W ⁡ ( T ) \operatorname{spec}T\subset W(T) . Let  E − 1 ​ E ∗ ​ v = λ ​ v E^{-1}E^{*}v=\lambda v , with  v ≠ 0 v\neq 0 .
Then  E ∗ ​ v = λ ​ E ​ v E^{*}v=\lambda Ev . Writing  q = v ∗ ​ E ​ v q=v^{*}Ev , we have 
 

 
 
 | q | ≥ dist ⁡ ( w , W ⁡ ( T ) ) ​ ‖ v ‖ 2 > 0 , q ¯ = λ ​ q . |q|\geq\operatorname{dist}(w,W(T))\|v\|^{2}>0,\qquad\overline{q}=\lambda q. 
 
 
 Thus every eigenvalue has  | λ | = 1 |\lambda|=1 , and ( 2.9 )
is zero. Applying this to each eigenvalue proves the support assertion.
Eigenvalue continuity as an unordered multiset
also proves continuity of  Ψ T \Psi_{T}  off  spec ⁡ T \operatorname{spec}T . 
 
 
 On a fixed bounded set, the determinant norm bound in ( 2.7 ) gives, with a sufficiently large constant  M M , 
 

 
 
 0 ≤ Ψ T ​ ( w ) ≤ M − ∑ j log ⁡ | w − λ j ​ ( T ) | ≤ M ′ + ∑ j log − ⁡ | w − λ j ​ ( T ) | . 0\leq\Psi_{T}(w)\leq M-\sum_{j}\log|w-\lambda_{j}(T)|\leq M^{\prime}+\sum_{j}\log^{-}|w-\lambda_{j}(T)|. 
 
 
 Consequently  Ψ T ∈ L q ​ ( ℂ ) \Psi_{T}\in L^{q}(\mathbb{C})  for every finite  q ≥ 1 q\geq 1 . Scaling in the definition proves ( 2.11 ). The zero matrix is included, with  Ψ 0 = 0 \Psi_{0}=0  almost everywhere.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 2.2.  A compensated ridge decomposition 

 
 Write  v θ = ( cos ⁡ θ , sin ⁡ θ ) v_{\theta}=(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)  and
 av θ = ( 2 π ) − 1 ∫ 0 2 ​ π d θ \mathop{\mathrm{av}}\nolimits_{\theta}=(2\pi)^{-1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\,\mathrm{d}\theta . 
 
 
 Lemma 2.2 . 
 
 Let  f ∈ C c ∞ ​ ( ℝ 2 ) f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})  be real and satisfy  f ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 f(0)=0 . Define 
 

 
 (2.12) 
 
 g θ ​ ( t ) = 1 2 ​ π ​ ∫ ℝ 2 Δ ​ f ​ ( w ) ​ ( log ⁡ | v θ ⋅ w − t | − log ⁡ | v θ ⋅ w | ) ​ d ​ m 2 ​ ( w ) . g_{\theta}(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Delta f(w)\bigl(\log|v_{\theta}\cdot w-t|-\log|v_{\theta}\cdot w|\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}m_{2}(w). 
 
 
 Then  g θ g_{\theta}  is smooth,  g θ ​ ( 0 ) = 0 g_{\theta}(0)=0 , and its derivatives on
bounded intervals are uniformly bounded in  θ \theta . Moreover, 
 

 
 (2.13) 
 
 f ⁡ ( z ) = av θ g θ ​ ( v θ ⋅ z ) . f(z)=\mathop{\mathrm{av}}\nolimits_{\theta}g_{\theta}(v_{\theta}\cdot z). 
 
 
 For compact  K , Z ⊂ ℝ 2 K,Z\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}  and  R < ∞ R<\infty , 
 

 
 (2.14) 
 
 sup θ ∈ [ 0 , 2 ​ π ] , z ∈ Z | a | ≤ R ∫ K ( | log ⁡ | v θ ⋅ ( w − z ) − a | | + | log ⁡ | v θ ⋅ w | | ) ​ d ​ m 2 ​ ( w ) < ∞ . \sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta\in[0,2\pi],\,z\in Z\\
|a|\leq R\end{subarray}}\int_{K}\left(|\log|v_{\theta}\cdot(w-z)-a||+|\log|v_{\theta}\cdot w||\right)\,\mathrm{d}m_{2}(w)<\infty. 
 
 
 Thus Fubini’s theorem applies to integration in the angular and
planar variables for finite sums with bounded measurable shifts  a = a j ​ ( θ ) a=a_{j}(\theta) . For each  R 1 < ∞ R_{1}<\infty , 
 

 
 (2.15) 
 
 | g θ ​ ( t ) | + | t 2 ​ g θ ′′ ​ ( t ) + 2 ​ t ​ g θ ′ ​ ( t ) | ≤ C R 1 ​ | t | , | t | ≤ R 1 . |g_{\theta}(t)|+|t^{2}g_{\theta}^{\prime\prime}(t)+2tg_{\theta}^{\prime}(t)|\leq C_{R_{1}}|t|,\qquad|t|\leq R_{1}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Choose  R 0 R_{0}  so every  K − z K-z ,  z ∈ Z ∪ { 0 } z\in Z\cup\{0\} , lies in the disk
of radius  R 0 R_{0} . Coordinates along  v θ v_{\theta}  bound the first
logarithmic integral in ( 2.14 ) by 
 

 
 
 2 ​ R 0 ​ ∫ − R 0 R 0 | log ⁡ | s − a | | ​ 𝑑 s ≤ 2 ​ R 0 ​ ∫ − R 0 − R R 0 + R | log ⁡ | t | | ​ 𝑑 t < ∞ . 2R_{0}\int_{-R_{0}}^{R_{0}}|\log|s-a||\,\mathrm{d}s\leq 2R_{0}\int_{-R_{0}-R}^{R_{0}+R}|\log|t||\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty. 
 
 
 The second term is the case  a = 0 , z = 0 a=0,z=0 ; Tonelli gives the stated
Fubini consequence. 
 
 
 The directional integrals
 a θ ​ ( s ) = ∫ ℝ Δ ​ f ​ ( s ​ v θ + u ​ v θ ⟂ ) ​ 𝑑 u a_{\theta}(s)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Delta f(sv_{\theta}+uv_{\theta}^{\perp})\,\mathrm{d}u 
have common compact support and uniform bounds on all derivatives.
After translation, the first term of ( 2.12 ) is
 ( 2 ​ π ) − 1 ​ ∫ log ⁡ | u | ​ a θ ​ ( u + t ) ​ 𝑑 u (2\pi)^{-1}\int\log|u|a_{\theta}(u+t)\,\mathrm{d}u .
Differentiation falls on  a θ a_{\theta} , proving the uniform smoothness.
The subtraction gives  g θ ​ ( 0 ) = 0 g_{\theta}(0)=0  and therefore
( 2.15 ). 
 
 
 Finally, 
 

 
 
 av θ ( log ⁡ | v θ ⋅ ( w − z ) | − log ⁡ | v θ ⋅ w | ) = log | w − z | − log ⁡ | w | \mathop{\mathrm{av}}\nolimits_{\theta}\bigl(\log|v_{\theta}\cdot(w-z)|-\log|v_{\theta}\cdot w|\bigr)=\log|w-z|-\log|w| 
 
 
 locally integrably in  w w , since the two constants  − log ⁡ 2 -\log 2  cancel.
The established Fubini bound and
 Δ ​ log ⁡ | w − z | = 2 ​ π ​ δ z \Delta\log|w-z|=2\pi\delta_{z}  give 
 

 
 
 av θ g θ ​ ( v θ ⋅ z ) = 1 2 ​ π ​ ∫ Δ ​ f ​ ( w ) ​ ( log ⁡ | w − z | − log ⁡ | w | ) ​ d ​ m 2 ​ ( w ) = f ⁡ ( z ) − f ⁡ ( 0 ) . \mathop{\mathrm{av}}\nolimits_{\theta}g_{\theta}(v_{\theta}\cdot z)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\Delta f(w)(\log|w-z|-\log|w|)\,\mathrm{d}m_{2}(w)=f(z)-f(0). 
 
 
 Thus the logarithms are paired with smooth area tests before the
tracial ridge identity is used.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 2.3.  The smooth comparison identity 

 
 Put  ℰ = z ⋅ ∇ \mathcal{E}=z\cdot\nabla  and  𝒬 = ℰ ⁡ ( ℰ + 1 ) \mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{E}+1) . 
 
 
 Lemma 2.3 . 
 
 For a real-valued smooth function  f f  on a neighbourhood of
 { 0 } ∪ supp ⁡ μ A , B ∪ supp ⁡ Ψ T ∪ spec ⁡ T \{0\}\cup\operatorname{supp}\mu_{A,B}\cup\operatorname{supp}\Psi_{T}\cup\operatorname{spec}T , 
 

 
 (2.16) 
 
 d ​ f ​ ( 0 ) + ∫ 𝒬 ​ f ​ d ​ μ A , B − ∑ j f ⁡ ( λ j ​ ( T ) ) = 1 2 ​ π ​ ∫ ℂ Ψ T ​ Δ ​ f ​ d ​ m 2 . df(0)+\int\mathcal{Q}f\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{A,B}-\sum_{j}f(\lambda_{j}(T))=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\Psi_{T}\Delta f\,\mathrm{d}m_{2}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Choose a compactly supported cutoff equal to one on a neighbourhood of
 supp ⁡ μ A , B \operatorname{supp}\mu_{A,B} ,  supp ⁡ Ψ T \operatorname{supp}\Psi_{T} , and the spectrum of  T T , and subtract
 f ⁡ ( 0 ) f(0) . It is therefore enough to prove the identity for
 f ∈ C c ∞ ​ ( ℝ 2 ) f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})  with  f ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 f(0)=0 . 
 
 
 The one-variable inverse of  ℋ \mathcal{H}  is
 Q = t ∂ t ( t ∂ t + 1 ) Q=t\partial_{t}(t\partial_{t}+1) 
 [ 3 , Remark 2, p. 40] . If  G ⁡ ( r ) = g ⁡ ( r ​ u ) G(r)=g(ru) ,
integration by parts gives 
 

 
 
 ℋ ⁡ ( Q ​ g ) ​ ( u ) = ∫ 0 1 ( 1 − r ) ​ { r ​ G ′′ ​ ( r ) + 2 ​ G ′ ​ ( r ) } ​ 𝑑 r = g ⁡ ( u ) − g ⁡ ( 0 ) . \mathcal{H}(Qg)(u)=\int_{0}^{1}(1-r)\{rG^{\prime\prime}(r)+2G^{\prime}(r)\}\,\mathrm{d}r=g(u)-g(0). 
 
 
 The Euler operator commutes with ridge composition. Consequently
Lemma  2.2  and the full tracial ridge identity
( 2.1 ) imply 
 

 
 (2.17) 
 
 ∫ 𝒬 ​ f ​ d ​ μ A , B = av θ tr ⁡ g θ ​ ( A ​ cos ⁡ θ + B ​ sin ⁡ θ ) . \int\mathcal{Q}f\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{A,B}=\mathop{\mathrm{av}}\nolimits_{\theta}\operatorname{tr}g_{\theta}(A\cos\theta+B\sin\theta). 
 
 
 This use of Fubini is absolute: on the compact support of
 μ A , B \mu_{A,B} , ( 2.15 ) gives
 | Q ​ g θ ​ ( v θ ⋅ z ) | ≤ C ​ | z | |Qg_{\theta}(v_{\theta}\cdot z)|\leq C|z| , and
( 2.5 ) with exponent one gives
 ∫ | z | ​ d ​ μ A , B < ∞ \int|z|\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{A,B}<\infty . 
 
 
 Let  X θ = A ​ cos ⁡ θ + B ​ sin ⁡ θ X_{\theta}=A\cos\theta+B\sin\theta . Inserting
( 2.12 ) and using the spectral theorem, 
 

 
 
 tr ⁡ g θ ​ ( X θ ) \displaystyle\operatorname{tr}g_{\theta}(X_{\theta}) 
 = 1 2 ​ π ​ ∫ Δ ​ f ​ ( w ) ​ { log ⁡ | det ( v θ ⋅ w ​ I − X θ ) | − d ​ log ⁡ | v θ ⋅ w | } ​ d ​ m 2 ​ ( w ) . \displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\Delta f(w)\left\{\log\left|\det(v_{\theta}\cdot w\,I-X_{\theta})\right|-d\log|v_{\theta}\cdot w|\right\}\,\mathrm{d}m_{2}(w). 
 
 
 Here
 | det ( v θ ⋅ w ​ I − X θ ) | = | det Re ⁡ ( e − i ​ θ ​ ( T − w ​ I ) ) | |\det(v_{\theta}\cdot w\,I-X_{\theta})|=|\det\operatorname{Re}(e^{-i\theta}(T-wI))| .
Every eigenvalue of  X θ X_{\theta}  lies in
 [ − ( ‖ A ‖ + ‖ B ‖ ) , ‖ A ‖ + ‖ B ‖ ] [-(\|A\|+\|B\|),\|A\|+\|B\|] . Taking the eigenvalues in increasing
order makes  a j ​ ( θ ) = λ j ​ ( X θ ) a_{j}(\theta)=\lambda_{j}(X_{\theta})  continuous (hence measurable)
in  θ \theta . Lemma  2.2  therefore applies to these bounded shifts and justifies
the interchange of angular and planar integration by absolute
integrability. Averaging in  θ \theta  therefore gives 
 

 
 (2.18) 
 
 av θ tr ⁡ g θ ​ ( X θ ) = 1 2 ​ π ​ ∫ Δ ​ f ​ ( w ) ​ ( L T ​ ( w ) − d ​ log ⁡ | w | ) ​ d ​ m 2 ​ ( w ) . \mathop{\mathrm{av}}\nolimits_{\theta}\operatorname{tr}g_{\theta}(X_{\theta})=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\Delta f(w)(L_{T}(w)-d\log|w|)\,\mathrm{d}m_{2}(w). 
 
 
 By definition  L T = Ψ T + log ⁡ | det ( T − w ​ I ) | L_{T}=\Psi_{T}+\log|\det(T-wI)| . Green’s formula and
 log ⁡ | det ( T − w ​ I ) | = ∑ j log ⁡ | w − λ j ​ ( T ) | \log|\det(T-wI)|=\sum_{j}\log|w-\lambda_{j}(T)|  yield 
 

 
 
 1 2 ​ π ​ ∫ Δ ​ f ​ ( w ) ​ log ⁡ | det ( T − w ​ I ) | ​ d ​ m 2 ​ ( w ) = ∑ j f ⁡ ( λ j ​ ( T ) ) , \frac{1}{2\pi}\int\Delta f(w)\log|\det(T-wI)|\,\mathrm{d}m_{2}(w)=\sum_{j}f(\lambda_{j}(T)), 
 
 
 while the same formula with  log ⁡ | w | \log|w|  gives  f ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 f(0)=0 . Finally,
 Ψ T ∈ L q \Psi_{T}\in L^{q}  for every finite  q q  and  Δ ​ f \Delta f  is bounded and
compactly supported, so its term is absolutely integrable. Combining
these identities with ( 2.17 ) proves
( 2.16 ). Restoring the subtracted constant gives
the term  d ​ f ​ ( 0 ) df(0) .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 2.4.  Nonhomogeneous transfer and homogeneous consequences 

 
 For planar  f f  with  f ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 f(0)=0 , put
 ℋ ​ f ​ ( z ) = ∫ 0 1 ( 1 − r ) ​ f ​ ( r ​ z ) ​ 𝑑 r / r \mathcal{H}f(z)=\int_{0}^{1}(1-r)f(rz)\,\mathrm{d}r/r  and define 
 

 
 (2.19) 
 
 Θ T ​ ( w ) = ∫ 0 1 1 − r r ​ Ψ T ​ ( w / r ) ​ 𝑑 r . \Theta_{T}(w)=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1-r}{r}\Psi_{T}(w/r)\,\mathrm{d}r. 
 
 
 This function is nonnegative and supported in  | w | ≤ ‖ T ‖ |w|\leq\|T\| .
Minkowski’s inequality and planar scaling give 
 

 
 (2.20) 
 
 ‖ Θ T ‖ s ≤ ‖ Ψ T ‖ s ​ ∫ 0 1 ( 1 − r ) ​ r 2 / s − 1 ​ 𝑑 r = s 2 2 ​ ( s + 2 ) ​ ‖ Ψ T ‖ s , 1 ≤ s < ∞ . \|\Theta_{T}\|_{s}\leq\|\Psi_{T}\|_{s}\int_{0}^{1}(1-r)r^{2/s-1}\,\mathrm{d}r=\frac{s^{2}}{2(s+2)}\|\Psi_{T}\|_{s},\qquad 1\leq s<\infty. 
 
 
 
 
 Theorem 2.4 . 
 
 Let  T = A + i ​ B T=A+iB  be a finite matrix, and let  D D  be an open disk centered
at zero containing
 supp ⁡ μ A , B ∪ supp ⁡ Ψ T ∪ spec ⁡ T \operatorname{supp}\mu_{A,B}\cup\operatorname{supp}\Psi_{T}\cup\operatorname{spec}T .
Let  f ∈ W loc 2 , s ​ ( D ) f\in W^{2,s}_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)  be real-valued, with
 1 < s < ∞ 1<s<\infty , and use its continuous representative. If  f ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 f(0)=0 , then 
 

 
 (2.21) 
 
 ∑ j ( ℋ ​ f ) ​ ( λ j ​ ( T ) ) − ∫ f ​ d ​ μ A , B = 1 2 ​ π ​ ∫ Θ T ​ ( − Δ ​ f ) ​ d ​ m 2 . \sum_{j}(\mathcal{H}f)(\lambda_{j}(T))-\int f\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{A,B}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\Theta_{T}(-\Delta f)\,\mathrm{d}m_{2}. 
 
 
 All terms are finite; the right side is nonnegative if  Δ ​ f ≤ 0 \Delta f\leq 0 . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 For smooth  f f , radial integration gives
 𝒬 ​ ℋ ​ f = f \mathcal{Q}\mathcal{H}f=f  and
 Δ ​ ℋ ​ f ​ ( z ) = ∫ 0 1 r ⁡ ( 1 − r ) ​ Δ ​ f ​ ( r ​ z ) ​ 𝑑 r \Delta\mathcal{H}f(z)=\int_{0}^{1}r(1-r)\Delta f(rz)\,\mathrm{d}r .
Apply Lemma  2.3  to  ℋ ​ f \mathcal{H}f  and change variables
 w = r ​ z w=rz  in the potential term. On a closed disk containing the
supports and their radial contractions, absolute Fubini follows from
 ‖ Ψ T ‖ 1 ​ ‖ Δ ​ f ‖ ∞ ​ ∫ 0 1 r ⁡ ( 1 − r ) ​ 𝑑 r < ∞ \|\Psi_{T}\|_{1}\|\Delta f\|_{\infty}\int_{0}^{1}r(1-r)\,\mathrm{d}r<\infty .
This proves ( 2.21 ) for smooth local tests. 
 
 
 Choose nested closed disks in  D D  whose interiors contain all the
supports. After multiplying  f f  by an outer cutoff equal to one on
the larger disk, mollify and subtract the value at zero:
 f ε = f ∗ η ε − ( f ∗ η ε ) ​ ( 0 ) f_{\varepsilon}=f*\eta_{\varepsilon}-(f*\eta_{\varepsilon})(0) .
Choose  0 < α < min ⁡ { 1 , 2 − 2 / s } 0<\alpha<\min\{1,2-2/s\} . Sobolev–Morrey embedding gives
 f ε → f f_{\varepsilon}\to f  in  C 0 , α C^{0,\alpha}  and  W 2 , s W^{2,s}  on the inner
disk. Put
 a ε = ‖ f ε − f ‖ C 0 , α → 0 a_{\varepsilon}=\|f_{\varepsilon}-f\|_{C^{0,\alpha}}\to 0 
there. Since  f ε ​ ( 0 ) = f ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 f_{\varepsilon}(0)=f(0)=0 , the measure and spectral
errors are bounded by 
 

 
 
 a ε ​ ∫ | z | α ​ d ​ μ A , B , a ε α ⁡ ( α + 1 ) ​ ∑ j | λ j ​ ( T ) | α . a_{\varepsilon}\int|z|^{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{A,B},\qquad\frac{a_{\varepsilon}}{\alpha(\alpha+1)}\sum_{j}|\lambda_{j}(T)|^{\alpha}. 
 
 
 They tend to zero by ( 2.5 ). The potential error is
at most
 ‖ Θ T ‖ s ′ ​ ‖ Δ ⁡ ( f ε − f ) ‖ s / ( 2 ​ π ) \|\Theta_{T}\|_{s^{\prime}}\|\Delta(f_{\varepsilon}-f)\|_{s}/(2\pi) ,
where  s ′ = s / ( s − 1 ) s^{\prime}=s/(s-1) , and tends to zero by
( 2.20 ). The same estimates establish finiteness of
all three terms for  f f , and passage to the limit proves the theorem.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Corollary 2.5 . 
 
 Let  U U  satisfy the hypotheses of Theorem  2.4 .
For finite families of real coefficients  c j , u j , v j c_{j},u_{j},v_{j} , let
 ϕ j : ℝ → ℝ \phi_{j}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}  be measurable, with  ϕ j ​ ( 0 ) = 0 \phi_{j}(0)=0  and
 ∫ − M M | ϕ j ​ ( t ) / t | ​ 𝑑 t < ∞ \int_{-M}^{M}|\phi_{j}(t)/t|\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty  for every  M > 0 M>0 , and set
 F ⁡ ( x , y ) = ∑ j c j ​ ϕ j ​ ( u j ​ x + v j ​ y ) F(x,y)=\sum_{j}c_{j}\phi_{j}(u_{j}x+v_{j}y) . Then 
 

 
 
 ∑ k ( ℋ ​ U ) ​ ( λ k ​ ( T ) ) − ∑ j c j ​ tr ⁡ ( ℋ ​ ϕ j ) ​ ( u j ​ A + v j ​ B ) \displaystyle\sum_{k}(\mathcal{H}U)(\lambda_{k}(T))-\sum_{j}c_{j}\operatorname{tr}(\mathcal{H}\phi_{j})(u_{j}A+v_{j}B) 
 = ∫ ( U − F ) ​ d ​ μ A , B \displaystyle=\int(U-F)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{A,B} 
 
 
 (2.22) 
 
 
 + 1 2 ​ π ∫ Θ T ( − Δ U ) d m 2 . \displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\Theta_{T}(-\Delta U)\,\mathrm{d}m_{2}. 
 
 
 All terms are finite. Both remainders are nonnegative if
 U ≥ F U\geq F  on  D D  and  Δ ​ U ≤ 0 \Delta U\leq 0 . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The full ridge identity ( 2.1 ), applied to  | ϕ j | |\phi_{j}| ,
proves absolute integrability of each ridge. Its signed sum for
 ϕ j \phi_{j}  gives
 ∫ F ​ d ​ μ A , B = ∑ j c j ​ tr ⁡ ( ℋ ​ ϕ j ) ​ ( u j ​ A + v j ​ B ) \int F\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{A,B}=\sum_{j}c_{j}\operatorname{tr}(\mathcal{H}\phi_{j})(u_{j}A+v_{j}B) .
Combining this with ( 2.21 ) proves the identity,
and positivity gives the final assertion.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Corollary 2.6 . 
 
 Let  U U  be real-valued,  p p -homogeneous, and belong to
 C 1 ​ ( ℂ ) ∩ W loc 2 , q ​ ( ℂ ) C^{1}(\mathbb{C})\cap W^{2,q}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{C})  for some  q > 1 q>1 .
For finite families of real coefficients  c j , u j , v j c_{j},u_{j},v_{j} , set 
 

 
 
 F ⁡ ( x , y ) = ∑ j c j ​ | u j ​ x + v j ​ y | p , ℱ p ​ ( A , B ) = ∑ j c j ​ tr ⁡ | u j ​ A + v j ​ B | p . F(x,y)=\sum_{j}c_{j}|u_{j}x+v_{j}y|^{p},\qquad\mathcal{F}_{p}(A,B)=\sum_{j}c_{j}\operatorname{tr}|u_{j}A+v_{j}B|^{p}. 
 
 
 Then 
 

 
 
 ∑ k U ⁡ ( λ k ​ ( T ) ) − ℱ p ​ ( A , B ) \displaystyle\sum_{k}U(\lambda_{k}(T))-\mathcal{F}_{p}(A,B) 
 = p ⁡ ( p + 1 ) ​ ∫ ( U − F ) ​ d ​ μ A , B \displaystyle=p(p+1)\int(U-F)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{A,B} 
 
 
 (2.23) 
 
 
 + 1 2 ​ π ∫ ℂ Ψ T ( − Δ U ) d m 2 . \displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\Psi_{T}(-\Delta U)\,\mathrm{d}m_{2}. 
 
 
 Both remainder terms are nonnegative if  U ≥ F U\geq F  and  Δ ​ U ≤ 0 \Delta U\leq 0 .
We call such a function  U U  an admissible superharmonic majorant of  F F . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Homogeneity gives  ℋ ​ U = U / [ p ⁡ ( p + 1 ) ] \mathcal{H}U=U/[p(p+1)]  and
 Δ ​ U ​ ( r ​ z ) = r p − 2 ​ Δ ​ U ​ ( z ) \Delta U(rz)=r^{p-2}\Delta U(z)  almost everywhere.
Hölder’s inequality and the compact support of  Ψ T \Psi_{T}  give
 J = ∫ Ψ T ​ | Δ ​ U | ​ d ​ m 2 < ∞ J=\int\Psi_{T}|\Delta U|\,\mathrm{d}m_{2}<\infty .
Tonelli’s theorem and the substitution  w = r ​ z w=rz  therefore yield 
 

 
 
 ∫ Θ T ​ | Δ ​ U | ​ d ​ m 2 = J ​ ∫ 0 1 ( 1 − r ) ​ r p − 1 ​ 𝑑 r = J p ⁡ ( p + 1 ) . \int\Theta_{T}|\Delta U|\,\mathrm{d}m_{2}=J\int_{0}^{1}(1-r)r^{p-1}\,\mathrm{d}r=\frac{J}{p(p+1)}. 
 
 
 The same change of variables is consequently valid for the signed
Laplacian integral. Apply Corollary  2.5 
with  ϕ j ​ ( t ) = | t | p \phi_{j}(t)=|t|^{p} , for which
 ℋ ​ ϕ j = ϕ j / [ p ⁡ ( p + 1 ) ] \mathcal{H}\phi_{j}=\phi_{j}/[p(p+1)] , and multiply by  p ⁡ ( p + 1 ) p(p+1) .
This proves ( 2.23 ), including its positivity assertion.
∎ 
 
 
 
 For a homogeneous  V V  in the same regularity class and a nilpotent
 N = A + i ​ B N=A+iB , Corollary  2.6  with  F = 0 F=0  specializes to 
 

 
 (2.24) 
 
 p ⁡ ( p + 1 ) ​ ∫ ℂ V ​ d ​ μ A , B = 1 2 ​ π ​ ∫ ℂ Ψ N ​ Δ ​ V ​ d ​ m 2 . p(p+1)\int_{\mathbb{C}}V\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{A,B}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\Psi_{N}\Delta V\,\mathrm{d}m_{2}. 
 
 
 Corollary  2.6  supplies the forward implication for
every finite signed real ridge sum. Sections  3 – 3.4 
prove the converse, establishing the necessity and sufficiency of
the superharmonic-majorant condition for the full class in Theorem  1.3 . 
 
 
 
 2.5.  The sharp coordinate specialization 

 
 The sharp coordinate estimate uses the classical explicit superharmonic
majorant. The obstacle classification treats general signed ridge sums
and identifies their optimal spectral correction. 
 
 
 Corollary 2.7 . 
 
 For every  1 < p < ∞ 1<p<\infty , 
 

 
 (2.25) 
 
 sup N ≠ 0 ​ finite ​ nilpotent ‖ Im ⁡ N ‖ p ‖ Re ⁡ N ‖ p = sup ℰ ‖ H ℰ ‖ 𝒮 p → 𝒮 p = C p . \sup_{N\neq 0\ {\rm finite\ nilpotent}}\frac{\|\operatorname{Im}N\|_{p}}{\|\operatorname{Re}N\|_{p}}=\sup_{\mathcal{E}}\|H_{\mathcal{E}}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{p}\to\mathcal{S}_{p}}=C_{p}. 
 
 
 Both estimates hold uniformly at every matrix amplification. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  U p U_{p}  be the sectorwise superharmonic majorant in
Appendix  A  and put  V ⁡ ( x , y ) = U p ​ ( y , x ) V(x,y)=U_{p}(y,x) .
Its elementary verification gives 
 

 
 
 V ⁡ ( x , y ) ≥ | y | p − C p p ​ | x | p , Δ ​ V ≤ 0 , V ⁡ ( x , 0 ) ≤ 0 . V(x,y)\geq|y|^{p}-C_{p}^{p}|x|^{p},\qquad\Delta V\leq 0,\qquad V(x,0)\leq 0. 
 
 
 For nilpotent  N = A + i ​ B N=A+iB , the spectral term in
Corollary  2.6  is zero, yielding
 ‖ B ‖ p ≤ C p ​ ‖ A ‖ p \|B\|_{p}\leq C_{p}\|A\|_{p} .
For Hermitian  X X , the matrix  T = X + i ​ H ℰ ​ X T=X+iH_{\mathcal{E}}X  is block upper
triangular with Hermitian diagonal blocks, so its spectrum is real.
The same corollary gives 
 

 
 
 ‖ H ℰ ​ X ‖ p p − C p p ​ ‖ X ‖ p p ≤ ∑ λ ∈ spec ⁡ T V ⁡ ( λ ) ≤ 0 . \|H_{\mathcal{E}}X\|_{p}^{p}-C_{p}^{p}\|X\|_{p}^{p}\leq\sum_{\lambda\in\operatorname{spec}T}V(\lambda)\leq 0. 
 
 
 For arbitrary  X X , apply this to
 ( 0 X X ∗ 0 ) \left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&X\\
X^{*}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right) 
and the amplified flag; both  p p th powers acquire the factor two.
Arbitrary block sizes also give the bound at every amplification. 
 
 
 For sharpness, analytic polynomials  q q  with  q ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 q(0)=0  approach the
classical Pichorides constant  [ 14 ,  9 ] .
Lemma  3.1  transfers their ratios to  q ⁡ ( S n ) q(S_{n}) .
These strictly upper-triangular matrices satisfy
 Im ⁡ q ⁡ ( S n ) = H ℰ ​ Re ⁡ q ⁡ ( S n ) \operatorname{Im}q(S_{n})=H_{\mathcal{E}}\operatorname{Re}q(S_{n})  for the standard flag,
giving both lower bounds in ( 2.25 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 3.  Proof of the classification and optimal correction 

 
 3.1.  Finite Toeplitz sections 

 
 The lower bounds in the two parts use the same finite-section limit.
Its singular-value form handles arbitrary complex symbols; its
self-adjoint form supplies the continuous spectral tests needed for the
nilpotent classification. 
 
 
 Lemma 3.1 . 
 
 Let  Q ⁡ ( z ) = ∑ k = − r r Q k ​ z k Q(z)=\sum_{k=-r}^{r}Q_{k}z^{k}  be an  M d ​ ( ℂ ) M_{d}(\mathbb{C}) -valued
trigonometric polynomial, with  d d  and  r r  fixed, and put 
 

 
 
 A n ​ ( Q ) = ( Q j − i ) 1 ≤ i , j ≤ n , n > 2 ​ r . A_{n}(Q)=(Q_{j-i})_{1\leq i,j\leq n},\qquad n>2r. 
 
 
 For every  1 ≤ p < ∞ 1\leq p<\infty , 
 

 
 (3.1) 
 
 lim n → ∞ 1 n ​ Tr n ​ d ​ | A n ​ ( Q ) | p = ∫ 0 2 ​ π Tr d ⁡ | Q ⁡ ( e i ​ t ) | p ​ d ​ t 2 ​ π . \lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{Tr}_{nd}|A_{n}(Q)|^{p}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\operatorname{Tr}_{d}|Q(e^{it})|^{p}\frac{dt}{2\pi}. 
 
 
 If  Q ⁡ ( e i ​ t ) Q(e^{it})  is Hermitian for every  t t  and  g g  is continuous on a
common interval containing the spectra of  A n ​ ( Q ) A_{n}(Q)  and  Q ⁡ ( e i ​ t ) Q(e^{it}) , then 
 

 
 (3.2) 
 
 lim n → ∞ 1 n ​ Tr n ​ d ⁡ g ⁡ ( A n ​ ( Q ) ) = ∫ 0 2 ​ π Tr d ⁡ g ⁡ ( Q ⁡ ( e i ​ t ) ) ​ d ​ t 2 ​ π . \lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{Tr}_{nd}g(A_{n}(Q))=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\operatorname{Tr}_{d}g(Q(e^{it}))\frac{dt}{2\pi}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  S n S_{n}  be the cyclic shift with a one at  ( i , j ) (i,j)  when
 j − i ≡ 1 ( mod n ) j-i\equiv 1\pmod{n} , and set 
 

 
 
 C n ​ ( Q ) = ∑ k = − r r S n k ⊗ Q k , R = ∑ k = − r r ‖ Q k ‖ . C_{n}(Q)=\sum_{k=-r}^{r}S_{n}^{k}\otimes Q_{k},\qquad R=\sum_{k=-r}^{r}\|Q_{k}\|. 
 
 
 The difference  E n = A n ​ ( Q ) − C n ​ ( Q ) E_{n}=A_{n}(Q)-C_{n}(Q)  is supported in the first and last
 r r  block rows. Hence 
 

 
 
 rank E n ≤ 2 r d , ∥ E n ∥ ≤ 2 R , ∥ A n ( Q ) ∥ , ∥ C n ( Q ) ∥ ≤ R . \operatorname{rank}E_{n}\leq 2rd,\qquad\|E_{n}\|\leq 2R,\qquad\|A_{n}(Q)\|,\ \|C_{n}(Q)\|\leq R. 
 
 
 The last bound for the Toeplitz matrix follows by compressing the
Laurent operator, or directly by the triangle inequality for its
partial-shift terms. Therefore 
 

 
 
 ( 1 n ​ Tr n ​ d ​ | E n | p ) 1 / p ≤ 2 ​ R ​ ( 2 ​ r ​ d / n ) 1 / p ⟶ 0 . \left(\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{Tr}_{nd}|E_{n}|^{p}\right)^{1/p}\leq 2R(2rd/n)^{1/p}\longrightarrow 0. 
 
 
 The discrete Fourier transform on the first tensor factor makes
 C n ​ ( Q ) C_{n}(Q)  block diagonal, with blocks  Q ⁡ ( ζ n ℓ ) Q(\zeta_{n}^{\ell}) ,
 0 ≤ ℓ < n 0\leq\ell<n , where  ζ n = e 2 ​ π ​ i / n \zeta_{n}=e^{2\pi i/n} . The triangle inequality
in the Schatten  p p  norm scaled by  n − 1 / p n^{-1/p}  and the Riemann-sum limit prove
( 3.1 ). This uses singular values, without
assuming that  Q Q  or its Toeplitz sections are normal. 
 
 
 For the second assertion both matrices are Hermitian. For an integer
 k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 , telescoping the difference of their  k k th powers and using
 ‖ E n ‖ 1 ≤ 2 ​ R ​ rank ⁡ E n \|E_{n}\|_{1}\leq 2R\operatorname{rank}E_{n}  gives 
 

 
 
 1 n ​ | Tr n ​ d ⁡ ( A n ​ ( Q ) k − C n ​ ( Q ) k ) | ≤ 2 ​ k ​ R k ​ rank ⁡ E n n ⟶ 0 . \frac{1}{n}\left|\operatorname{Tr}_{nd}(A_{n}(Q)^{k}-C_{n}(Q)^{k})\right|\leq\frac{2kR^{k}\operatorname{rank}E_{n}}{n}\longrightarrow 0. 
 
 
 The constant polynomial has equal traces on both sides. The same
conclusion follows for every polynomial, and uniform polynomial
approximation on the common spectral interval proves the assertion for
 g g . Fourier block diagonalization and the Riemann-sum limit now yield
( 3.2 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 3.2.  Nilpotent Toeplitz realization 

 
 Let  S n S_{n}  have ones on its first superdiagonal and zeros elsewhere.
For  q ⁡ ( z ) = ∑ k = 1 d α k ​ z k q(z)=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\alpha_{k}z^{k}  put  N n = q ⁡ ( S n ) N_{n}=q(S_{n}) , which is
strictly upper triangular. For real  a , b a,b , set 
 

 
 
 X n = a ​ Re ⁡ N n + b ​ Im ⁡ N n , h ⁡ ( t ) = a ​ Re ⁡ q ⁡ ( e i ​ t ) + b ​ Im ⁡ q ⁡ ( e i ​ t ) . X_{n}=a\operatorname{Re}N_{n}+b\operatorname{Im}N_{n},\qquad h(t)=a\operatorname{Re}q(e^{it})+b\operatorname{Im}q(e^{it}). 
 
 
 
 
 For the scalar Hermitian symbol  Q ⁡ ( e i ​ t ) = h ⁡ ( t ) Q(e^{it})=h(t) , the Toeplitz
section  A n ​ ( Q ) A_{n}(Q)  is exactly  X n X_{n} .
Lemma  3.1 , with  d = 1 d=1 , therefore gives, for
every continuous real function  g g  on a common interval containing
the spectra of  X n X_{n}  and the range of  h h , 
 

 
 (3.3) 
 
 lim n → ∞ 1 n ​ tr ⁡ g ⁡ ( X n ) = ∫ 0 2 ​ π g ⁡ ( h ⁡ ( t ) ) ​ d ​ t 2 ​ π . \lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{tr}g(X_{n})=\int_{0}^{2\pi}g(h(t))\frac{\,\mathrm{d}t}{2\pi}. 
 
 
 
 
 Taking  g ⁡ ( s ) = | s | p g(s)=|s|^{p}  and summing the finitely many directions gives 
 

 
 (3.4) 
 
 1 n ​ ℱ p ​ ( Re ⁡ q ⁡ ( S n ) , Im ⁡ q ⁡ ( S n ) ) ⟶ ∫ 0 2 ​ π F ⁡ ( q ⁡ ( e i ​ t ) ) ​ d ​ t 2 ​ π . \frac{1}{n}\mathcal{F}_{p}(\operatorname{Re}q(S_{n}),\operatorname{Im}q(S_{n}))\longrightarrow\int_{0}^{2\pi}F(q(e^{it}))\frac{\,\mathrm{d}t}{2\pi}. 
 
 
 Thus (N) implies (P). It also follows that a strict failure of (P)
produces a finite nilpotent counterexample for all sufficiently
large  n n . 
 
 
 Lemma 3.2 . 
 
 For  p > 0 p>0  and  h ∈ L 1 ​ ( 𝕋 , ℝ ) h\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})  put  V ⁡ ( r ​ e i ​ θ ) = r p ​ h ​ ( θ ) V(re^{i\theta})=r^{p}h(\theta)  for  r > 0 r>0

 ⟨ Δ V , φ ⟩ = ∫ 0 ∞ r p − 1 ⟨ ρ , φ ( r e i ⋅ ) ⟩ d r . \langle\Delta V,\varphi\rangle=\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{p-1}\langle\rho,\varphi(re^{i\cdot})\rangle\,\mathrm{d}r. 
 
 
 If  ρ \rho  is a finite signed measure,  h h  has a Lipschitz representative

If  p > 1 p>1 ,  h ∈ W 2 , q ​ ( 𝕋 ) h\in W^{2,q}(\mathbb{T}) ,  q > 1 q>1 , and  q ⁡ ( p − 2 ) + 2 > 0 q(p-2)+2>0 ,
then  V ∈ C 1 ​ ( ℂ ) ∩ W loc 2 , q ​ ( ℂ ) V\in C^{1}(\mathbb{C})\cap W^{2,q}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{C}) . 
 
 
 
 Proof.

primitive gives  h ′ ∈ B ​ V h^{\prime}\in BV  and  h h  Lipschitz. The radial measure

and extends by zero, while the Hessian is bounded by
 C ​ r p − 2 ​ ( | h | + | h ′ | + | h ′′ | ) Cr^{p-2}(|h|+|h^{\prime}|+|h^{\prime\prime}|) . The radial integral  ∫ 0 1 r q ⁡ ( p − 2 ) + 1 ​ 𝑑 r \int_{0}^{1}r^{q(p-2)+1}\,\mathrm{d}r  is finite;
gradient boundary terms  O ⁡ ( ε p ) O(\varepsilon^{p})  make it the global weak Hessian.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 3.3.  The homogeneous Poisson envelope 

 
 For a continuous real function  G G  on  ℂ \mathbb{C} , let 
 

 
 (3.5) 
 
 𝒫 ( − G ) ( z ) = inf h ​  holomorphic near  ​ 𝔻 ¯ h ⁡ ( 0 ) = z ∫ 0 2 ​ π − G ( h ( e i ​ t ) ) d ​ t 2 ​ π . \mathcal{P}(-G)(z)=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}h\text{ holomorphic near }\overline{\mathbb{D}}\\
h(0)=z\end{subarray}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}-G(h(e^{it}))\frac{\,\mathrm{d}t}{2\pi}. 
 
 
 The Poletsky–Rosay theorem says that this function is subharmonic
(possibly identically  − ∞ -\infty ) and is the largest subharmonic
minorant of  − G -G . The disc maps extend holomorphically past the closed
unit disc; constant discs are allowed. We use the theorem on  ℂ \mathbb{C} 
only; see  [ 16 ,  17 ]  and the formulation in
 [ 11 , Section 2] .
The analytic-test/majorant implication in the next lemma is also
Cole’s principle  [ 9 , Theorem 2.1] , applied to  − G -G  with
the test set  E = { 0 } E=\{0\} . We include the envelope argument to specify
the least majorant and the angular regularity used below. 
 
 
 Lemma 3.3 . 
 
 Let  G G  be continuous and  p p -homogeneous. Suppose
 ∫ G ⁡ ( q ⁡ ( e i ​ t ) ) ​ 𝑑 t / ( 2 ​ π ) ≤ 0 \int G(q(e^{it}))\,\mathrm{d}t/(2\pi)\leq 0  for every polynomial  q q  with

 U ∗ = − 𝒫 ⁡ ( − G ) U_{*}=-\mathcal{P}(-G)

of  G G . Its angular part is Lipschitz. It is harmonic on the open set
 { U ∗ > G } \{U_{*}>G\} . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Taylor polynomials approximate each disc in ( 3.5 )
uniformly on  𝔻 ¯ \overline{\mathbb{D}} , preserving its center; continuity of  G G 
on their common compact range passes the boundary averages to the
limit. Put  v = 𝒫 ⁡ ( − G ) v=\mathcal{P}(-G) . Every disc centered at zero therefore
has boundary average of  − G -G  at least zero, and the zero disc has
average zero. Hence 
 

 
 (3.6) 
 
 v ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 . v(0)=0. 
 
 
 The Poletsky–Rosay theorem therefore gives a subharmonic  v v  which is
not identically  − ∞ -\infty  and is the largest subharmonic minorant of
 − G -G . 
 
 
 For  r > 0 r>0 , multiplication by  r r  is a bijection between discs centered
at  z z  and discs centered at  r ​ z rz . Since  G ⁡ ( r ​ ζ ) = r p ​ G ​ ( ζ ) G(r\zeta)=r^{p}G(\zeta) , 
 

 
 (3.7)

 Local integrability, Fubini and ( 3.7 ) give
 h ∈ L 1 ​ ( 𝕋 ) h\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T})  with  v ⁡ ( r ​ e i ​ θ ) = r p ​ h ​ ( θ ) v(re^{i\theta})=r^{p}h(\theta)  almost everywhere.
Nonnegative product tests supported in an annulus in Lemma  3.2  give 
 

 
 (3.8) 
 
 ρ = h ′′ + p 2 ​ h ≥ 0 . \rho=h^{\prime\prime}+p^{2}h\geq 0. 
 
 
 Positivity on the compact circle makes  ρ \rho  a finite measure.
The lemma gives a continuous subharmonic  V V , with  V ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 V(0)=0  and Lipschitz  h h .
Its almost-everywhere agreement with  v v  fixes the upper-semicontinuous
subharmonic representative, so  v = V v=V  everywhere. 
 
 
 Set  U ∗ = − v U_{*}=-v . Since  v ≤ − G v\leq-G ,  U ∗ ≥ G U_{*}\geq G ; and because  v v  is the
largest subharmonic minorant of  − G -G ,  U ∗ U_{*}  is the least superharmonic
majorant of  G G . It remains to prove harmonicity on the noncontact
set. Fix  z 0 z_{0}  with  U ∗ ​ ( z 0 ) > G ⁡ ( z 0 ) U_{*}(z_{0})>G(z_{0}) . By continuity, choose a closed
disc  D ¯ ​ ( z 0 , r ) \overline{D}(z_{0},r)  contained in this set so small that 
 

 
 
 inf ∂ D U ∗ > sup D ¯ G . \inf_{\partial D}U_{*}>\sup_{\overline{D}}G. 
 
 
 Let  H D H_{D}  be the harmonic extension to  D D  of  U ∗ | ∂ D U_{*}|_{\partial D} .
The minimum principle gives
 H D ≥ inf ∂ D U ∗ > G H_{D}\geq\inf_{\partial D}U_{*}>G  on  D D , while superharmonicity gives

at most  U ∗ U_{*} , so its circle averages are at most those of  U ∗ U_{*} .
This proves the super-mean-value inequality there as well. Since

Thus  U ∗ U_{*}  is harmonic on the noncontact set.
∎ 
 
 
 
 If  G ⁡ ( − z ) = G ⁡ ( z ) G(-z)=G(z) , the transformation  h ↦ − h h\mapsto-h  in the envelope
shows  U ∗ ​ ( − z ) = U ∗ ​ ( z ) U_{*}(-z)=U_{*}(z) . Notice that this lemma did not use matrices;
the connection to matrices is ( 3.4 ). 
 
 
 
 3.4.  Regularity and completion of the proof 

 
 Lemma 3.4 . 
 
 In Lemma  3.3 , suppose the angular obstacle
 f ⁡ ( θ ) = G ⁡ ( e i ​ θ ) f(\theta)=G(e^{i\theta})  belongs to  C 1 ​ ( 𝕋 ) ∩ W 2 , q ​ ( 𝕋 ) C^{1}(\mathbb{T})\cap W^{2,q}(\mathbb{T})

 C 1 ​ ( 𝕋 ) ∩ W 2 , q ​ ( 𝕋 ) C^{1}(\mathbb{T})\cap W^{2,q}(\mathbb{T}) , and, almost everywhere, 
 

 
 (3.9) 
 
 u ′′ = f ′′ 𝟏 { u = f } − p 2 u 𝟏 { u > f } . u^{\prime\prime}=f^{\prime\prime}\mathbf{1}_{\{u=f\}}-p^{2}u\mathbf{1}_{\{u>f\}}. 
 
 
 If additionally  q ⁡ ( p − 2 ) + 2 > 0 q(p-2)+2>0 , then  U ∗ ∈ 𝒰 p U_{*}\in\mathcal{U}_{p} . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The preceding lemma gives Lipschitz  u u  and
 u ′′ = − p 2 ​ u − ρ u^{\prime\prime}=-p^{2}u-\rho  for a finite positive measure  ρ \rho . Thus  u ′ u^{\prime}  has
a bounded-variation representative and therefore has one-sided limits
at every point. On  O = { u > f } O=\{u>f\} , harmonicity gives
 u ′′ = − p 2 ​ u u^{\prime\prime}=-p^{2}u , so  ρ \rho  is supported on the closed contact set
 K = { u = f } K=\{u=f\} . 
 
 
 Fix  θ ∈ K \theta\in K . Since  u − f ≥ 0 u-f\geq 0  and  ( u − f ) ​ ( θ ) = 0 (u-f)(\theta)=0 , the left
and right difference quotients give

 The atom of the distributional derivative  u ′′ u^{\prime\prime}  at  θ \theta  is the

 The two inequalities force

 ρ ​ { θ } = 0 \rho\{\theta\}=0 . There are no atoms on  O O  either. A BV function
whose derivative has no atoms has a continuous representative, so

 Put  w = u ′ − f ′ w=u^{\prime}-f^{\prime} , a continuous BV function vanishing on  K K .
The derivative of a continuous BV function charges no level set. Indeed,
choose  χ ∈ C c 1 ​ ( ℝ ) \chi\in C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{R})  with  χ ′ ​ ( 0 ) = 1 \chi^{\prime}(0)=1 . The continuous BV

 This chain rule follows from the mean-value theorem in the Stieltjes

against  | D ​ w | |Dw|  makes the right side tend to  D w | { w = 0 } Dw|_{\{w=0\}} .
Thus  D ​ w | K = 0 Dw|_{K}=0 . On  O O  we have  D ​ w = ( − p 2 ​ u − f ′′ ) ​ d ​ θ Dw=(-p^{2}u-f^{\prime\prime})\,\mathrm{d}\theta , so

This proves ( 3.9 ) and  u ∈ C 1 ​ ( 𝕋 ) ∩ W 2 , q ​ ( 𝕋 ) u\in C^{1}(\mathbb{T})\cap W^{2,q}(\mathbb{T}) . 
 
 
 Lemma  3.2  gives the asserted planar regularity;
domination and superharmonicity already follow from the envelope.
∎ 
 
 
 
 For a finite ridge sum, each nonzero angular direction is a multiple
of  | cos ⁡ ( θ − α ) | p |\cos(\theta-\alpha)|^{p} . If  1 < p < 2 1<p<2 , it belongs to  W 2 , q W^{2,q}

 Proof of Theorem  1.3 . 
 
 Lemma  3.1  proves (N) ⇒ \Rightarrow (P), even if only
the indicated Toeplitz nilpotents are tested. Lemmas

with the claimed least majorant and contact equations.
For (S) ⇒ \Rightarrow (N), apply ( 2.23 ). Both remainder terms on
the right are nonnegative, while every eigenvalue of  N N  is zero
and  U ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 U(0)=0 . Thus  ℱ p ≤ 0 \mathcal{F}_{p}\leq 0 . 
 
 
 It remains to prove ( 1.8 ). The spectral identity
applied to  U ∗ U_{*}  gives, whenever  spec ⁡ T = { z } \operatorname{spec}T=\{z\} ,
 n − 1 ​ ℱ p ​ ( Re ⁡ T , Im ⁡ T ) ≤ U ∗ ​ ( z ) n^{-1}\mathcal{F}_{p}(\operatorname{Re}T,\operatorname{Im}T)\leq U_{*}(z) .
Conversely, apply Lemma  3.1  to each translated real

 By Taylor approximation of discs centered at  z z , the supremum of
these integrals is  − 𝒫 ⁡ ( − F ) ​ ( z ) = U ∗ ​ ( z ) -\mathcal{P}(-F)(z)=U_{*}(z) . All the shifted
Toeplitz matrices have spectrum  { z } \{z\} , which proves both equalities.
If ( 1.9 ) holds, these same tests force
 G ⁡ ( z ) ≥ U ∗ ​ ( z ) G(z)\geq U_{*}(z) . The converse follows by summing  G ≥ U ∗ G\geq U_{*}  in the
spectral inequality.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark 3.5 . 
 
 If  p = 2 p=2  and  F ⁡ ( x , y ) = α ​ x 2 + 2 ​ β ​ x ​ y + γ ​ y 2 F(x,y)=\alpha x^{2}+2\beta xy+\gamma y^{2} , then for
nilpotent  N = A + i ​ B N=A+iB , the identity  tr ⁡ N 2 = 0 \operatorname{tr}N^{2}=0  gives
 tr ⁡ A 2 = tr ⁡ B 2 \operatorname{tr}A^{2}=\operatorname{tr}B^{2}  and  tr ⁡ A ​ B = 0 \operatorname{tr}AB=0 . Thus 
 

 
 
 ℱ 2 ​ ( A , B ) = ( α + γ ) ​ tr ⁡ A 2 . \mathcal{F}_{2}(A,B)=(\alpha+\gamma)\operatorname{tr}A^{2}. 
 
 
 The nilpotent inequality holds exactly when  α + γ ≤ 0 \alpha+\gamma\leq 0 .
This is also exactly  Δ ​ F ≤ 0 \Delta F\leq 0 , and  U = F U=F  is a superharmonic majorant.
This also identifies the harmonic angular mode of order two. 
 
 
 
 Remark 3.6 . 
 
 Angular smoothing of an arbitrary majorant preserves superharmonicity
but need not preserve exact domination of the original obstacle.
Adding  ε ​ | z | p \varepsilon|z|^{p}  can restore domination but may destroy
superharmonicity. The contact-set regularity argument produces an
admissible majorant at the optimal constant and preserves both
conditions. 
 
 
 
 Corollary 3.7 . 
 
 Let  1 < p < ∞ 1<p<\infty . For fixed real directions  ( a j , b j ) j = 1 m (a_{j},b_{j})_{j=1}^{m} , the closed cones
generated by 
 

 
 
 ( tr ⁡ | a j ​ Re ⁡ N + b j ​ Im ⁡ N | p ) j = 1 m and ( ∫ 0 2 ​ π | a j ​ Re ⁡ q ⁡ ( e i ​ t ) + b j ​ Im ⁡ q ⁡ ( e i ​ t ) | p ​ d ​ t 2 ​ π ) j = 1 m \bigl(\operatorname{tr}|a_{j}\operatorname{Re}N+b_{j}\operatorname{Im}N|^{p}\bigr)_{j=1}^{m}\quad\text{and}\quad\left(\int_{0}^{2\pi}|a_{j}\operatorname{Re}q(e^{it})+b_{j}\operatorname{Im}q(e^{it})|^{p}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}t}{2\pi}\right)_{j=1}^{m} 
 
 
 coincide, where  N N  ranges over finite nilpotents and  q ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 q(0)=0 .
Let  P P  be a finite signed real ridge sum and let  Q ≢ 0 Q\not\equiv 0 
be a finite real ridge sum with nonnegative coefficients, with trace
functionals  𝒫 p , 𝒬 p \mathcal{P}_{p},\mathcal{Q}_{p} . Then 
 

 
 (3.10) 
 
 γ ∗ \displaystyle\gamma_{*} 
 : = sup N ≠ 0 ​ nilpotent 𝒫 p ​ ( Re ⁡ N , Im ⁡ N ) 𝒬 p ​ ( Re ⁡ N , Im ⁡ N ) \displaystyle:=\sup_{N\neq 0\ {\rm nilpotent}}\frac{\mathcal{P}_{p}(\operatorname{Re}N,\operatorname{Im}N)}{\mathcal{Q}_{p}(\operatorname{Re}N,\operatorname{Im}N)} 
 
 
 (3.11) 
 
 
 = sup q ≠ 0 ​ polynomial q ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 ∫ P ⁡ ( q ⁡ ( e i ​ t ) ) ​ 𝑑 t ∫ Q ⁡ ( q ⁡ ( e i ​ t ) ) ​ 𝑑 t ∈ ℝ . \displaystyle=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\neq 0\ {\rm polynomial}\\
q(0)=0\end{subarray}}\frac{\int P(q(e^{it}))\,\mathrm{d}t}{\int Q(q(e^{it}))\,\mathrm{d}t}\in\mathbb{R}. 
 
 
 The parameter  γ ∗ \gamma_{*}  is the least real  γ \gamma  for which
 P − γ ​ Q P-\gamma Q  has an admissible superharmonic majorant, and such a
majorant exists at  γ = γ ∗ \gamma=\gamma_{*} . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The two closed cones have the same polar by
Theorem  1.3 , so finite-dimensional separation
identifies them. Choose a term  η ​ | a ​ x + b ​ y | p \eta|ax+by|^{p}  of  Q Q , with
 η > 0 \eta>0  and  a 2 + b 2 > 0 a^{2}+b^{2}>0 . Rotate a nilpotent by  a − i ​ b a-ib  and apply
Corollary  2.7  to obtain, for every real  u , v u,v , 
 

 
 
 ‖ u ​ A + v ​ B ‖ p ≤ | u ​ a + v ​ b | + C p ​ | v ​ a − u ​ b | a 2 + b 2 ​ ‖ a ​ A + b ​ B ‖ p . \|uA+vB\|_{p}\leq\frac{|ua+vb|+C_{p}|va-ub|}{a^{2}+b^{2}}\,\|aA+bB\|_{p}. 
 
 
 Thus  | 𝒫 p ​ ( A , B ) | ≤ C ​ 𝒬 p ​ ( A , B ) |\mathcal{P}_{p}(A,B)|\leq C\mathcal{Q}_{p}(A,B)  for a finite
coefficient-dependent  C C . For this direction,  a ​ A + b ​ B = 0 aA+bB=0  would make
the rotated nilpotent skew-Hermitian, hence zero. A vanishing scalar
boundary part similarly forces  q = 0 q=0  by harmonic uniqueness.
Thus both denominators are positive. Finally, applying the classification to
 P − γ ​ Q P-\gamma Q  for each real  γ \gamma  identifies the two suprema and
supplies a majorant at their common finite value.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 4.  Proof of the unified realization theorem 

 
 The classification supplies the superharmonic majorants used below.
We first compare the optimal constant in the analytic-disc inequality
with a scalar operator norm on  L p ​ ( ℝ ) L^{p}(\mathbb{R}) , then use finite-dimensional
realization and sampling to prove the norm identities. 
 
 
 4.1.  Scalar localization and optimal constants 

 
 Lemma 4.1 . 
 
 For the real pairs and weights in Theorem  1.4 , 
 

 
 (4.1) 
 
 Λ p ​ ( P ) ≤ ‖ 𝐇 ‖ scalar ≤ ( ∑ j w j ​ K p ​ ( a j , b j ) p ) 1 / p < ∞ . \Lambda_{p}(P)\leq\|\mathbf{H}\|_{\rm scalar}\leq\left(\sum_{j}w_{j}K_{p}(a_{j},b_{j})^{p}\right)^{1/p}<\infty. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The scalar norm is finite by the individual real-pencil bounds
 [ 9 ,  10 ] . Let  q ≠ 0 q\neq 0 ,  q ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 q(0)=0 , and put
 f ⁡ ( t ) = Re ⁡ q ⁡ ( e i ​ t ) f(t)=\operatorname{Re}q(e^{it}) ,  g ⁡ ( t ) = Im ⁡ q ⁡ ( e i ​ t ) g(t)=\operatorname{Im}q(e^{it}) .
They are real trigonometric polynomials with zero mean and
 H 𝕋 ​ f = g H_{\mathbb{T}}f=g , using multiplier  − i ​ sgn ⁡ ( k ) -i\operatorname{sgn}(k) .
Choose a nonzero real Schwartz function  χ \chi  with Fourier
transform supported in  ( − 1 / 2 , 1 / 2 ) (-1/2,1/2) , with convention
 h ^ ​ ( ξ ) = ∫ h ⁡ ( t ) ​ e − i ​ t ​ ξ ​ 𝑑 t \widehat{h}(\xi)=\int h(t)e^{-it\xi}\,\mathrm{d}t .
For  L ≥ 1 L\geq 1 , the positive- and negative-frequency components of
 f L ​ ( t ) = χ ⁡ ( t / L ) ​ f ​ ( t ) f_{L}(t)=\chi(t/L)f(t)  have Fourier supports in their respective
half-lines. Consequently, 
 

 
 
 H ​ f L ​ ( t ) = χ ⁡ ( t / L ) ​ g ​ ( t ) . Hf_{L}(t)=\chi(t/L)g(t). 
 
 
 For continuous  2 ​ π 2\pi -periodic  h h , 
 

 
 
 lim L → ∞ 1 L ​ ∫ ℝ | χ ⁡ ( t / L ) | p ​ h ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t = ( ∫ ℝ | χ ⁡ ( s ) | p ​ 𝑑 s ) ​ ∫ 0 2 ​ π h ⁡ ( t ) ​ d ​ t 2 ​ π . \lim_{L\to\infty}\frac{1}{L}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\chi(t/L)|^{p}h(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\chi(s)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)\int_{0}^{2\pi}h(t)\frac{\,\mathrm{d}t}{2\pi}. 
 
 
 Approximate  h h  uniformly by trigonometric polynomials and apply
the Riemann–Lebesgue lemma to nonzero frequencies. Applying the
limit to the input and the weighted output  p p th powers shows
that each disc quotient is at most  ‖ 𝐇 ‖ scalar p \|\mathbf{H}\|_{\rm scalar}^{p} .
The denominator is positive, since  Re ⁡ q ≡ 0 \operatorname{Re}q\equiv 0  and  q ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 q(0)=0 
would imply  q = 0 q=0 . 
 
 
 Corollary  3.7 , with  Q = | x | p Q=|x|^{p} , consequently gives
 Λ p ​ ( P ) ≤ ‖ 𝐇 ‖ scalar \Lambda_{p}(P)\leq\|\mathbf{H}\|_{\rm scalar} .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 4.2.  Simultaneous finite realization 

 
 The half-shift diagonalization of the scalar sign matrix is already
given in  [ 13 , Lemma 1] : it is the case  α = 1 / 2 \alpha=1/2 ,
 V 0 = 1 V_{0}=1 ,  V m = 0 V_{m}=0  for  m ≠ 0 m\neq 0  of the model below, up to normalization.
We allow arbitrary Hermitian block coefficients and realize all
shifts using the same nilpotent matrix, as required for the joint
operator-norm identity.
For Hermitian  V 0 , … , V n − 1 ∈ M d V_{0},\ldots,V_{n-1}\in M_{d} , put
 S = ∑ m V m S=\sum_{m}V_{m} ,  V ¯ = S / n \bar{V}=S/n ,  θ j = 2 ​ π ​ j / n \theta_{j}=2\pi j/n , and
 V ^ ​ ( t ) = ∑ m = 0 n − 1 V m ​ e − i ​ m ​ t \widehat{V}(t)=\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}V_{m}e^{-imt} .
The indices  j , k , ℓ j,k,\ell  in the finite matrices below range over
 0 , … , n − 1 0,\ldots,n-1 . Define the nilpotent block matrix 
 

 
 (4.2) 
 
 N j ​ k = { 2 ​ V ^ ​ ( θ j − θ k ) / n , j < k , 0 , j ≥ k . N_{jk}=\begin{cases}2\widehat{V}(\theta_{j}-\theta_{k})/n,&j<k,\\
0,&j\geq k.\end{cases} 
 
 
 
 
 Proposition 4.2 . 
 
 Let  A = Re ⁡ N A=\operatorname{Re}N ,  B = Im ⁡ N B=\operatorname{Im}N ,  θ = π ​ α \theta=\pi\alpha ,  0 < α < 1 0<\alpha<1 , and 
 

 
 
 F j ​ ℓ = n − 1 / 2 e − i ​ ℓ ​ θ j , ( G α ) j ​ ℓ = n − 1 / 2 e − i ⁡ ( ℓ + α ) ​ θ j . F_{j\ell}=n^{-1/2}e^{-i\ell\theta_{j}},\qquad(G_{\alpha})_{j\ell}=n^{-1/2}e^{-i(\ell+\alpha)\theta_{j}}. 
 
 
 With tensor products by  I d I_{d}  understood, 
 

 
 (4.3) 
 
 F ∗ ​ A ​ F \displaystyle F^{*}AF 
 = diag ℓ ⁡ ( V ℓ − V ¯ ) , \displaystyle=\operatorname{diag}_{\ell}(V_{\ell}-\bar{V}),

 Proof. 
 
 Hermitian coefficients give  V ^ ​ ( − t ) ∗ = V ^ ​ ( t ) \widehat{V}(-t)^{*}=\widehat{V}(t) .

 For  t = j − k > 0 t=j-k>0  the second pencil has coefficient
 c t = e − i ​ θ ​ V ^ ​ ( 2 ​ π ​ t / n ) / n c_{t}=e^{-i\theta}\widehat{V}(2\pi t/n)/n ; for  t < 0 t<0  the factor is
