 Expanding  V ^ \widehat{V}  proves ( 4.4 ), with only scalar
Fourier factors passing the matrix coefficients.
∎ 
 
 
 
 At  α = 1 / 2 \alpha=1/2  this is the half-integer diagonalization of  − B -B . 
 
 
 The same elementary norm identity removes both the mean correction here
and the block diagonal in the Schur realization below: 
 

 
 (4.5) 
 
 ‖ ( Z / n ) ℓ = 1 n ‖ ℓ p ​ ( 𝒮 p ) = ‖ Z ⊗ I n / n ‖ p = n 1 / p − 1 ​ ‖ Z ‖ p ⟶ 0 . \|(Z/n)_{\ell=1}^{n}\|_{\ell^{p}(\mathcal{S}_{p})}=\|Z\otimes I_{n}/n\|_{p}=n^{1/p-1}\|Z\|_{p}\longrightarrow 0. 
 
 
 The equality follows from the repeated singular values and the limit
uses  p > 1 p>1 . 
 
 
 Lemma 4.3 . 
 
 For  a j = cos ⁡ ( π ​ α j ) a_{j}=\cos(\pi\alpha_{j}) ,  b j = sin ⁡ ( π ​ α j ) b_{j}=\sin(\pi\alpha_{j}) ,
 0 < α j < 1 0<\alpha_{j}<1 , one has 
 

 
 
 ‖ 𝐑 ‖ ℓ p ​ ( ℤ , 𝒮 p ​ ( 𝒦 ) ) → ℓ p m ​ ( ℓ p ​ ( ℤ , 𝒮 p ​ ( 𝒦 ) ) ) ≤ Λ p ​ ( P ) . \|\mathbf{R}\|_{\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z};\mathcal{S}_{p}(\mathcal{K}))\to\ell_{p}^{m}(\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z};\mathcal{S}_{p}(\mathcal{K})))}\leq\Lambda_{p}(P). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Write  C = Λ p ​ ( P ) < ∞ C=\Lambda_{p}(P)<\infty , by Lemma  4.1 . Taking  N ∗ N^{*}  simultaneously changes every
 b j b_{j}  to  − b j -b_{j}  without changing  C C .
For a Hermitian sequence  V 0 , … , V n − 1 V_{0},\ldots,V_{n-1} , put  S = ∑ m V m S=\sum_{m}V_{m} ,
 V ¯ = S / n \bar{V}=S/n , and define 
 

 
 
 ( ℛ n , α ​ V ) ℓ = sin ⁡ ( π ​ α ) n ​ ∑ m = 0 n − 1 cot ⁡ π ⁡ ( ℓ + α − m ) n ​ V m . (\mathscr{R}_{n,\alpha}V)_{\ell}=\frac{\sin(\pi\alpha)}{n}\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}\cot\frac{\pi(\ell+\alpha-m)}{n}\,V_{m}. 
 
 
 Proposition  4.2  uses the same  N N  and  A A  for
every  α j \alpha_{j} . Summing the  p p th powers after the separate output
unitary conjugations therefore yields 
 

 
 
 ( ∑ j w j ​ ‖ ℛ n , α j ​ V − a j ​ ( V ¯ ) ℓ ‖ p p ) 1 / p ≤ C ​ ‖ V − ( V ¯ ) ℓ ‖ p . \left(\sum_{j}w_{j}\|\mathscr{R}_{n,\alpha_{j}}V-a_{j}(\bar{V})_{\ell}\|_{p}^{p}\right)^{1/p}\leq C\|V-(\bar{V})_{\ell}\|_{p}. 
 
 
 The norm here includes the finite sequence variable. By
( 4.5 ) and the triangle inequality, 
 

 
 (4.6) 
 
 ( ∑ j w j ​ ‖ ℛ n , α j ​ V ‖ p p ) 1 / p ≤ C ​ ‖ V ‖ p + ( C + ( ∑ j w j ​ | a j | p ) 1 / p ) ​ n 1 / p − 1 ​ ‖ S ‖ p . \left(\sum_{j}w_{j}\|\mathscr{R}_{n,\alpha_{j}}V\|_{p}^{p}\right)^{1/p}\leq C\|V\|_{p}+\left(C+\Bigl(\sum_{j}w_{j}|a_{j}|^{p}\Bigr)^{1/p}\right)n^{1/p-1}\|S\|_{p}. 
 
 
 For sufficiently large  n n , regard a fixed finitely supported sequence
on  ℤ \mathbb{Z}  as a sequence on  ℤ / n ​ ℤ \mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}  by assigning zero to unused
residue classes. Its norm and sum are independent of  n n .
On each fixed finite set of output indices, the factors
 n − 1 ​ cot ⁡ ( π ⁡ ( ℓ + α j − m ) / n ) n^{-1}\cot(\pi(\ell+\alpha_{j}-m)/n)  converge to
 [ π ⁡ ( ℓ + α j − m ) ] − 1 [\pi(\ell+\alpha_{j}-m)]^{-1} , uniformly over the input support
and the finitely many  j j . Restrict ( 4.6 )
to that window and let  n → ∞ n\to\infty ; the error vanishes because
 p > 1 p>1 . Increasing the output window gives the asserted estimate by
monotone convergence. 
 
 
 For arbitrary matrix coefficients use the Hermitian dilation
 V ↦ ( 0 V V ∗ 0 ) V\mapsto\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&V\\
V^{*}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right) .
All kernels are real, and the input and every output  p p th power
norm acquire the same factor two. Density of finitely supported
finite-rank sequences and common finite-dimensional compressions
give the bounded extension for every  𝒦 \mathcal{K} .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 4.3.  Sampling 

 
 Lemma 4.4 . 
 
 For  f ∈ C c ∞ ​ ( ℝ , M d ) f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};M_{d})  and  v j h = h 1 / p ​ f ​ ( h ​ j ) v^{h}_{j}=h^{1/p}f(hj) , 
 

 
 (4.7) 
 
 h − 1 / p ( R α v h ) ℓ ⟶ cos ( π α ) f ( x ) + sin ( π α ) H f ( x ) , x = h ℓ , h^{-1/p}(R_{\alpha}v^{h})_{\ell}\longrightarrow\cos(\pi\alpha)f(x)+\sin(\pi\alpha)Hf(x),\qquad x=h\ell, 
 
 
 uniformly on compact sets of such  x x .
For the shifted pairs of Lemma  4.3 , 
 

 
 (4.8) 
 
 ‖ 𝐇 ‖ L p ​ ( ℝ , 𝒮 p ​ ( 𝒦 ) ) → ℓ p m ​ ( L p ​ ( ℝ , 𝒮 p ​ ( 𝒦 ) ) ) ≤ ‖ 𝐑 ‖ . \|\mathbf{H}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{S}_{p}(\mathcal{K}))\to\ell_{p}^{m}(L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{S}_{p}(\mathcal{K})))}\leq\|\mathbf{R}\|. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The left side of ( 4.7 ) is
 sin ⁡ ( π ​ α ) ​ π − 1 ​ ∑ k f ⁡ ( x − h ​ k ) / ( k + α ) \sin(\pi\alpha)\pi^{-1}\sum_{k}f(x-hk)/(k+\alpha) .
Fix  δ > 0 \delta>0  and  M = ⌊ δ / h ⌋ M=\lfloor\delta/h\rfloor .
The constant term on  | k | ≤ M |k|\leq M  tends to
 π ​ cot ⁡ ( π ​ α ) ​ f ​ ( x ) \pi\cot(\pi\alpha)f(x)  before the prefactor is applied, by the
paired partial fractions of  π ​ cot ⁡ ( π ​ z ) \pi\cot(\pi z) .
The remaining inner sum has norm at most
 C ​ h ​ ∑ 1 ≤ | k | ≤ M | k | / | k + α | ≤ C α ​ δ Ch\sum_{1\leq|k|\leq M}|k|/|k+\alpha|\leq C_{\alpha}\delta .
For the outer sum write 
 

 
 
 f ⁡ ( x − h ​ k ) k + α = h ​ f ⁡ ( x − h ​ k ) h ​ k + α ​ h . \frac{f(x-hk)}{k+\alpha}=h\,\frac{f(x-hk)}{hk+\alpha h}. 
 
 
 Fix also  R > 0 R>0 . Because  f f  has compact support, for  | x | ≤ R |x|\leq R  all
nonzero summands lie in one fixed compact  t t -interval. On
 | t | ≥ δ / 2 |t|\geq\delta/2  the functions
 ( x , t ) ↦ f ⁡ ( x − t ) / ( t + α ​ h ) (x,t)\mapsto f(x-t)/(t+\alpha h)  converge uniformly, together with a
uniform Lipschitz bound in  t t , to  f ⁡ ( x − t ) / t f(x-t)/t . The uniform
Riemann-sum estimate therefore gives 
 

 
 (4.9) 
 
 sup | x | ≤ R ‖ h ​ ∑ | h ​ k | > δ f ⁡ ( x − h ​ k ) h ​ k + α ​ h − ∫ | t | > δ f ⁡ ( x − t ) t ​ 𝑑 t ‖ ⟶ 0 . \sup_{|x|\leq R}\left\|h\!\sum_{|hk|>\delta}\frac{f(x-hk)}{hk+\alpha h}-\int_{|t|>\delta}\frac{f(x-t)}{t}\,\,\mathrm{d}t\right\|\longrightarrow 0. 
 
 
 Since  M = ⌊ δ / h ⌋ M=\lfloor\delta/h\rfloor , the condition  | k | > M |k|>M  is exactly
equivalent to  | h ​ k | > δ |hk|>\delta  for integer  k k , so ( 4.9 )
is the limit for the actual outer sum. In the missing symmetric
interval, 
 

 
 
 ‖ ∫ | t | < δ f ⁡ ( x − t ) − f ⁡ ( x ) t ​ 𝑑 t ‖ ≤ C R ​ δ \left\|\int_{|t|<\delta}\frac{f(x-t)-f(x)}{t}\,\,\mathrm{d}t\right\|\leq C_{R}\delta 
 
 
 by the mean-value theorem, uniformly for  | x | ≤ R |x|\leq R ; the constant part
has zero symmetric principal value. Together with the inner-sum bound
above, this proves the asserted uniform convergence by first letting
 h ↓ 0 h\downarrow 0  with  δ \delta  fixed and then letting
 δ ↓ 0 \delta\downarrow 0 . 
 
 
 For the joint norm estimate, set  v k h = h 1 / p ​ f ​ ( h ​ k ) v_{k}^{h}=h^{1/p}f(hk) .
Then  ∑ k ‖ v k h ‖ p p → ‖ f ‖ L p ​ ( 𝒮 p ) p \sum_{k}\|v_{k}^{h}\|_{p}^{p}\to\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathcal{S}_{p})}^{p} .
The local limit gives uniform convergence on each compact
output interval for all finitely many shifts. Sum the weighted
output  p p th powers, pass to the Riemann integrals on that interval,
and then increase the interval to  ℝ \mathbb{R} . Monotone convergence gives
( 4.8 ). Smooth finite-rank functions
are dense, so the estimate extends to the stated spaces. 
 
 
 ∎ 
 
 
 
 
 4.4.  Schur realization 

 
 Lemma 4.5 . 
 
 For arbitrary real pairs and nonnegative weights,
 sup ℰ ‖ 𝐓 ℰ ‖ = Λ p ​ ( P ) \sup_{\mathcal{E}}\|\mathbf{T}_{\mathcal{E}}\|=\Lambda_{p}(P) .
The corresponding multiplier for the standard rank-one projections
on  ℓ 2 ​ ( ℕ ) \ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})  has the same norm. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 If  X = X ∗ X=X^{*}  and  D ℰ ​ X = 0 D_{\mathcal{E}}X=0 , then
 N = 2 ​ ∑ r < s E r ​ X ​ E s N=2\sum_{r<s}E_{r}XE_{s}  is nilpotent with
 Re ⁡ N = X \operatorname{Re}N=X  and  Im ⁡ N = H ℰ ​ X \operatorname{Im}N=H_{\mathcal{E}}X .
Equation ( 1.14 ) gives the joint estimate.
For arbitrary zero-block-diagonal  X X , Hermitian dilation and the
flag  E r ⊗ I 2 E_{r}\otimes I_{2}  give the same bound, because every real pencil
commutes with taking adjoints and all  p p th powers gain a factor two. 
 
 
 For general  X X , let  u = n − 1 / 2 ( 1 , … , 1 ) u=n^{-1/2}(1,\ldots,1) ,  P = u ​ u ∗ P=uu^{*} , and put 
 

 
 
 Y n = X ⊗ P − D ℰ ​ X ⊗ I n / n , J ​ ξ = ξ ⊗ u . Y_{n}=X\otimes P-D_{\mathcal{E}}X\otimes I_{n}/n,\qquad J\xi=\xi\otimes u. 
 
 
 The flag  ℰ n = ( E r ⊗ e s ​ s ) \mathcal{E}_{n}=(E_{r}\otimes e_{ss}) , in lexicographic order,
has  D ℰ n ​ Y n = 0 D_{\mathcal{E}_{n}}Y_{n}=0 . Direct block summation gives 
 

 
 (4.10) 
 
 J ∗ ​ Y n ​ J = X − D ℰ ​ X / n , J ∗ ​ H ℰ n ​ ( Y n ) ​ J = H ℰ ​ X . J^{*}Y_{n}J=X-D_{\mathcal{E}}X/n,\qquad J^{*}H_{\mathcal{E}_{n}}(Y_{n})J=H_{\mathcal{E}}X. 
 
 
 The within-block signs cancel since  ∑ r , s sgn ⁡ ( r − s ) = 0 \sum_{r,s}\operatorname{sgn}(r-s)=0 .
Compression is contractive for each output. Summing their  p p th
powers and using 
 

 
 
 ‖ Y n − X ⊗ P ‖ p = n 1 / p − 1 ​ ‖ D ℰ ​ X ‖ p ⟶ 0 \|Y_{n}-X\otimes P\|_{p}=n^{1/p-1}\|D_{\mathcal{E}}X\|_{p}\longrightarrow 0 
 
 
 proves the upper bound on all  X X .
Conversely, Schur triangularization realizes every nilpotent as a
strictly upper-triangular matrix for a rank-one flag and supplies
the lower bound. Finite principal sections and density give the
infinite ordered transform.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 4.5.  Canonical amplification and trace normalization 

 
 The Schatten amplification converts the tracial matrix estimates in both
parts of the paper to the usual completely bounded estimates. We record
this conversion once, keeping the canonical operator-space matrix norm
distinct from the tracial  L p L^{p}  norm. 
 
 
 Lemma 4.6 . 
 
 Let  1 < p < ∞ 1<p<\infty , and let  𝒬 , ℛ 1 , … , ℛ m \mathcal{Q},\mathcal{R}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{R}_{m}  be semifinite tracial von Neumann algebras. Equip their
 L p L^{p}  spaces and the finite  ℓ p \ell^{p}  direct sum with their canonical
operator-space structures. For a bounded linear map 
 

 
 
 T : L p ​ ( 𝒬 ) ⟶ ⨁ j = 1 m L p p ​ ( ℛ j ) T:L^{p}(\mathcal{Q})\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{j=1}^{m}{}_{\!p}L^{p}(\mathcal{R}_{j}) 
 
 
 one has 
 

 
 (4.11) 
 
 ‖ T ‖ cb = sup n ≥ 1 ‖ id M n ⊗ T ‖ tr , \|T\|_{\mathrm{cb}}=\sup_{n\geq 1}\|\operatorname{id}_{M_{n}}\otimes T\|_{\mathrm{tr}}, 
 
 
 where the norm on the right is between the corresponding tracial spaces 
 

 
 
 L p ​ ( M n ​ ⊗ ¯ ​ 𝒬 ) and ⨁ j = 1 m L p p ​ ( M n ​ ⊗ ¯ ​ ℛ j ) . L^{p}(M_{n}\,\overline{\otimes}\,\mathcal{Q})\quad\hbox{and}\quad\bigoplus_{j=1}^{m}{}_{\!p}L^{p}(M_{n}\,\overline{\otimes}\,\mathcal{R}_{j}). 
 
 
 The matrix trace in these spaces is the ordinary unnormalized trace.
Replacing it simultaneously in the domain and range by
 n − 1 ​ Tr n n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}_{n}  does not change any amplification norm. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The standard Schatten Fubini identification and matrix-norm recovery
formula are 
 

 
 (4.12) 
 
 𝒮 p n ​ [ L p ​ ( 𝒬 ) ] \displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{p}^{n}[L^{p}(\mathcal{Q})] 
 = L p ​ ( M n ​ ⊗ ¯ ​ 𝒬 ) , \displaystyle=L^{p}(M_{n}\,\overline{\otimes}\,\mathcal{Q}), 
 
 
 (4.13) 
 
 ‖ x ‖ M n ​ ( L p ​ ( 𝒬 ) ) \displaystyle\|x\|_{M_{n}(L^{p}(\mathcal{Q}))} 
 = sup a , b ∈ M n ‖ a ‖ 𝒮 2 ​ p n , ‖ b ‖ 𝒮 2 ​ p n ≤ 1 ‖ ( a ⊗ I ) ​ x ​ ( b ⊗ I ) ‖ L p ​ ( M n ​ ⊗ ¯ ​ 𝒬 ) . \displaystyle=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,b\in M_{n}\\
\|a\|_{\mathcal{S}_{2p}^{n}},\,\|b\|_{\mathcal{S}_{2p}^{n}}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\|(a\otimes I)x(b\otimes I)\|_{L^{p}(M_{n}\,\overline{\otimes}\,\mathcal{Q})}. 
 
 
 Here the Schatten norms are unnormalized. These identities, and the
equivalence of complete contraction with contraction at every Schatten
 p p -amplification, are recalled in
 [ 35 , Section 2, equations (2.8)–(2.9)] ; see also
 [ 19 , Corollary 1.4 and Lemma 1.7] .
The range is the  L p L^{p}  space of the finite direct-sum algebra
 ⨁ j ℛ j \bigoplus_{j}\mathcal{R}_{j} , with the direct-sum trace, so the same
identifications apply there. 
 
 
 For completeness, if all tracial amplification norms are at most  C C ,
entrywise amplification commutes with multiplication by the scalar
matrices  a , b a,b . Formula ( 4.13 ) therefore gives 
 

 
 
 ‖ ( id M n ⊗ T ) ​ x ‖ M n ​ ( F ) ≤ C ​ ‖ x ‖ M n ​ ( L p ​ ( 𝒬 ) ) , F = ⨁ j = 1 m L p p ​ ( ℛ j ) . \|(\operatorname{id}_{M_{n}}\otimes T)x\|_{M_{n}(F)}\leq C\|x\|_{M_{n}(L^{p}(\mathcal{Q}))},\qquad F=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{m}{}_{\!p}L^{p}(\mathcal{R}_{j}). 
 
 
 Thus  ‖ T ‖ cb ≤ C \|T\|_{\mathrm{cb}}\leq C . Conversely, the standard Schatten
amplification characterization bounds every
 id 𝒮 p n ⊗ T \operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{S}_{p}^{n}}\otimes T  by
 ‖ T ‖ cb \|T\|_{\mathrm{cb}} ; ( 4.12 ) identifies its norm
with the tracial amplification norm. This proves
( 4.11 ). Normalizing the matrix trace multiplies
both the domain and range norms by  n − 1 / p n^{-1/p} , proving the final assertion.
∎ 
 
 
 
 In particular, a uniform estimate for the amplified flags in the Hilbert
part and a uniform estimate for  M n ​ ( ℬ ) ⊂ M n ​ ( ℳ ) M_{n}(\mathcal{B})\subset M_{n}(\mathcal{M}) 
in the subdiagonal part give the corresponding completely bounded
estimates by the same lemma. The sharp tracial estimates themselves
are proved separately in the two parts. 
 
 
 In the present cases the
Fubini identifications for vector-valued noncommutative  L p L^{p} 
spaces are 
 

 
 (4.14) 
 
 𝒮 p d ​ [ L p ​ ( ℝ ) ] = L p ​ ( ℝ , 𝒮 p d ) , 𝒮 p d ​ [ 𝒮 p ​ ( ℓ 2 ) ] = 𝒮 p ​ ( ℂ d ⊗ ℓ 2 ) . \mathcal{S}_{p}^{d}[L^{p}(\mathbb{R})]=L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{S}_{p}^{d}),\qquad\mathcal{S}_{p}^{d}[\mathcal{S}_{p}(\ell^{2})]=\mathcal{S}_{p}(\mathbb{C}^{d}\otimes\ell^{2}). 
 
 
 The norms in the right-hand spaces use the unnormalized trace, consistently
with the rest of the paper. Formula ( 4.11 )
compares the corresponding amplification norms of a map. 
 
 
 Proof of Theorem  1.4 . 
 
 Lemma  4.1  gives finiteness, and
Corollary  3.7  gives the variational and superharmonic
majorant statements. For the shifted pairs, scalar embedding, sampling,
finite-window transference, and the same lemma give the following chain of inequalities: 
 

 
 (4.15) 
 
 ‖ 𝐇 ‖ scalar ≤ ‖ 𝐇 ‖ 𝒮 p ≤ ‖ 𝐑 ‖ 𝒮 p ≤ Λ p ​ ( P ) ≤ ‖ 𝐇 ‖ scalar . \|\mathbf{H}\|_{\rm scalar}\leq\|\mathbf{H}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{p}}\leq\|\mathbf{R}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{p}}\leq\Lambda_{p}(P)\leq\|\mathbf{H}\|_{\rm scalar}. 
 
 
 Applying sampling also with  𝒦 = ℂ \mathcal{K}=\mathbb{C}  and embedding scalar
sequences shows  ‖ 𝐑 ‖ scalar = Λ p ​ ( P ) \|\mathbf{R}\|_{\rm scalar}=\Lambda_{p}(P) .
Lemma  4.5  supplies the Schur model. 
 
 
 For general pairs with  b j ≠ 0 b_{j}\neq 0 , multiplying an output by  − 1 -1 
if necessary permits the representation
 ( a j , b j ) = ε j ​ r j ​ ( cos ⁡ ( π ​ α j ) , sin ⁡ ( π ​ α j ) ) (a_{j},b_{j})=\varepsilon_{j}r_{j}(\cos(\pi\alpha_{j}),\sin(\pi\alpha_{j})) , where  ε j ∈ { − 1 , 1 } \varepsilon_{j}\in\{-1,1\} ,
 r j > 0 r_{j}>0 , and  0 < α j < 1 0<\alpha_{j}<1 .
Absorb  r j p r_{j}^{p}  into  w j w_{j} . The outputs with  b j = 0 b_{j}=0  add the
same constant  ∑ b j = 0 w j ​ | a j | p \sum_{b_{j}=0}w_{j}|a_{j}|^{p}  to every norm ratio to the
 p p th power, including the nilpotent and disc ratios. This proves
all real pairs, including the case of zero weights.
Finally  tr ⁡ N 2 = 0 \operatorname{tr}N^{2}=0  gives  tr ⁡ A 2 = tr ⁡ B 2 \operatorname{tr}A^{2}=\operatorname{tr}B^{2}  and  tr ⁡ A ​ B = 0 \operatorname{tr}AB=0 ,
which proves the stated quadratic value. 
 
 
 For completely bounded norms, the same Fubini identifications hold for
 ℓ p ​ ( ℤ ) \ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z})  and finite  ℓ p \ell^{p}  direct sums.
Thus the  d d th Schatten amplification of  𝐇 \mathbf{H}  or  𝐑 \mathbf{R} 
is exactly its  𝒮 p d \mathcal{S}_{p}^{d} -valued version, whose norm is  Λ p ​ ( P ) \Lambda_{p}(P) .
The  d d th amplification of  𝐓 ℰ \mathbf{T}_{\mathcal{E}}  is the same
joint pencil for the block flag  ( I d ⊗ E j ) (I_{d}\otimes E_{j}) , up to interchange
of tensor factors. Hence its norm is at most  Λ p ​ ( P ) \Lambda_{p}(P) , uniformly
in  d d  and the flag. Taking the supremum in the amplification
characterization and using the operator-norm lower bounds proves
the completely bounded norm assertions of the theorem. 
 
 
 For the standard infinite Schur order, finite principal sections
and density at every amplification level give the same upper
bound; nilpotent tests on rank-one flags give the lower bound.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Proof of Corollary  1.5 . 
 
 For one output, the scalar theorem of  [ 9 ,  10 ]  gives
( 1.18 ). Theorem  1.4  and the preceding
infinite-order argument give every stated norm equality.
Scalar reflection, homogeneity, and duality give
( 1.19 )–( 1.20 ).
Only the signed diagonal convention remains, and it is proved next.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Corollary 4.7 . 
 
 Use the real pairs and weights of Theorem  1.4 . For real row
levels  λ 1 , … , λ r \lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{r}  and column levels  μ 1 , … , μ s \mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{s} ,
define the joint rectangular multiplier 
 

 
 
 ( 𝐌 λ , μ ​ X ) ℓ = ( w ℓ 1 / p ​ [ a ℓ + i ​ b ℓ ​ sgn ⁡ ( λ j − μ k ) ] ​ X j ​ k ) j , k . (\mathbf{M}_{\lambda,\mu}X)_{\ell}=\bigl(w_{\ell}^{1/p}[a_{\ell}+ib_{\ell}\operatorname{sgn}(\lambda_{j}-\mu_{k})]X_{jk}\bigr)_{j,k}. 
 
 
 On rectangular Schatten matrices with their canonical corner operator-space
structure, it is the restriction of a single joint flag multiplier on  ℂ r + s \mathbb{C}^{r+s} 
to a rectangular corner, under a complete isometry. Hence its
operator and completely bounded norms are at most  Λ p ​ ( P ) \Lambda_{p}(P) ,
and their suprema over all finite lists and sizes equal  Λ p ​ ( P ) \Lambda_{p}(P) .
For one output this gives the bound and uniform sharp constant  K p ​ ( a , b ) K_{p}(a,b) . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Embed  X X  in the off-diagonal corner from the  μ \mu -space to the
 λ \lambda -space of their direct sum, and order the combined levels,
grouping ties. This embedding preserves singular values at every
Schatten amplification and is completely isometric by
( 4.13 ). The resulting joint flag preserves the
corner and has exactly the displayed symbols.
Theorem  1.4  gives both upper bounds. Equal square lists
recover the standard rank-one joint orders; their uniform sharpness
in Lemma  4.5  gives both lower bounds.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 4.6.  Diagonal conventions for the signed Schur multiplier 

 
 For  n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 , define Schur multipliers on  𝒮 p n \mathcal{S}_{p}^{n}  by 
 

 
 
 ( S 0 ( n ) ​ X ) j ​ k = sgn ⁡ ( j − k ) ​ X j ​ k , S + ( n ) = S 0 ( n ) + D n , S − ( n ) = S 0 ( n ) − D n , (S_{0}^{(n)}X)_{jk}=\operatorname{sgn}(j-k)X_{jk},\qquad S_{+}^{(n)}=S_{0}^{(n)}+D_{n},\qquad S_{-}^{(n)}=S_{0}^{(n)}-D_{n}, 
 
 
 where  D n D_{n}  is the diagonal conditional expectation. Thus  S + ( n ) S_{+}^{(n)}  has symbol
 + 1 +1  on  j ≥ k j\geq k  and  − 1 -1  on  j < k j<k , while  S − ( n ) S_{-}^{(n)}  has diagonal
value  − 1 -1 . Notice that  ℋ 0 = i ​ S 0 \mathscr{H}_{0}=iS_{0}  on finite principal sections. 
 
 
 Lemma 4.8 . 
 
 Fix  1 < p < ∞ 1<p<\infty . For every  d ≥ 1 d\geq 1 , put 
 

 
 
 ‖ S ‖ p , d = ‖ S ⊗ id 𝒮 p d ‖ 𝒮 p ​ ( ℂ n ⊗ ℂ d ) → 𝒮 p ​ ( ℂ n ⊗ ℂ d ) \|S\|_{p,d}=\|S\otimes\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{S}_{p}^{d}}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{p}(\mathbb{C}^{n}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{d})\to\mathcal{S}_{p}(\mathbb{C}^{n}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{d})} 
 
 
 when  S S  acts on  n × n n\times n  matrices. Then 
 

 
 (4.16) 
 
 ‖ S 0 ( n ) ‖ p , d ≤ ‖ S + ( n ) ‖ p , d = ‖ S − ( n ) ‖ p , d ≤ ‖ S 0 ( 2 ​ n ) ‖ p , d . \|S_{0}^{(n)}\|_{p,d}\leq\|S_{+}^{(n)}\|_{p,d}=\|S_{-}^{(n)}\|_{p,d}\leq\|S_{0}^{(2n)}\|_{p,d}. 
 
 
 Consequently the dimension suprema agree for all three conventions,
both for operator norms and completely bounded norms. The infinite
multipliers  S 0 , S + , S − S_{0},S_{+},S_{-}  extend to  𝒮 p ​ ( ℓ 2 ) \mathcal{S}_{p}(\ell^{2})  and satisfy 
 

 
 (4.17) 
 
 ‖ S ε ‖ 𝒮 p → 𝒮 p = ‖ S ε ‖ cb , 𝒮 p = C p , ε ∈ { 0 , + , − } . \|S_{\varepsilon}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{p}\to\mathcal{S}_{p}}=\|S_{\varepsilon}\|_{\mathrm{cb},\mathcal{S}_{p}}=C_{p},\qquad\varepsilon\in\{0,+,-\}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Take  λ j = 2 ​ j \lambda_{j}=2j  and  μ k = 2 ​ k − 1 \mu_{k}=2k-1  in
Corollary  4.7 . Since
 sgn ⁡ ( λ j − μ k ) \operatorname{sgn}(\lambda_{j}-\mu_{k})  is  + 1 +1  for  j ≥ k j\geq k  and  − 1 -1  otherwise,
 i ​ S + ( n ) iS_{+}^{(n)}  is a completely isometric corner restriction of
 i ​ S 0 ( 2 ​ n ) iS_{0}^{(2n)} . This proves the last inequality in
( 4.16 ) at every amplification level;
it is the row–column interleaving of  [ 18 , Proposition 6.1] . 
 
 
 Let  R n ​ e j = e n + 1 − j R_{n}e_{j}=e_{n+1-j}  and let  ℛ n \mathcal{R}_{n}  be conjugation by  R n R_{n} .
Direct comparison of symbols gives 
 

 
 
 S − ( n ) = − ℛ n ​ S + ( n ) ​ ℛ n , S 0 ( n ) = 1 2 ​ ( S + ( n ) + S − ( n ) ) . S_{-}^{(n)}=-\mathcal{R}_{n}S_{+}^{(n)}\mathcal{R}_{n},\qquad S_{0}^{(n)}=\tfrac{1}{2}(S_{+}^{(n)}+S_{-}^{(n)}). 
 
 
 Conjugation by  R n ⊗ I d R_{n}\otimes I_{d}  is isometric, so the first identity
identifies the two norms and the second gives the remaining inequality
in ( 4.16 ). Taking the supremum over  n n 
proves equality of the dimension suprema at each  d d ; then taking the
supremum over  d d  and using ( 4.11 ) proves the
completely bounded norm assertion. Finite principal sections and density give
the infinite extensions. Since  ℋ 0 = i ​ S 0 \mathscr{H}_{0}=iS_{0} , the already proved
zero-diagonal part of Corollary  1.5  gives the common
value  C p C_{p} , proving ( 4.17 ) and completing that
corollary.
∎ 
 
 
 
 The norm equalities concern suprema over matrix dimensions.
The norms need not agree in a fixed dimension:  S 0 ( 1 ) = 0 S_{0}^{(1)}=0 
whereas  S + ( 1 ) = I S_{+}^{(1)}=I . Order reversal and multiplication by  i i  preserve
the norms, reconciling the conventions in  [ 18 , Theorem 6.2] . 
 
 
 
 
 5.  Accretive spectral comparison in finite algebras 

 
 Throughout this section and the Riesz arguments below,  ( ℳ , τ ) (\mathcal{M},\tau)  and  ℬ \mathcal{B}  are as in Section  1 . The letter  H = G + i ​ K H=G+iK  denotes a bounded accretive element; it does not denote the Hilbert transform in this section. The symbol  Det τ \operatorname{Det}_{\tau}  denotes the Fuglede–Kadison determinant, whereas  Δ \Delta  continues to denote the planar Laplacian in the matrix spectral comparisons. 
 
 
 For a bounded  A ∈ ℳ A\in\mathcal{M} , its decreasing singular-value function is 
 

 
 
 μ v ​ ( A ) = inf { c ≥ 0 : τ ​ 1 ( c , ∞ ) ​ ( | A | ) ≤ v } , 0 < v < 1 . \mu_{v}(A)=\inf\{c\geq 0:\tau 1_{(c,\infty)}(|A|)\leq v\},\qquad 0<v<1. 
 
 
 Spectral layer-cake integration gives  τ ​ f ​ ( | A | ) = ∫ 0 1 f ⁡ ( μ v ​ ( A ) ) ​ 𝑑 v \tau f(|A|)=\int_{0}^{1}f(\mu_{v}(A))\,dv  for continuous  f f . The equivalent projection formula is 
 

 
 
 μ v ( A ) = inf { ∥ A e ∥ : e = e ∗ = e 2 ∈ ℳ , τ ( I − e ) ≤ v } . \mu_{v}(A)=\inf\{\|Ae\|:e=e^{*}=e^{2}\in\mathcal{M},\ \tau(I-e)\leq v\}. 
 
 
 To see the equivalence, use the spectral projection below any level larger than the distribution inverse for one direction. For the other, if  ‖ A ​ e ‖ ≤ c \|Ae\|\leq c , the spectral projection  f = 1 ( c , ∞ ) ​ ( | A | ) f=1_{(c,\infty)}(|A|)  has  f ∧ e = 0 f\wedge e=0 ; the polar decomposition of  ( I − e ) ​ f (I-e)f  gives  τ ​ f ≤ τ ⁡ ( I − e ) \tau f\leq\tau(I-e) . In particular, 
 

 
 (5.1) 
 
 | μ v ​ ( A ) − μ v ​ ( B ) | ≤ ‖ A − B ‖ . |\mu_{v}(A)-\mu_{v}(B)|\leq\|A-B\|. 
 
 
 
 
 Theorem 5.1 . 
 
 Let  H = G + i ​ K ∈ ℳ H=G+iK\in\mathcal{M} , where  G ≥ 0 G\geq 0  and  K = K ∗ K=K^{*} , and put 
 

 
 
 g ⁡ ( v ) = μ v ​ ( G ) , x ⁡ ( v ) = μ v ​ ( H ∗ ​ H ) , z ⁡ ( v ) = g ⁡ ( v ) + i ​ x ⁡ ( v ) − g ​ ( v ) 2 . g(v)=\mu_{v}(G),\qquad x(v)=\mu_{v}(H^{*}H),\qquad z(v)=g(v)+i\sqrt{x(v)-g(v)^{2}}. 
 
 
 The square root is well defined. Let  w : ( 0 , ∞ ) → [ 0 , ∞ ) w:(0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)  be
measurable with 
 

 
 (5.2) 
 
 ∫ 0 1 w ⁡ ( u ) u ​ 𝑑 u + ∫ 1 ∞ w ⁡ ( u ) u 2 ​ 𝑑 u < ∞ . \int_{0}^{1}\frac{w(u)}{u}\,du+\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{w(u)}{u^{2}}\,du<\infty. 
 
 
 For real  a , b a,b , define, on the closed right half-plane, 
 

 
 (5.3) 
 
 φ ⁡ ( z ) = a + b ​ z + ∫ 0 ∞ w ⁡ ( u ) ​ [ z / u − log ⁡ ( 1 + z / u ) ] ​ 𝑑 u . \varphi(z)=a+bz+\int_{0}^{\infty}w(u)[z/u-\log(1+z/u)]\,du. 
 
 
 Define  φ ⁡ ( H ) \varphi(H)  by the corresponding norm-convergent operator integral,
with constant term  a ​ I aI . Then 
 

 
 (5.4) 
 
 ∫ 0 1 Re ⁡ φ ⁡ ( z ⁡ ( v ) ) ​ 𝑑 v − Re ⁡ τ ​ φ ​ ( H ) = 1 2 ​ ∫ 0 ∞ w ⁡ ( u ) ​ ℒ u ​ ( G , H ∗ ​ H ) ​ 𝑑 u ≥ 0 , \int_{0}^{1}\operatorname{Re}\varphi(z(v))\,dv-\operatorname{Re}\tau\varphi(H)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}w(u)\mathcal{L}_{u}(G,H^{*}H)\,du\geq 0, 
 
 
 where 
 

 
 (5.5) 
 
 ℒ u ​ ( G , X ) = τ ​ log ⁡ ( I + 2 ​ G / u + X / u 2 ) − ∫ 0 1 log ⁡ ( 1 + 2 ​ g ​ ( v ) / u + x ⁡ ( v ) / u 2 ) ​ 𝑑 v ≥ 0 . \mathcal{L}_{u}(G,X)=\tau\log(I+2G/u+X/u^{2})-\int_{0}^{1}\log(1+2g(v)/u+x(v)/u^{2})\,dv\geq 0. 
 
 
 All assertions, including the exact defect identity, hold for
 G ≥ 0 G\geq 0  without invertibility. 
 
 
 
 Corollary 5.2 . 
 
 For  H , G , g , x , z H,G,g,x,z  as in Theorem  5.1  and  1 < s < 2 1<s<2 ,
put  η s = s ​ sin ⁡ ( π ⁡ ( s − 1 ) ) / π \eta_{s}=s\sin(\pi(s-1))/\pi .
The principal accretive powers, including boundary values, are
defined in Subsection  5.3 . Then 
 

 
 (5.6) 
 
 Re ⁡ τ ​ H s ≤ ∫ 0 1 Re ⁡ z ​ ( v ) s ​ 𝑑 v , \operatorname{Re}\tau H^{s}\leq\int_{0}^{1}\operatorname{Re}z(v)^{s}\,dv, 
 
 
 and for every  G ≥ 0 G\geq 0  the exact defect is 
 

 
 (5.7) 
 
 ∫ 0 1 Re ⁡ z ​ ( v ) s ​ 𝑑 v − Re ⁡ τ ​ H s = η s 2 ​ ∫ 0 ∞ u s − 1 ​ ℒ u ​ ( G , H ∗ ​ H ) ​ 𝑑 u ≥ 0 . \int_{0}^{1}\operatorname{Re}z(v)^{s}\,dv-\operatorname{Re}\tau H^{s}=\frac{\eta_{s}}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}u^{s-1}\mathcal{L}_{u}(G,H^{*}H)\,du\geq 0. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Corollary 5.3 . 
 
 For  G ≥ 0 G\geq 0 ,  K = K ∗ K=K^{*}  in  ℳ \mathcal{M} , and  1 < s < 2 1<s<2 , set 
 

 
 
 H = G + i ​ K , α = π 2 ​ s , r = sec ⁡ α , a = tan ⁡ α . H=G+iK,\qquad\alpha=\frac{\pi}{2s},\qquad r=\sec\alpha,\qquad a=\tan\alpha. 
 
 
 Principal accretive powers, including boundary values, are defined in Subsection  5.3 . Then 
 

 
 (5.8) 
 
 𝒟 s ​ ( G , K ) = r s ​ τ ​ G s − τ ​ | H | s − a ​ Re ⁡ τ ​ H s ≥ 0 . \quad\mathcal{D}_{s}(G,K)=r^{s}\tau G^{s}-\tau|H|^{s}-a\operatorname{Re}\tau H^{s}\geq 0.\quad 
 
 
 Equality holds for  H = ( 1 ± i ​ a ) ​ G H=(1\pm ia)G  with  G ≥ 0 G\geq 0 . 
 
 
 
 5.1.  Singular-value dominance and logarithmic rearrangement 

 
 Lemma 5.4 . 
 
 If  H = G + i ​ K H=G+iK  with  G ≥ 0 G\geq 0 , then 
 

 
 (5.9) 
 
 μ v ​ ( G ) ≤ μ v ​ ( H ) , 0 < v < 1 . \mu_{v}(G)\leq\mu_{v}(H),\qquad 0<v<1. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 For  c > 0 c>0  set  e = 1 ( c , ∞ ) ​ ( G ) e=1_{(c,\infty)}(G)  and  f = 1 ( c , ∞ ) ​ ( | H | ) f=1_{(c,\infty)}(|H|) . Their ranges satisfy  e ∧ ( I − f ) = 0 e\wedge(I-f)=0 . Indeed, a nonzero vector  ξ \xi  in the intersection would give 
 

 
 
 Re ⁡ ⟨ H ​ ξ , ξ ⟩ = ⟨ G ​ ξ , ξ ⟩ > c ​ ‖ ξ ‖ 2 , ‖ H ​ ξ ‖ ≤ c ​ ‖ ξ ‖ , \operatorname{Re}\langle H\xi,\xi\rangle=\langle G\xi,\xi\rangle>c\|\xi\|^{2},\qquad\|H\xi\|\leq c\|\xi\|, 
 
 
 a contradiction. Take the polar decomposition  f ​ e = v ​ | f ​ e | fe=v|fe|  in  ℳ \mathcal{M} . The initial support is  e e , because  e ∧ ( I − f ) = 0 e\wedge(I-f)=0 , and the range support is dominated by  f f . Hence 
 

 
 
 τ ​ e = τ ⁡ ( v ∗ ​ v ) = τ ⁡ ( v ​ v ∗ ) ≤ τ ​ f . \tau e=\tau(v^{*}v)=\tau(vv^{*})\leq\tau f. 
 
 
 Thus the distribution function of  G G  is dominated by that of  | H | |H|  at every  c > 0 c>0 . Taking generalized inverses proves ( 5.9 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma 5.5 . 
 
 For bounded  A , B ≥ 0 A,B\geq 0 , 
 

 
 (5.10) 
 
 τ ​ log ⁡ ( I + A + B ) ≥ ∫ 0 1 log ⁡ ( 1 + μ v ​ ( A ) + μ v ​ ( B ) ) ​ 𝑑 v . \tau\log(I+A+B)\geq\int_{0}^{1}\log(1+\mu_{v}(A)+\mu_{v}(B))\,dv. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We give a direct proof of the relevant rearrangement inequality; see  [ 28 ]  for its general formulation. For  A ≥ 0 A\geq 0  and  0 ≤ q ≤ 1 0\leq q\leq 1 , the Ky Fan formula is 
 

 
 
 ∫ 0 q μ v ( A ) d v = sup { τ ( A Q ) : 0 ≤ Q ≤ I , τ Q = q } . \int_{0}^{q}\mu_{v}(A)\,dv=\sup\{\tau(AQ):0\leq Q\leq I,\ \tau Q=q\}. 
 
 
 Indeed, layer-cake integration and positivity give 
 

 
 
 τ ⁡ ( A ​ Q ) = ∫ 0 ∞ τ ⁡ ( Q ​ 1 ( c , ∞ ) ​ ( A ) ) ​ 𝑑 c ≤ ∫ 0 ∞ min ⁡ { q , τ ​ 1 ( c , ∞ ) ​ ( A ) } ​ 𝑑 c = ∫ 0 q μ v ​ ( A ) ​ 𝑑 v . \tau(AQ)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\tau\bigl(Q1_{(c,\infty)}(A)\bigr)\,dc\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\min\{q,\tau 1_{(c,\infty)}(A)\}\,dc=\int_{0}^{q}\mu_{v}(A)\,dv. 
 
 
 For the reverse inequality, take the spectral projection above the threshold  μ q ​ ( A ) \mu_{q}(A)  and add, if necessary, a scalar multiple of the level projection to obtain trace  q q . The endpoint cases are immediate. This choice uses positive contractions and works in algebras with atoms. 
 
 
 Put  h ⁡ ( v ) = μ v ​ ( A + B ) h(v)=\mu_{v}(A+B)  and  ν ⁡ ( v ) = μ v ​ ( A ) + μ v ​ ( B ) \nu(v)=\mu_{v}(A)+\mu_{v}(B) . Applying the formula to the sum gives 
 

 
 
 ∫ 0 q h ⁡ ( v ) ​ 𝑑 v ≤ ∫ 0 q ν ⁡ ( v ) ​ 𝑑 v ( 0 ≤ q ≤ 1 ) , ∫ 0 1 h = ∫ 0 1 ν . \int_{0}^{q}h(v)\,dv\leq\int_{0}^{q}\nu(v)\,dv\quad(0\leq q\leq 1),\qquad\int_{0}^{1}h=\int_{0}^{1}\nu. 
 
 
 Both functions are decreasing, so for  c ≥ 0 c\geq 0 , 
 

 
 
 ∫ 0 1 ( h − c ) + ​ 𝑑 v = sup 0 ≤ q ≤ 1 ( ∫ 0 q h ​ 𝑑 v − c ​ q ) ≤ ∫ 0 1 ( ν − c ) + ​ 𝑑 v . \int_{0}^{1}(h-c)_{+}\,dv=\sup_{0\leq q\leq 1}\left(\int_{0}^{q}h\,dv-cq\right)\leq\int_{0}^{1}(\nu-c)_{+}\,dv. 
 
 
 Using the elementary identity 
 

 
 
 t − log ⁡ ( 1 + t ) = ∫ 0 ∞ ( t − c ) + ( 1 + c ) 2 ​ 𝑑 c ( t ≥ 0 ) t-\log(1+t)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{(t-c)_{+}}{(1+c)^{2}}\,dc\qquad(t\geq 0) 
 
 
 and Tonelli, we obtain 
 

 
 
 ∫ 0 1 [ h − log ⁡ ( 1 + h ) ] ​ 𝑑 v ≤ ∫ 0 1 [ ν − log ⁡ ( 1 + ν ) ] ​ 𝑑 v . \int_{0}^{1}[h-\log(1+h)]\,dv\leq\int_{0}^{1}[\nu-\log(1+\nu)]\,dv. 
 
 
 The linear terms cancel, which proves ( 5.10 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 5.2.  Logarithmic kernels and the exact comparison 

 
 For a bounded accretive  A A  and  u > 0 u>0 , 
 

 
 (5.11) 
 
 log ⁡ ( I + A / u ) = ∫ 0 1 ( A / u ) ​ ( I + t ​ A / u ) − 1 ​ 𝑑 t , ‖ ( I + t ​ A / u ) − 1 ‖ ≤ 1 . \log(I+A/u)=\int_{0}^{1}(A/u)(I+tA/u)^{-1}\,dt,\qquad\|(I+tA/u)^{-1}\|\leq 1. 
 
 
 The logarithm is its principal holomorphic value, since
 Re ⁡ ( I + A / u ) ≥ I \operatorname{Re}(I+A/u)\geq I . Subtracting ( 5.11 ) gives 
 

 
 
 A / u − log ⁡ ( I + A / u ) = ∫ 0 1 t ​ ( A / u ) 2 ​ ( I + t ​ A / u ) − 1 ​ 𝑑 t . A/u-\log(I+A/u)=\int_{0}^{1}t(A/u)^{2}(I+tA/u)^{-1}\,dt. 
 
 
 Consequently, if  ‖ A ‖ ≤ M \|A\|\leq M , then for every  u > 0 u>0 , 
 

 
 (5.12) 
 
 ‖ A / u − log ⁡ ( I + A / u ) ‖ ≤ min ⁡ { 2 ​ M u , M 2 2 ​ u 2 } . \|A/u-\log(I+A/u)\|\leq\min\left\{\frac{2M}{u},\frac{M^{2}}{2u^{2}}\right\}. 
 
 
 The same bound holds for scalar  A A  in the closed right half-plane. Thus ( 5.2 ) makes all the
compensated integrals norm convergent, uniformly on bounded sets of
accretive elements. For each fixed  u u , resolvent continuity in
( 5.11 ) and dominated convergence also show that  φ ⁡ ( A ) \varphi(A) 
is norm continuous under uniformly bounded accretive perturbations. 
 
 
 Lemma 5.6 . 
 
 For every  B ∈ ℳ B\in\mathcal{M} ,  log ⁡ Det τ ⁡ ( e B ) = Re ⁡ τ ​ B \log\operatorname{Det}_{\tau}(e^{B})=\operatorname{Re}\tau B . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Determinant multiplicativity and the norm Taylor expansion give 
 

 
 
 2 ​ log ⁡ Det τ ⁡ ( e B ) = n ​ τ ​ log ⁡ ( e B ∗ / n ​ e B / n ) = τ ⁡ ( B ∗ + B ) + O ⁡ ( n − 1 ) . 2\log\operatorname{Det}_{\tau}(e^{B})=n\tau\log(e^{B^{*}/n}e^{B/n})=\tau(B^{*}+B)+O(n^{-1}). 
 
 
 Indeed, the product inside the logarithm is
 I + ( B ∗ + B ) / n + O ⁡ ( n − 2 ) I+(B^{*}+B)/n+O(n^{-2})  in norm. Letting  n → ∞ n\to\infty  proves the
assertion.
∎ 
 
 
 
 For bounded accretive  H = G + i ​ K H=G+iK , take  B = log ⁡ ( I + H / u ) B=\log(I+H/u) , its bounded
principal logarithm. Since  e B = I + H / u e^{B}=I+H/u , the lemma gives 
 

 
 (5.13) 
 
 Re ⁡ τ ​ log ⁡ ( I + H / u ) = 1 2 ​ τ ​ log ⁡ ( I + 2 ​ G / u + H ∗ ​ H / u 2 ) . \operatorname{Re}\tau\log(I+H/u)=\tfrac{1}{2}\tau\log\left(I+2G/u+H^{*}H/u^{2}\right). 
 
 
 
 
 Proof of Theorem  5.1 . 
 
 Lemma  5.4  gives  x ⁡ ( v ) ≥ g ​ ( v ) 2 x(v)\geq g(v)^{2}  and  | z ⁡ ( v ) | ≤ ‖ H ‖ |z(v)|\leq\|H\| .
Lemma  5.5 , applied to  2 ​ G / u 2G/u  and  H ∗ ​ H / u 2 H^{*}H/u^{2} , gives
 ℒ u ≥ 0 \mathcal{L}_{u}\geq 0 . The trace bridge ( 5.13 ) gives directly 
 

 
 
 ∫ 0 1 Re ⁡ [ z ⁡ ( v ) / u − log ⁡ ( 1 + z ⁡ ( v ) / u ) ] ​ 𝑑 v − Re ⁡ τ ⁡ [ H / u − log ⁡ ( I + H / u ) ] = 1 2 ​ ℒ u ​ ( G , H ∗ ​ H ) . \int_{0}^{1}\operatorname{Re}[z(v)/u-\log(1+z(v)/u)]\,dv-\operatorname{Re}\tau[H/u-\log(I+H/u)]=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}_{u}(G,H^{*}H). 
 
 
 The bounds ( 5.12 ) and ( 5.2 )
justify integrating this identity against  w w  with both compensations
intact. The real affine terms agree, so this proves
( 5.4 ), including absolute convergence and  G ≥ 0 G\geq 0 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 5.3.  The compensated determinant-pair identity 

 
 Set 
 

 
 
 η s = s ​ sin ⁡ ( π ⁡ ( s − 1 ) ) π > 0 . \eta_{s}=\frac{s\sin(\pi(s-1))}{\pi}>0. 
 
 
 For  Re ⁡ z > 0 \operatorname{Re}z>0  the following convergent, compensated formula holds: 
 

 
 (5.14) 
 
 z s = η s ​ ∫ 0 ∞ u s − 1 ​ [ z u − log ⁡ ( 1 + z u ) ] ​ 𝑑 u . z^{s}=\eta_{s}\int_{0}^{\infty}u^{s-1}\left[\frac{z}{u}-\log\left(1+\frac{z}{u}\right)\right]du. 
 
 
 For positive real  z z , integration by parts gives 
 

 
 
 ∫ 0 ∞ u s − 1 ​ [ z u − log ⁡ ( 1 + z / u ) ] ​ 𝑑 u = z 2 s ​ ∫ 0 ∞ u s − 2 u + z ​ 𝑑 u = π ​ z s s ​ sin ⁡ ( π ⁡ ( s − 1 ) ) . \int_{0}^{\infty}u^{s-1}\left[\frac{z}{u}-\log(1+z/u)\right]du=\frac{z^{2}}{s}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{u^{s-2}}{u+z}\,du=\frac{\pi z^{s}}{s\sin(\pi(s-1))}. 
 
 
 The boundary terms vanish since  1 < s < 2 1<s<2 . On compact subsets of the right half-plane the compensated integrand is uniformly  O ⁡ ( u − 1 + | log ⁡ u | ) O(u^{-1}+|\log u|)  at zero and  O ⁡ ( u − 2 ) O(u^{-2})  at infinity. It therefore defines a holomorphic function, and analytic continuation proves ( 5.14 ). The two terms are never integrated separately. 
 
 
 For  Re ⁡ A ≥ δ ​ I > 0 \operatorname{Re}A\geq\delta I>0 , choose a Dunford contour enclosing
its spectrum in  { Re z ≥ δ / 2 } \{\operatorname{Re}z\geq\delta/2\} . The uniform compensated
scalar bounds and the bounded resolvent on this contour permit
interchange with the contour integral. Hence ( 5.14 )
holds as a norm-convergent operator integral and gives the principal
holomorphic power  A s A^{s} .
For every bounded accretive  A A , we use the same formula for its
boundary value: 
 

 
 (5.15) 
 
 A s = η s ​ ∫ 0 ∞ u s − 1 ​ [ A / u − log ⁡ ( I + A / u ) ] ​ 𝑑 u . A^{s}=\eta_{s}\int_{0}^{\infty}u^{s-1}[A/u-\log(I+A/u)]\,du. 
 
 
 The bounds ( 5.12 ) show norm convergence and
continuity under uniformly bounded accretive perturbations, because
 u s − 2 u^{s-2}  at zero and  u s − 3 u^{s-3}  at infinity are integrable precisely
for  1 < s < 2 1<s<2 . This identifies ( 5.15 ) with the
continuous extension of the principal holomorphic power. 
 
 
 Lemma 5.7 . 
 
 Let  0 < β < 1 0<\beta<1 ,  b β = π / sin ⁡ ( π ​ β ) b_{\beta}=\pi/\sin(\pi\beta) , and  Re ⁡ a > 0 \operatorname{Re}a>0 .
Then, locally uniformly in  a a , the following integrals converge absolutely: 
 

 
 (5.16) 
 
 K β ​ ( a ) \displaystyle K_{\beta}(a) 
 : = ∫ 0 ∞ t β ​ [ ( t + a ) − 1 − t − 1 ] ​ 𝑑 t = − b β ​ a β , \displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\beta}[(t+a)^{-1}-t^{-1}]\,dt=-b_{\beta}a^{\beta}, 
 
 
 (5.17) 
 
 ∫ 0 ∞ t β ​ [ log ⁡ ( 1 + a / t ) − a / ( t + a ) ] ​ 𝑑 t \displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\beta}[\log(1+a/t)-a/(t+a)]\,dt 
 = β ​ b β 1 + β ​ a 1 + β . \displaystyle=\frac{\beta b_{\beta}}{1+\beta}a^{1+\beta}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 By ( 5.14 ),
 I ⁡ ( a ) := ∫ 0 ∞ t β ​ [ a / t − log ⁡ ( 1 + a / t ) ] ​ 𝑑 t = b β ​ a 1 + β / ( 1 + β ) I(a):=\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\beta}[a/t-\log(1+a/t)]\,dt=b_{\beta}a^{1+\beta}/(1+\beta) .
The differentiated integrand is locally uniformly  O ⁡ ( t β − 1 ) O(t^{\beta-1}) 
at zero and  O ⁡ ( t β − 2 ) O(t^{\beta-2})  at infinity. Thus differentiation is
valid, and the two displayed integrals are  − I ′ ​ ( a ) -I^{\prime}(a)  and  a ​ I ′ ​ ( a ) − I ⁡ ( a ) aI^{\prime}(a)-I(a) .
The logarithmic integrand is  O ⁡ ( t β ​ ( 1 + | log ⁡ t | ) ) O(t^{\beta}(1+|\log t|))  at zero and
 O ⁡ ( t β − 2 ) O(t^{\beta-2})  at infinity, proving the convergence assertion.
∎

 This identity retains the noncommutative pair  ( G , X ) (G,X) .

 Lemma 5.8 .

 Proof of Corollary  5.3 .

 5.5.  Determinant covariance and its exact residual 

 
 We prove the trace inequality stated in Theorem  1.2 ,
including its exact nonnegative residual.

of its determinant also gives  τ ​ log − ​ T t ​ ( z ) < ∞ \tau\log^{-}T_{t}(z)<\infty .

 The first inequality is the spectral integral of  x − 1 − log ⁡ x ≥ 0 x-1-\log x\geq 0 .

 Set  C β = b β ​ sin ⁡ ( β ​ θ ) / sin ⁡ θ C_{\beta}=b_{\beta}\sin(\beta\theta)/\sin\theta  and

 For  d ∈ [ m , L ] d\in[m,L] ,  0 < m ≤ L < ∞ 0<m\leq L<\infty , these integrands are uniformly

 6.  Analytic witnesses and the sharp Riesz theorem 

 
 We construct analytic test elements whose pairing with the retained
summand is exact. For  2 < p < 4 2<p<4 , factorization converts determinant
covariance into an  L 2 L^{2}  angle estimate. For  p > 4 p>4 , the positive
canonical estimate supplies the test element directly. The analytic
regularization behind that estimate is proved in
Appendix  D  without sharp boundedness.
Continuity and duality complete the retained projection; a
subdiagonal corner construction then gives the strict projection. 
 
 
 Lemma 6.1 . 
 
 Let  1 < p < ∞ 1<p<\infty ,  U ∈ ℬ U\in\mathcal{B} , and  0 ≠ W ∈ H p ′ 0\neq W\in H^{p^{\prime}} .
For every  Y ∈ ℬ 0 ∗ Y\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{*} , 
 

 
 (6.1) 
 
 ‖ U + Y ‖ p ≥ Re ⁡ τ ⁡ ( U ∗ ​ W ) ‖ W ‖ p ′ . \|U+Y\|_{p}\geq\frac{\operatorname{Re}\tau(U^{*}W)}{\|W\|_{p^{\prime}}}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Approximate  W W  in  L p ′ L^{p^{\prime}}  by bounded analytic elements.
Multiplicativity of  Φ \Phi  and trace preservation give
 τ ⁡ ( Y ∗ ​ W ) = 0 \tau(Y^{*}W)=0 , and Hölder’s inequality gives the assertion.
This uses no boundedness of the  L p L^{p}  projection.
∎ 
 
 
 
 6.1.  The positive canonical Hardy estimate 

 
 Let  P 2 P_{2}  be the  L 2 L^{2}  orthogonal projection onto  H 2 H^{2} , and set  𝒯 ℬ = 2 ​ P 2 − Φ \mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}=2P_{2}-\Phi . Appendix  C  proves that for bounded  g ≥ 0 g\geq 0 , 
 

 
 
 h = 𝒯 ℬ ​ g ∈ H 2 , Re ⁡ h = g , Φ ​ h = Φ ​ g ≥ 0 . h=\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}g\in H^{2},\qquad\operatorname{Re}h=g,\qquad\Phi h=\Phi g\geq 0. 
 
 
 Appendix  D  proves the bounded analytic regularization used below, independently of the sharp theorem. 
 
 
 Corollary 6.2 . 
 
 For  1 < s < 2 1<s<2  and bounded  g ≥ 0 g\geq 0 , 
 

 
 (6.2) 
 
 ‖ 𝒯 ℬ ​ g ‖ s ≤ sec ⁡ π 2 ​ s ​ ‖ g ‖ s . \|\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}g\|_{s}\leq\sec\frac{\pi}{2s}\,\|g\|_{s}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  b ∈ ℬ b\in\mathcal{B}  be bounded and accretive with  Φ ​ b ≥ 0 \Phi b\geq 0 .
For  u > 0 u>0  and  0 ≤ t ≤ 1 0\leq t\leq 1 ,  C = I + t ​ b / u C=I+tb/u  has  Re ⁡ C ≥ I \operatorname{Re}C\geq I ;
 ‖ I − r ​ C ‖ 2 ≤ 1 − 2 ​ r + r 2 ​ ‖ C ‖ 2 < 1 \|I-rC\|^{2}\leq 1-2r+r^{2}\|C\|^{2}<1  for sufficiently small  r > 0 r>0 .
A rescaled Neumann series gives  C − 1 ∈ ℬ C^{-1}\in\mathcal{B} , and multiplicativity gives
 Φ ⁡ ( C − 1 ) = ( I + t ​ Φ ​ b / u ) − 1 \Phi(C^{-1})=(I+t\Phi b/u)^{-1} .
Equation ( 5.11 ) therefore intertwines the compensated
logarithmic kernels under  Φ \Phi . By ( 5.12 ), their
norm-convergent Mellin integrals commute with  Φ \Phi , including when
 Φ ​ b \Phi b  has a kernel. Thus ( 5.15 ) gives
 Φ ⁡ ( b s ) = ( Φ ​ b ) s \Phi(b^{s})=(\Phi b)^{s}  and  τ ​ b s = τ ​ ( Φ ​ b ) s ≥ 0 \tau b^{s}=\tau(\Phi b)^{s}\geq 0 .
Corollary  5.3  yields
 ‖ b ‖ s ≤ sec ⁡ ( π / ( 2 ​ s ) ) ​ ‖ Re ⁡ b ‖ s \|b\|_{s}\leq\sec(\pi/(2s))\|\operatorname{Re}b\|_{s} .
For  h = 𝒯 ℬ ​ g h=\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}g , apply this bound to the bounded analytic
Yosida approximants  b ε = h ​ ( I + ε ​ h ) − 1 b_{\varepsilon}=h(I+\varepsilon h)^{-1}  from
Lemma  D.2 . Their real parts and expectations
are nonnegative, and convergence in  L 2 L^{2} , hence in  L s L^{s} , proves
( 6.2 ). Their analytic membership is established
independently in Appendix  D .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 6.2.  The middle exponent interval 

 
 Corollary  5.10  and analytic factorization supply this interval
without any previously established sharp projection estimate. 
 
 
 Corollary 6.3 . 
 
 For  2 ≤ p ≤ 4 2\leq p\leq 4 ,  U ∈ H p U\in H^{p}  and  Y ∈ ( H 0 p ) ∗ Y\in(H_{0}^{p})^{*} ,

 ‖ W ‖ p ′ ≤ h ​ ‖ R ‖ q = h ​ S 1 / q \|W\|_{p^{\prime}}\leq h\|R\|_{q}=hS^{1/q} .

 ∥ U + Y ∥ p ≥ h S − 1 / q ≥ sin ( π / p ) S 1 / 2 − 1 / q = sin ( π / p ) ∥ U ∥ p . \|U+Y\|_{p}\geq hS^{-1/q}\geq\sin(\pi/p)S^{1/2-1/q}=\sin(\pi/p)\|U\|_{p}. 
 
 
 For an arbitrary bounded  U ∈ ℬ U\in\mathcal{B} , the matrices 
 

 
 
 U ε = ( U ε ​ I ε ​ I 0 ) , U ε − 1 = ( 0 ε − 1 ​ I ε − 1 ​ I − ε − 2 ​ U ) U_{\varepsilon}=\begin{pmatrix}U&\varepsilon I\\

As  ε ↓ 0 \varepsilon\downarrow 0 , operator-norm continuity gives the desired

 6.3.  Completion of the all-exponent projection theorem 

 
 6.3.1.  Exponents above four 

 
 Proof of Theorem  1.1  for  p > 4 p>4 . 
 
 For bounded  U ∈ ℬ U\in\mathcal{B} ,  U ≠ 0 U\neq 0 , set 
 

 
 
 s = p p − 2 ∈ ( 1 , 2 ) , S = ‖ U ‖ p p , g = ( U ​ U ∗ ) ( p − 2 ) / 2 , h = 𝒯 ℬ ​ g , W = h ​ U . s=\frac{p}{p-2}\in(1,2),\qquad S=\|U\|_{p}^{p},\qquad g=(UU^{*})^{(p-2)/2},\quad h=\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}g,\quad W=hU. 
 
 
 Here  g g  is bounded and positive. Since  1 / p ′ = 1 / s + 1 / p 1/p^{\prime}=1/s+1/p , Corollary  6.2  and Hölder give 
 

 
 (6.4) 
 
 ‖ W ‖ p ′ ≤ sec ⁡ π 2 ​ s ​ ‖ U ‖ p p − 1 . \|W\|_{p^{\prime}}\leq\sec\frac{\pi}{2s}\,\|U\|_{p}^{p-1}. 
 
 
 Since  h ∈ H 2 h\in H^{2} , the bounded  ℬ \mathcal{B}  module property in
Appendix  C  and  H 2 ⊂ H p ′ H^{2}\subset H^{p^{\prime}}  give  W ∈ H p ′ W\in H^{p^{\prime}} .
On the other hand,  1 / s + 2 / p = 1 1/s+2/p=1  and trace cyclicity give the exact pairing 
 

 
 (6.5) 
 
 Re ⁡ τ ​ U ∗ ​ W = τ ⁡ ( ( U ​ U ∗ ) ​ Re ⁡ h ) = τ ⁡ ( ( U ​ U ∗ ) ​ g ) = S . \operatorname{Re}\tau U^{*}W=\tau\bigl((UU^{*})\operatorname{Re}h\bigr)=\tau\bigl((UU^{*})g\bigr)=S. 
 
 
 Lemma  6.1  consequently gives 
 

 
 
 ‖ U + Y ‖ p ≥ S ‖ W ‖ p ′ ≥ cos ⁡ π 2 ​ s ​ ‖ U ‖ p = sin ⁡ π p ​ ‖ U ‖ p . \|U+Y\|_{p}\geq\frac{S}{\|W\|_{p^{\prime}}}\geq\cos\frac{\pi}{2s}\,\|U\|_{p}=\sin\frac{\pi}{p}\,\|U\|_{p}. 
 
 
 Core density extends this estimate to  L p L^{p} .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 6.3.2.  Dual exponents 

 
 For  1 < p < 2 1<p<2  let  q = p ′ > 2 q=p^{\prime}>2 . The preceding arguments bound  P P  on  L q L^{q} . On  𝒞 \mathcal{C}  the trace adjoint of  P P  is itself by ( C.1 ). Its Banach adjoint therefore defines the bounded extension on  L p L^{p} , agrees with  P P  on the core, and has norm at most  csc ⁡ ( π / q ) = csc ⁡ ( π / p ) \csc(\pi/q)=\csc(\pi/p) . This proves the retained-diagonal assertion of Theorem  1.1 . 
 
 
 
 The strict projection and uniform sharpness 

 
 In  M 2 ​ ( ℳ ) M_{2}(\mathcal{M}) , with normalized matrix trace, consider 
 

 
 
 ℬ ~ = ( ℬ ℬ 0 ℬ ℬ ) , Φ ~ ​ ( x y z w ) = ( Φ ​ x 0 0 Φ ​ w ) . \widetilde{\mathcal{B}}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{B}&\mathcal{B}_{0}\\
\mathcal{B}&\mathcal{B}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\widetilde{\Phi}\begin{pmatrix}x&y\\
z&w\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\Phi x&0\\
0&\Phi w\end{pmatrix}. 
 
 
 It is a weak-star closed unital subdiagonal algebra:  ℬ 0 \mathcal{B}_{0}  is an ideal of  ℬ \mathcal{B} , the diagonal intersection is  𝒟 ⊕ 𝒟 \mathcal{D}\oplus\mathcal{D} , and  Φ ~ \widetilde{\Phi}  is multiplicative on it. Weak-star density and trace preservation follow entrywise. Exel’s finite-trace maximality theorem  [ 27 ]  makes it maximal. Its retained-diagonal projection on the upper-right corner is  P 0 P_{0} . Apply the already universal bound to that corner; the factor  2 − 1 / p 2^{-1/p}  in both corner norms cancels. This proves the strict bound. For bounded  U 0 ∈ ℬ 0 U_{0}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}  and
 Y 0 ∈ ℬ ∗ Y_{0}\in\mathcal{B}^{*} , it gives
 ‖ U 0 + Y 0 ‖ p ≥ sin ⁡ ( π / p ) ​ ‖ U 0 ‖ p \|U_{0}+Y_{0}\|_{p}\geq\sin(\pi/p)\|U_{0}\|_{p} , since
 P 0 ​ ( U 0 + Y 0 ) = U 0 P_{0}(U_{0}+Y_{0})=U_{0} . Approximate the two summands separately by
 ℬ \mathcal{B}  and  ℬ 0 ∗ \mathcal{B}_{0}^{*}  for the retained distance, and by  ℬ 0 \mathcal{B}_{0}  and
 ℬ ∗ \mathcal{B}^{*}  for the strict distance. Continuity of the  L p L^{p}  norm extends
both estimates to  H p + ( H 0 p ) ∗ H^{p}+(H_{0}^{p})^{*}  and  H 0 p + ( H p ) ∗ H_{0}^{p}+(H^{p})^{*} ,
respectively. 
 
 
 
 An explicit sharpness construction 

 
 Take  ℳ = L ∞ ​ ( 𝕋 ) \mathcal{M}=L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})  with normalized Haar trace and  ℬ = H ∞ ​ ( 𝕋 ) \mathcal{B}=H^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}) . For  0 < b < 1 / p 0<b<1/p  put 
 

 
 
 h b ​ ( z ) = ( 1 + z 1 − z ) b , c b = cos ⁡ ( π ​ b ) . h_{b}(z)=\left(\frac{1+z}{1-z}\right)^{b},\qquad c_{b}=\cos(\pi b). 
 
 
 The principal power is analytic in the disk and  h b ​ ( 0 ) = 1 h_{b}(0)=1 . Its boundary values have modulus  | cot ⁡ ( t / 2 ) | b |\cot(t/2)|^{b}  and argument  ± π b / 2 \pm\pi b/2 . Hence, pointwise almost everywhere, 
 

 
 
 | h b − c b ​ h b ¯ | = sin ⁡ ( π ​ b ) ​ | h b | . |h_{b}-c_{b}\overline{h_{b}}|=\sin(\pi b)|h_{b}|. 
 
 
 Set  F b = h b − c b ​ ( h b ¯ − 1 ) F_{b}=h_{b}-c_{b}(\overline{h_{b}}-1) . For fixed  b b , use first the bounded analytic functions  h b ​ ( ρ ​ z ) h_{b}(\rho z) ,  ρ < 1 \rho<1 , and their corresponding core decompositions. As  ρ ↑ 1 \rho\uparrow 1  they converge in  L p L^{p}  to the displayed functions: a bound by  C ​ | t | − b C|t|^{-b}  near zero supplies dominated convergence since  b ​ p < 1 bp<1 . Thus  P ​ F b = h b PF_{b}=h_{b} . The reverse triangle inequality gives 
 

 
 
 | ‖ F b ‖ p ‖ h b ‖ p − sin ⁡ ( π ​ b ) | ≤ | c b | ‖ h b ‖ p . \left|\frac{\|F_{b}\|_{p}}{\|h_{b}\|_{p}}-\sin(\pi b)\right|\leq\frac{|c_{b}|}{\|h_{b}\|_{p}}. 
 
 
 As  b ↑ 1 / p b\uparrow 1/p ,  ‖ h b ‖ p → ∞ \|h_{b}\|_{p}\to\infty , since  | cot ⁡ ( t / 2 ) | ∼ 2 / | t | |\cot(t/2)|\sim 2/|t|  near zero. The ratio therefore tends to  sin ⁡ ( π / p ) \sin(\pi/p) , proving  ‖ P ‖ ≥ csc ⁡ ( π / p ) \|P\|\geq\csc(\pi/p) . On the circle,  P 0 = M z ​ P ​ M z ¯ P_{0}=M_{z}PM_{\bar{z}} , where both multiplication operators are isometries; hence  ‖ P 0 ‖ = ‖ P ‖ \|P_{0}\|=\|P\| . This proves both uniform sharpness assertions and completes Theorem  1.1 . 
 
 
 Optimality of the coefficient in Theorem  1.2 . 
 
 Fix  0 < β < 1 0<\beta<1  and put  p = 2 ​ ( 1 + β ) p=2(1+\beta) , so that the squared coefficient
in ( 1.3 ) is  cos 2 ⁡ ( π / p ) \cos^{2}(\pi/p) .
If it could be replaced universally by  c < cos 2 ⁡ ( π / p ) c<\cos^{2}(\pi/p) , necessarily
 c ≥ 0 c\geq 0  by taking  D = I D=I  and a nonzero nilpotent  N N .
The invertible-case proof of Corollary  6.3  would
then give  h 2 ≥ ( 1 − c ) ​ S h^{2}\geq(1-c)S  in ( 6.3 ). Its exact pairing, matrix
argument and separate closure passage would consequently give
 ‖ U + Y ‖ p ≥ 1 − c ​ ‖ U ‖ p \|U+Y\|_{p}\geq\sqrt{1-c}\,\|U\|_{p}  for all the analytic pairs there.
Since  1 − c > sin ⁡ ( π / p ) \sqrt{1-c}>\sin(\pi/p) , this contradicts the circle sequence
 U = h b U=h_{b} ,  Y = − c b ​ ( h b ¯ − 1 ) Y=-c_{b}(\overline{h_{b}}-1)  constructed above, whose norm
ratio tends to  sin ⁡ ( π / p ) \sin(\pi/p) . Thus the coefficient is optimal.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 7.  Weighted angular realization and cone duality 

 
 Tracial joint spectral measures need not have finite total mass.
Indeed, for a nonzero nilpotent matrix  N N , one has  A = Re ⁡ N ≠ 0 A=\operatorname{Re}N\neq 0 , and 
 

 
 
 ∫ | x | q ​ d ​ μ A , B = tr ⁡ | A | q q ⁡ ( q + 1 ) ⟶ ∞ ( q ↓ 0 ) . \int|x|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{A,B}=\frac{\operatorname{tr}|A|^{q}}{q(q+1)}\longrightarrow\infty\quad(q\downarrow 0). 
 
 
 The compact support shows that a finite total mass would contradict
this divergence. We therefore use finite weighted pushforward measures on the circle.
Throughout this section,  C ⁡ ( 𝕋 ) C(\mathbb{T})  denotes the real-valued continuous
functions on  𝕋 \mathbb{T} . 
 
 
 Definition 7.1 . 
 
 For a nilpotent  N = A + i ​ B N=A+iB , define the finite positive measure  ν N \nu_{N} 
on  𝕋 \mathbb{T}  by 
 

 
 (7.1) 
 
 ∫ 𝕋 g ​ d ​ ν N = p ⁡ ( p + 1 ) ​ ∫ ℂ ∖ { 0 } | z | p ​ g ​ ( arg ⁡ z ) ​ d ​ μ A , B ​ ( z ) , g ∈ C ⁡ ( 𝕋 ) . \int_{\mathbb{T}}g\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{N}=p(p+1)\int_{\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}}|z|^{p}g(\arg z)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{A,B}(z),\qquad g\in C(\mathbb{T}). 
 
 
 For a polynomial  q q  with  q ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 q(0)=0 , define 
 

 
 (7.2) 
 
 ∫ 𝕋 g ​ d ​ σ q = ∫ 0 2 ​ π | q ⁡ ( e i ​ t ) | p ​ g ​ ( arg ⁡ q ⁡ ( e i ​ t ) ) ​ d ​ t 2 ​ π , \int_{\mathbb{T}}g\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{q}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}|q(e^{it})|^{p}g(\arg q(e^{it}))\frac{\,\mathrm{d}t}{2\pi}, 
 
 
 with integrand zero when  q ⁡ ( e i ​ t ) = 0 q(e^{it})=0 . Put 
 

 
 
 𝒮 p = { u ∈ C ⁡ ( 𝕋 ) : ℒ p ​ u := u ′′ + p 2 ​ u ≤ 0 ​  distributionally } , \mathscr{S}_{p}=\{u\in C(\mathbb{T}):\ \mathcal{L}_{p}u:=u^{\prime\prime}+p^{2}u\leq 0\text{ distributionally}\}, 
 
 
 and define its polar cone restricted to finite positive measures,
using the nonpositive-pairing convention, 
 

 
 (7.3) 
 
 𝒥 p = { ν ≥ 0 : ∫ u ​ 𝑑 ν ≤ 0 ​  for every  ​ u ∈ 𝒮 p } . \mathscr{J}_{p}=\{\nu\geq 0:\ \int u\,\mathrm{d}\nu\leq 0\text{ for every }u\in\mathscr{S}_{p}\}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Theorem 7.2 . 
 
 In the weak-* topology of finite measures paired with  C ⁡ ( 𝕋 ) C(\mathbb{T}) , 
 

 
 (7.4) 
 
 cone ⁡ { ν N : N ​  finite nilpotent } ¯ w ∗ = cone ⁡ { σ q : q ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 } ¯ w ∗ = 𝒥 p . \overline{\operatorname{cone}\{\nu_{N}:N\text{ finite nilpotent}\}}^{\,w^{*}}=\overline{\operatorname{cone}\{\sigma_{q}:q(0)=0\}}^{\,w^{*}}=\mathscr{J}_{p}. 
 
 
 The family  { ν N } \{\nu_{N}\}  is itself a convex cone, so its conic hull
can be omitted. Every measure in  𝒥 p \mathscr{J}_{p}  is consequently a
weak-* limit of a sequence of weighted angular measures of single
finite nilpotent matrices.
For each fixed polynomial  q q  with  q ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 q(0)=0 , 
 

 
 (7.5) 
 
 1 n ​ ν q ⁡ ( S n ) ⟶ σ q weak-* . \frac{1}{n}\nu_{q(S_{n})}\longrightarrow\sigma_{q}\qquad\text{weak-*}. 
 
 
 Equivalently, 
 

 
 (7.6) 
 
 𝒥 p = { ν ≥ 0 : ν = ℒ p ω , ω ∈ C ( 𝕋 ) , ω ≥ 0 } . \mathscr{J}_{p}=\{\nu\geq 0:\ \nu=\mathcal{L}_{p}\omega,\quad\omega\in C(\mathbb{T}),\quad\omega\geq 0\}. 
 
 
 Every such potential is Lipschitz. If  m = ν ⁡ ( 𝕋 ) m=\nu(\mathbb{T}) , then 
 

 
 (7.7) 
 
 ∫ 𝕋 ω ​ 𝑑 θ = m p 2 , ‖ ω ′ ‖ ∞ ≤ m , ‖ ω ‖ ∞ ≤ m 2 ​ π ​ p 2 + π ​ m . \int_{\mathbb{T}}\omega\,\mathrm{d}\theta=\frac{m}{p^{2}},\qquad\|\omega^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq m,\qquad\|\omega\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{m}{2\pi p^{2}}+\pi m. 
 
 
 Moreover,  ( ω , ν ) (\omega,\nu)  is approximated by nonnegative trigonometric
polynomials  ( ω k , v k ) (\omega_{k},v_{k})  satisfying  v k = ℒ p ​ ω k v_{k}=\mathcal{L}_{p}\omega_{k} ,
 ∫ v k ​ 𝑑 θ = m \int v_{k}\,\mathrm{d}\theta=m ,  ω k → ω \omega_{k}\to\omega  uniformly, and
 v k ​ d ​ θ → ν v_{k}\,\mathrm{d}\theta\to\nu  weak-*.
The realization uses weighted angular measures and allows growing
matrix dimensions. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We first prove the approximation assertion for continuous angular tests.
Put  N n = q ⁡ ( S n ) = A n + i ​ B n N_{n}=q(S_{n})=A_{n}+iB_{n} ,  μ n = n − 1 ​ μ A n , B n \mu_{n}=n^{-1}\mu_{A_{n},B_{n}} , and
 η n = | z | 2 ​ μ n \eta_{n}=|z|^{2}\mu_{n} . Their supports lie in a common compact square.
Define the finite measure 
 

 
 (7.8) 
 
 η = ∫ 0 2 ​ π ∫ 0 1 r ⁡ ( 1 − r ) ​ | q ⁡ ( e i ​ t ) | 2 ​ δ r ​ q ​ ( e i ​ t ) ​ 𝑑 r ​ d ​ t 2 ​ π . \eta=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{1}r(1-r)|q(e^{it})|^{2}\delta_{rq(e^{it})}\,\mathrm{d}r\,\frac{\,\mathrm{d}t}{2\pi}. 
 
 
 For every polynomial  P P , the homogeneous parts of  | z | 2 ​ P |z|^{2}P  are
spanned by powers of real linear forms. Equation ( 2.3 )
and Lemma  3.1  give  ∫ P ​ d ​ η n → ∫ P ​ 𝑑 η \int P\,\mathrm{d}\eta_{n}\to\int P\,\mathrm{d}\eta .
Uniform polynomial approximation therefore gives  η n → η \eta_{n}\to\eta  weakly.
Choose  0 < α < min ⁡ { p , 2 } 0<\alpha<\min\{p,2\} . The ridge moments and the uniform
operator norm bound give  sup n ∫ | z | α − 2 ​ d ​ η n < ∞ \sup_{n}\int|z|^{\alpha-2}\,\mathrm{d}\eta_{n}<\infty , while 
 

 
 
 ∫ | z | α − 2 ​ 𝑑 η = 1 α ⁡ ( α + 1 ) ​ ∫ 0 2 ​ π | q ⁡ ( e i ​ t ) | α ​ d ​ t 2 ​ π < ∞ . \int|z|^{\alpha-2}\,\mathrm{d}\eta=\frac{1}{\alpha(\alpha+1)}\int_{0}^{2\pi}|q(e^{it})|^{\alpha}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}t}{2\pi}<\infty. 
 
 
 For both measures, the origin tail of  | z | p − 2 ​ g ​ ( arg ⁡ z ) |z|^{p-2}g(\arg z)  is thus
 O ⁡ ( ‖ g ‖ ∞ ​ δ p − α ) O(\|g\|_{\infty}\delta^{p-\alpha}) .
Take a continuous radial cutoff  0 ≤ χ δ ≤ 1 0\leq\chi_{\delta}\leq 1 , zero on
 [ 0 , δ ] [0,\delta]  and one on  [ 2 ​ δ , ∞ ) [2\delta,\infty) .
Test weak convergence with  χ δ ​ ( | z | ) ​ | z | p − 2 ​ g ​ ( arg ⁡ z ) \chi_{\delta}(|z|)|z|^{p-2}g(\arg z)  and
then remove the cutoff. The radial integral
 ∫ 0 1 ( 1 − r ) ​ r p − 1 ​ 𝑑 r = 1 / [ p ⁡ ( p + 1 ) ] \int_{0}^{1}(1-r)r^{p-1}\,\mathrm{d}r=1/[p(p+1)]  proves ( 7.5 ). 
 
 
 Let  𝒞 N , 𝒞 P \mathcal{C}_{N},\mathcal{C}_{P}  be the closed cones in
( 7.4 ), and let  𝒱 p \mathcal{V}_{p}  denote the
cone of measures with nonnegative potentials on the right of ( 7.6 ).
We prove the inclusions 
 

 
 
 𝒞 P ⊂ 𝒞 N ⊂ 𝒱 p ⊂ 𝒥 p ⊂ 𝒞 P . \mathcal{C}_{P}\subset\mathcal{C}_{N}\subset\mathcal{V}_{p}\subset\mathscr{J}_{p}\subset\mathcal{C}_{P}. 
 
 
 The first inclusion is ( 7.5 ). For a finite
nilpotent define 
 

 
 (7.9) 
 
 ω N ( p ) ​ ( θ ) = 1 2 ​ π ​ ∫ 0 ∞ Ψ N ​ ( r ​ e i ​ θ ) ​ r p − 1 ​ 𝑑 r ≥ 0 . \omega_{N}^{(p)}(\theta)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Psi_{N}(re^{i\theta})r^{p-1}\,\mathrm{d}r\geq 0. 
 
 
 It is integrable by compact support and the logarithmic bound at zero.
Testing ( 2.24 ) with smooth angular profiles gives 
 

 
 (7.10) 
 
 ν N = ℒ p ​ ω N ( p ) . \nu_{N}=\mathcal{L}_{p}\omega_{N}^{(p)}. 
 
 
 Such homogeneous tests have the required local Sobolev regularity
by choosing  q > 1 q>1  close enough to one. 
 
 
 If  ω ∈ L 1 ​ ( 𝕋 ) \omega\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T})  is nonnegative and  ℒ p ​ ω = ν ≥ 0 \mathcal{L}_{p}\omega=\nu\geq 0 ,
testing against  1 1  gives  p 2 ​ ∫ ω ​ 𝑑 θ = m = ν ⁡ ( 𝕋 ) p^{2}\int\omega\,\mathrm{d}\theta=m=\nu(\mathbb{T}) .
Hence  ω ′′ = ν − p 2 ​ ω ​ d ​ θ \omega^{\prime\prime}=\nu-p^{2}\omega\,\mathrm{d}\theta  has total variation at most
 2 ​ m 2m . The derivative  ω ′ \omega^{\prime}  has a periodic BV representative of
mean zero. Its essential oscillation is at most half its total
variation: the two arcs joining any two Lebesgue points each
contribute at least their difference. Thus
 ‖ ω ′ ‖ ∞ ≤ osc ⁡ ( ω ′ ) ≤ m \|\omega^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq\operatorname{osc}(\omega^{\prime})\leq m .
Its primitive is Lipschitz, and the shorter circular arc gives
 osc ⁡ ( ω ) ≤ π ​ m \operatorname{osc}(\omega)\leq\pi m .
Comparison with the mean gives the last bound in
( 7.7 ). In particular ( 7.9 ) has a
nonnegative continuous representative, and every generator belongs
to  𝒱 p \mathcal{V}_{p} .
These bounds also show that  𝒱 p \mathcal{V}_{p}  is weak-* closed.
Indeed, let a net  ν γ ∈ 𝒱 p \nu_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{V}_{p}  converge weak-* to
 ν \nu , and choose nonnegative continuous potentials  ω γ \omega_{\gamma} 
with  ℒ p ​ ω γ = ν γ \mathcal{L}_{p}\omega_{\gamma}=\nu_{\gamma} . The limit  ν \nu  is positive.
Testing against  1 1  shows that, after restricting to a tail, the
masses are uniformly bounded. The estimates
( 7.7 ) then make the potentials uniformly
bounded and equi-Lipschitz. By Arzelà–Ascoli, a subnet converges
uniformly to a nonnegative continuous function  ω \omega .
For every  φ ∈ C ∞ ​ ( 𝕋 ) \varphi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}) , passage to the limit gives 
 

 
 
 ∫ 𝕋 φ ​ 𝑑 ν = ∫ 𝕋 ω ​ ℒ p ​ φ ​ 𝑑 θ . \int_{\mathbb{T}}\varphi\,\,\mathrm{d}\nu=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\omega\,\mathcal{L}_{p}\varphi\,\,\mathrm{d}\theta. 
 
 
 Thus  ν = ℒ p ​ ω ∈ 𝒱 p \nu=\mathcal{L}_{p}\omega\in\mathcal{V}_{p} . Since  𝒱 p \mathcal{V}_{p}  is a
convex cone containing every generator, this proves
 𝒞 N ⊂ 𝒱 p \mathcal{C}_{N}\subset\mathcal{V}_{p} . 
 
 
 For  ν = ℒ p ​ ω ∈ 𝒱 p \nu=\mathcal{L}_{p}\omega\in\mathcal{V}_{p}  and  u ∈ 𝒮 p u\in\mathscr{S}_{p} , smooth
angular convolutions satisfy 
 

 
 
 ∫ u ​ 𝑑 ν = lim ε ↓ 0 ∫ ω ​ ℒ p ​ u ε ​ 𝑑 θ ≤ 0 . \int u\,\mathrm{d}\nu=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\int\omega\mathcal{L}_{p}u_{\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}\theta\leq 0. 
 
 
 Thus  𝒱 p ⊂ 𝒥 p \mathcal{V}_{p}\subset\mathscr{J}_{p} . 
 
 
 For the reverse inclusion, suppose a finite positive measure
 ν ∈ 𝒥 p \nu\in\mathscr{J}_{p}  lies outside  𝒞 P \mathcal{C}_{P} . Hahn–Banach
separation for the weak-* closed convex cone gives  g ∈ C ⁡ ( 𝕋 ) g\in C(\mathbb{T}) 
with 
 

 
 
 ∫ g ​ d ​ σ q ≤ 0 for all  ​ q ​ ( 0 ) = 0 , ∫ g ​ 𝑑 ν > 0 . \int g\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{q}\leq 0\quad\text{for all }q(0)=0,\qquad\int g\,\mathrm{d}\nu>0. 
 
 
 Set  G ⁡ ( r ​ e i ​ θ ) = r p ​ g ​ ( θ ) G(re^{i\theta})=r^{p}g(\theta)  and  G ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 G(0)=0 .
Lemma  3.3  gives a continuous homogeneous
superharmonic majorant with angular part  u ∈ 𝒮 p u\in\mathscr{S}_{p} .
Since  g ≤ u g\leq u  and  ν ≥ 0 \nu\geq 0 , one obtains
 ∫ g ​ 𝑑 ν ≤ ∫ u ​ 𝑑 ν ≤ 0 \int g\,\mathrm{d}\nu\leq\int u\,\mathrm{d}\nu\leq 0 , a contradiction.
Thus  𝒥 p ⊂ 𝒞 P \mathscr{J}_{p}\subset\mathcal{C}_{P} , which proves both descriptions of the cone. 
 
 
 For the final realization assertion, the tracial measures are additive
under direct sums and transform by pushforward under positive scalar
dilation, by uniqueness in Heinävaara’s representation
 [ 2 ] . It follows that 
 

 
 
 ν N ⊕ M = ν N + ν M , ν r ​ N = r p ν N ( r ≥ 0 ) . \nu_{N\oplus M}=\nu_{N}+\nu_{M},\qquad\nu_{rN}=r^{p}\nu_{N}\quad(r\geq 0). 
 
 
 Thus the first generating family is a convex cone. Explicitly, for
a finite combination  ∑ ℓ = 1 L t ℓ ​ σ q ℓ \sum_{\ell=1}^{L}t_{\ell}\sigma_{q_{\ell}} ,
 t ℓ ≥ 0 t_{\ell}\geq 0 , one may use the single nilpotent matrix 
 

 
 
 M n = ⨁ ℓ = 1 L ( t ℓ / n ) 1 / p ​ q ℓ ​ ( S n ) . M_{n}=\bigoplus_{\ell=1}^{L}(t_{\ell}/n)^{1/p}q_{\ell}(S_{n}). 
 
 
 Its angular measure converges to that combination by
( 7.5 ). Weak-* topology on mass-bounded positive
measures on the circle is metrizable. Approximating an arbitrary
member of the closed cone and using a diagonal argument gives
the required sequence; mass bounds are obtained by including the test  g = 1 g=1 . 
 
 
 Finally let  K k K_{k}  be the nonnegative Fejér kernels normalized by
 ∫ K k ​ 𝑑 θ = 1 \int K_{k}\,\mathrm{d}\theta=1 . Put  ω k = K k ∗ ω \omega_{k}=K_{k}*\omega  and
 v k = K k ∗ ν v_{k}=K_{k}*\nu . Positivity, the mass identity, and
 v k = ℒ p ​ ω k v_{k}=\mathcal{L}_{p}\omega_{k}  are preserved; the asserted convergences are the
approximate-identity properties. At an integer exponent  p ≥ 2 p\geq 2 , every  ν ∈ 𝒥 p \nu\in\mathscr{J}_{p} 
satisfies  ∫ 𝕋 e i ​ p ​ θ ​ 𝑑 ν ​ ( θ ) = 0 \int_{\mathbb{T}}e^{ip\theta}\,\,\mathrm{d}\nu(\theta)=0 , since both
signs of  cos ⁡ ( p ​ θ ) \cos(p\theta)  and  sin ⁡ ( p ​ θ ) \sin(p\theta)  belong to  𝒮 p \mathscr{S}_{p} .
Any two potentials for the same measure differ by
 a ​ cos ⁡ ( p ​ θ ) + b ​ sin ⁡ ( p ​ θ ) a\cos(p\theta)+b\sin(p\theta) , with  a , b ∈ ℝ a,b\in\mathbb{R} .
The bounds above hold for every nonnegative potential, so no
normalization of this kernel component is needed in the
compactness argument.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 8.  Sharp consequences 

 
 We first pass the finite trace certificate to compact operators and
obtain the exact comparison for a prescribed spectral set, followed
by its real diagonal perturbation consequence. At exponent four,
the directional moment disk gives an explicit signed classification
and joint norm formula. We then realize the analytic-distance
theorem as sharp triangular and Fourier projection bounds. 
 
 
 8.1.  Compact spectral corrections 

 
 Compressing a quasinilpotent operator need not preserve quasinilpotence.
The spectral correction must be passed to the limit. 
 
 
 Theorem 8.1 . 
 
 Let  1 < p < ∞ 1<p<\infty  and  T n → T T_{n}\to T  in  𝒮 p \mathcal{S}_{p} , with all operators
compact. List their nonzero eigenvalues with algebraic multiplicities
and append countably many zeros. For two such lists define 
 

 
 
 d p ​ ( Λ ⁡ ( S ) , Λ ⁡ ( T ) ) = inf σ ( ∑ j ≥ 1 | λ j ​ ( S ) − λ σ ⁡ ( j ) ​ ( T ) | p ) 1 / p , d_{p}(\Lambda(S),\Lambda(T))=\inf_{\sigma}\left(\sum_{j\geq 1}|\lambda_{j}(S)-\lambda_{\sigma(j)}(T)|^{p}\right)^{1/p}, 
 
 
 where  σ \sigma  ranges over bijections of  ℕ \mathbb{N} .
Then  d p ​ ( Λ ⁡ ( T n ) , Λ ⁡ ( T ) ) → 0 d_{p}(\Lambda(T_{n}),\Lambda(T))\to 0 . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 For  S ∈ 𝒮 p S\in\mathcal{S}_{p} , select finitely many generalized eigenspaces with
 0 < | λ j ​ ( S ) | ≤ δ 0<|\lambda_{j}(S)|\leq\delta  and write
 b p = ∑ j | λ j ​ ( S ) | p b^{p}=\sum_{j}|\lambda_{j}(S)|^{p} . In a Schur basis on their invariant
sum  E E , take  D = diag ⁡ ( λ ¯ j ​ | λ j | p − 2 ) D=\operatorname{diag}(\overline{\lambda}_{j}|\lambda_{j}|^{p-2}) 
and extend it by zero. Invariance gives
 tr ⁡ ( D ​ S ) = tr E ⁡ ( D ​ S | E ) = b p \operatorname{tr}(DS)=\operatorname{tr}_{E}(DS|_{E})=b^{p} , while
 ‖ D ‖ ≤ δ p − 1 \|D\|\leq\delta^{p-1}  and  ‖ D ‖ p ′ = b p − 1 \|D\|_{p^{\prime}}=b^{p-1} .
For every finite-rank  F F , trace Hölder therefore gives 
 

 
 
 b p ≤ a + b p − 1 ​ ε , a = δ p − 1 ​ ‖ F ‖ 1 , ε = ‖ S − F ‖ p . b^{p}\leq a+b^{p-1}\varepsilon,\qquad a=\delta^{p-1}\|F\|_{1},\quad\varepsilon=\|S-F\|_{p}. 
 
 
 If  b > ε b>\varepsilon , then
 ( b − ε ) p ≤ b p − 1 ​ ( b − ε ) ≤ a (b-\varepsilon)^{p}\leq b^{p-1}(b-\varepsilon)\leq a ;
otherwise  b ≤ ε b\leq\varepsilon . Exhausting the small spectrum yields 
 

 
 (8.1) 
 
 b δ ​ ( S ) := ( ∑ 0 < | λ j ​ ( S ) | ≤ δ | λ j ​ ( S ) | p ) 1 / p ≤ ‖ S − F ‖ p + δ 1 / p ′ ​ ‖ F ‖ 1 1 / p . b_{\delta}(S):=\left(\sum_{0<|\lambda_{j}(S)|\leq\delta}|\lambda_{j}(S)|^{p}\right)^{1/p}\leq\|S-F\|_{p}+\delta^{1/p^{\prime}}\|F\|_{1}^{1/p}. 
 
 
 Taking  F = 0 F=0  and  δ ≥ ‖ S ‖ \delta\geq\|S\|  also proves
 ∑ j | λ j ​ ( S ) | p ≤ ‖ S ‖ p p \sum_{j}|\lambda_{j}(S)|^{p}\leq\|S\|_{p}^{p} .
Apply ( 8.1 ) to  S = T n S=T_{n} , let  δ ↓ 0 \delta\downarrow 0  with
 F F  fixed, and then approximate  T T  by finite-rank  F F . Thus 
 

 
 (8.2) 
 
 lim δ ↓ 0 lim sup n b δ ​ ( T n ) = 0 . \lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\limsup_{n}b_{\delta}(T_{n})=0. 
 
 
 
 
 Choose  | z | = δ |z|=\delta  avoiding  spec ⁡ T \operatorname{spec}T . Norm convergence and
Riesz projections  [ 20 , Chapter IV]  match the finite eigenvalue
multisets outside this circle, including algebraic multiplicities.
Let their cardinality be  m δ m_{\delta}  and maximum matching distance
 η n , δ → 0 \eta_{n,\delta}\to 0 , with  η n , δ = 0 \eta_{n,\delta}=0  if  m δ = 0 m_{\delta}=0 .
Match the remaining padded lists arbitrarily. Then 
 

 
 
 d p ​ ( Λ ⁡ ( T n ) , Λ ⁡ ( T ) ) p ≤ m δ ​ η n , δ p + 2 p − 1 ​ ( b δ ​ ( T n ) p + b δ ​ ( T ) p ) . d_{p}(\Lambda(T_{n}),\Lambda(T))^{p}\leq m_{\delta}\eta_{n,\delta}^{p}+2^{p-1}\bigl(b_{\delta}(T_{n})^{p}+b_{\delta}(T)^{p}\bigr). 
 
 
 First let  n → ∞ n\to\infty , then  δ ↓ 0 \delta\downarrow 0  through admissible
circles. Both tails vanish, proving the theorem.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Corollary 8.2 . 
 
 Under Theorem  8.1 , every continuous  h h  on
a common spectral disk with  | h ⁡ ( z ) | ≤ M ​ | z | p |h(z)|\leq M|z|^{p}  satisfies 
 

 
 (8.3) 
 
 ∑ j h ⁡ ( λ j ​ ( T n ) ) ⟶ ∑ j h ⁡ ( λ j ​ ( T ) ) . \sum_{j}h(\lambda_{j}(T_{n}))\longrightarrow\sum_{j}h(\lambda_{j}(T)). 
 
 
 The series converge absolutely. The convergence is uniform over any
family of continuous tests  ℋ \mathcal{H}  with the same growth bound and equicontinuity
on each closed annulus away from zero. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Absolute convergence follows from the spectral  ℓ p \ell^{p}  bound.
For a common disk  | z | ≤ R |z|\leq R , let  ω ℋ , δ \omega_{\mathcal{H},\delta}  be
a common modulus on  δ ≤ | z | ≤ R \delta\leq|z|\leq R . The matching above gives 
 

 
 
 sup h ∈ ℋ | ∑ j h ⁡ ( λ j ​ ( T n ) ) − ∑ j h ⁡ ( λ j ​ ( T ) ) | \displaystyle\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\left|\sum_{j}h(\lambda_{j}(T_{n}))-\sum_{j}h(\lambda_{j}(T))\right| 
 ≤ m δ ​ ω ℋ , δ ​ ( η n , δ ) \displaystyle\leq m_{\delta}\omega_{\mathcal{H},\delta}(\eta_{n,\delta}) 
 
 
 (8.4) 
 
 
 + M ⁡ ( b δ ​ ( T n ) p + b δ ​ ( T ) p ) . \displaystyle\quad+M\bigl(b_{\delta}(T_{n})^{p}+b_{\delta}(T)^{p}\bigr). 
 
 
 Let  n → ∞ n\to\infty , then  δ ↓ 0 \delta\downarrow 0  and use
( 8.2 ). A singleton family gives the first assertion.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Corollary 8.3 . 
 
 For every  T = A + i ​ B ∈ 𝒮 p T=A+iB\in\mathcal{S}_{p}  and every superharmonic majorant  U ∈ 𝒰 p U\in\mathcal{U}_{p}  with  U ≥ F U\geq F , 
 

 
 (8.5) 
 
 ℱ p ​ ( A , B ) ≤ ∑ k U ⁡ ( λ k ​ ( T ) ) , \mathcal{F}_{p}(A,B)\leq\sum_{k}U(\lambda_{k}(T)), 
 
 
 with an absolutely convergent series. When  F F  satisfies
Theorem  1.3 , one may take its least majorant
 U ∗ U_{*} ; it is pointwise optimal already among finite-matrix spectral
corrections by ( 1.8 ). In particular
 ℱ p ​ ( A , B ) ≤ 0 \mathcal{F}_{p}(A,B)\leq 0  if  T T  is quasinilpotent. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Choose finite-rank orthogonal projections  P N P_{N}  converging strongly
to  I I  on a separable reducing subspace containing the ranges of
 T T  and  T ∗ T^{*} . Finite-rank approximation gives
 P N ​ T ​ P N → T P_{N}TP_{N}\to T  in  𝒮 p \mathcal{S}_{p} . Apply Corollary  2.6  to
these compressions and Corollary  8.2  to  U U .
The Hermitian pencils converge in  𝒮 p \mathcal{S}_{p}  as well.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Schatten  p p -norm convergence is essential: viewed on  ℓ 2 \ell^{2} 
by zero extension,  T N = N − 1 / p I N T_{N}=N^{-1/p}I_{N}  tends to zero
in operator norm and has bounded  𝒮 p \mathcal{S}_{p}  norm, but its eigenvalue
 p p -mass stays one. 
 
 
 Let  U p , a , b U_{p,a,b}  be the least majorant of
 F p , a , b ​ ( x , y ) = | a ​ x + b ​ y | p − K p ​ ( a , b ) p ​ | x | p F_{p,a,b}(x,y)=|ax+by|^{p}-K_{p}(a,b)^{p}|x|^{p} , supplied by the
classification and Corollary  1.5 .
Corollary  2.6  and Corollary  8.3 
give, for finite matrices and compact  T ∈ 𝒮 p T\in\mathcal{S}_{p}  respectively, 
 

 
 (8.6) 
 
 tr ⁡ | a ​ A + b ​ B | p − K p ​ ( a , b ) p ​ tr ⁡ | A | p ≤ ∑ k U p , a , b ​ ( λ k ​ ( T ) ) . \operatorname{tr}|aA+bB|^{p}-K_{p}(a,b)^{p}\operatorname{tr}|A|^{p}\leq\sum_{k}U_{p,a,b}(\lambda_{k}(T)). 
 
 
 For finite matrices the exact difference is ( 2.23 )
with  F = F p , a , b F=F_{p,a,b} . For quasinilpotent  T T  the sum vanishes, proving
the spectral form of Corollary  1.6 . 
 
 
 The classical coordinate superharmonic majorant  U p U_{p}  recorded in
Appendix  A  gives the more symmetric specialization 
 

 
 (8.7) 
 
 tr ⁡ | A | p − C p p ​ tr ⁡ | B | p ≤ ∑ k U p ​ ( λ k ​ ( T ) ) . \operatorname{tr}|A|^{p}-C_{p}^{p}\operatorname{tr}|B|^{p}\leq\sum_{k}U_{p}(\lambda_{k}(T)). 
 
 
 The scalar majorant is verified in Appendix  A ;
its matrix and compact-operator consequences follow as in
Section  2.5 . For nilpotent matrices the
spectral sum vanishes, whereas the two nonnegative integrals in
( 2.23 ) need not vanish. 
 
 
 Proof of Corollary  1.6 . 
 
 The classical qualitative theorem of Matsaev  [ 21 ]  gives
Schatten membership of the other self-adjoint part from either one;
see also  [ 23 , Chapter III]  and  [ 24 ] .
The optimal-majorant assertion of Theorem  1.4 , with
one output and Corollary  1.5 , supplies a majorant of
 | a ​ x + b ​ y | p − K p ​ ( a , b ) p ​ | x | p |ax+by|^{p}-K_{p}(a,b)^{p}|x|^{p}  that vanishes at zero.
Corollary  8.3  applied to  T ∈ 𝒮 p T\in\mathcal{S}_{p} 
therefore gives ( 1.23 ). For the second formulation
apply the first one to  i ​ T = − B + i ​ A iT=-B+iA  and use
 K p ​ ( 0 , 1 ) = C p K_{p}(0,1)=C_{p} . Sharpness follows from the finite-nilpotent supremum
in Corollary  1.5 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Proof of Corollary  1.7 . 
 
 For  K ≥ C p K\geq C_{p} , Proposition  A.1  gives the admissible
majorant  U p , K ​ ( x , y ) = U p ​ ( x , ( K / C p ) ​ y ) U_{p,K}(x,y)=U_{p}(x,(K/C_{p})y)  of  | x | p − K p ​ | y | p |x|^{p}-K^{p}|y|^{p} , nonpositive
on  Σ p , K \Sigma_{p,K} . If  L Ω < ∞ L_{\Omega}<\infty , take  K = L Ω K=L_{\Omega}  in
Corollary  8.3 ; its absolutely convergent
spectral sum is nonpositive, proving ( 1.26 ).
Scalar matrices give the lower bound  sup Ω r \sup_{\Omega}r .
For the other lower bound, fix  z 0 ∈ Ω z_{0}\in\Omega  and a nonzero finite
nilpotent  N N . The matrices  z 0 ​ I + t ​ N z_{0}I+tN  have spectrum  { z 0 } \{z_{0}\} , and
as  t → ∞ t\to\infty  their real-to-imaginary norm ratios tend to
 ‖ Re ⁡ N ‖ p / ‖ Im ⁡ N ‖ p \|\operatorname{Re}N\|_{p}/\|\operatorname{Im}N\|_{p} . Corollary  2.7 ,
applied also after rotation by  i i , makes these limits arbitrarily
close to  C p C_{p} . This proves ( 1.25 ), including
the absence of a finite bound when the scalar supremum is infinite.
The stated spectral-set criterion follows immediately.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 8.2.  Real diagonal perturbations 

 
 Corollary 8.4 . 
 
 For every  κ ∈ ℝ \kappa\in\mathbb{R} , 
 

 
 (8.8) 
 
 sup ℰ ‖ H ℰ + κ ​ D ℰ ‖ = sup ℰ ‖ H ℰ + κ ​ D ℰ ‖ cb = max ⁡ { C p , | κ | } . \sup_{\mathcal{E}}\|H_{\mathcal{E}}+\kappa D_{\mathcal{E}}\|=\sup_{\mathcal{E}}\|H_{\mathcal{E}}+\kappa D_{\mathcal{E}}\|_{\rm cb}=\max\{C_{p},|\kappa|\}. 
 
 
 On  𝒮 p ​ ( ℓ 2 ) \mathcal{S}_{p}(\ell^{2}) , the multiplier  ℋ 0 + κ ​ D \mathscr{H}_{0}+\kappa D  has the same
operator norm and completely bounded norm, where  D D  is the
diagonal conditional expectation. More generally, for a finite
flag and real  κ j \kappa_{j} , 
 

 
 (8.9) 
 
 ‖ H ℰ ​ X + ∑ j κ j ​ E j ​ X ​ E j ‖ p ≤ max ⁡ { C p , max j ⁡ | κ j | } ​ ‖ X ‖ p , \left\|H_{\mathcal{E}}X+\sum_{j}\kappa_{j}E_{j}XE_{j}\right\|_{p}\leq\max\{C_{p},\max_{j}|\kappa_{j}|\}\,\|X\|_{p}, 
 
 
 with the same bound at every amplification level.
For every bounded real sequence  𝛋 = ( κ j ) \boldsymbol{\kappa}=(\kappa_{j}) ,
define  D 𝛋 ​ X = ∑ j κ j ​ e j ​ j ​ X ​ e j ​ j D_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}X=\sum_{j}\kappa_{j}e_{jj}Xe_{jj}  on
 𝒮 p ​ ( ℓ 2 ​ ( ℕ ) ) \mathcal{S}_{p}(\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})) . Then 
 

 
 (8.10) 
 
 ‖ ℋ 0 + D 𝜿 ‖ = ‖ ℋ 0 + D 𝜿 ‖ cb = max ⁡ { C p , ‖ 𝜿 ‖ ∞ } . \|\mathscr{H}_{0}+D_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\|=\|\mathscr{H}_{0}+D_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\|_{\rm cb}=\max\{C_{p},\|\boldsymbol{\kappa}\|_{\infty}\}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Put  K = max ⁡ { C p , max j ⁡ | κ j | } K=\max\{C_{p},\max_{j}|\kappa_{j}|\} . For  X = X ∗ X=X^{*} , set
 Y = H ℰ ​ X + ∑ j κ j ​ E j ​ X ​ E j Y=H_{\mathcal{E}}X+\sum_{j}\kappa_{j}E_{j}XE_{j} .
The block upper-triangular matrix  i ⁡ ( X + i ​ Y ) = − Y + i ​ X i(X+iY)=-Y+iX  has diagonal
blocks  ( − κ j + i ) ​ E j ​ X ​ E j (-\kappa_{j}+i)E_{j}XE_{j} , so its spectrum lies in  Σ p , K \Sigma_{p,K} .
Corollary  1.7  gives  ‖ Y ‖ p ≤ K ​ ‖ X ‖ p \|Y\|_{p}\leq K\|X\|_{p} .
Hermitian dilation gives the same bound for arbitrary  X X , since
all  κ j \kappa_{j}  are real. Applying the argument to  ( I d ⊗ E j ) (I_{d}\otimes E_{j}) 
proves ( 8.9 ) at every amplification level. 
 
 
 For a bounded sequence  𝜿 \boldsymbol{\kappa} , the diagonal series
converges in  𝒮 p \mathcal{S}_{p}  and contractivity of  D D  gives
 ‖ D 𝜿 ​ X ‖ p ≤ ‖ 𝜿 ‖ ∞ ​ ‖ X ‖ p \|D_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}X\|_{p}\leq\|\boldsymbol{\kappa}\|_{\infty}\|X\|_{p} .
Finite principal sections and density therefore give the upper
bound in ( 8.10 ) at every amplification level.
The tests  X = e j ​ j X=e_{jj}  give the lower bound  ‖ 𝜿 ‖ ∞ \|\boldsymbol{\kappa}\|_{\infty} .
The zero-diagonal matrices  X = Re ⁡ q ⁡ ( S n ) X=\operatorname{Re}q(S_{n})  from
Corollary  2.7  give the lower bound  C p C_{p} .
These prove the infinite operator norm, and
( 4.11 ) gives the same complete norm.
For constant  κ \kappa , the same two tests over finite flags prove
( 8.8 ); the infinite assertion is the constant-sequence
case of ( 8.10 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 This real diagonal perturbation preserves self-adjointness. The signed conventions
 S 0 ± D S_{0}\pm D  from Lemma  4.8  instead become
 H ℰ ± i ​ D ℰ H_{\mathcal{E}}\pm iD_{\mathcal{E}}  after multiplication by  i i ;
their normalization comparison is a separate assertion. 
 
 
 
 8.3.  Fourth-power moment geometry 

 
 Theorem 8.5 . 
 
 For every nonzero finite nilpotent  N = A + i ​ B N=A+iB , its directional
fourth moments have the form 
 

 
 (8.11) 
 
 tr ⁡ | A ​ cos ⁡ θ + B ​ sin ⁡ θ | 4 = M ⁡ ( 1 + u ​ cos ⁡ 2 ​ θ + v ​ sin ⁡ 2 ​ θ ) , M > 0 . \operatorname{tr}|A\cos\theta+B\sin\theta|^{4}=M(1+u\cos 2\theta+v\sin 2\theta),\qquad M>0. 
 
 
 With  ρ = 2 ​ 2 / 3 \rho=2\sqrt{2}/3 , the closure of all these normalized moment
pairs is precisely  { ( u , v ) : u 2 + v 2 ≤ ρ 2 } \{(u,v):u^{2}+v^{2}\leq\rho^{2}\} .
For arbitrary real  a , b a,b , homogeneity gives 
 

 
 (8.12) 
 
 1 M ​ tr ⁡ | a ​ A + b ​ B | 4 = ( a 2 + b 2 ) 2 + ( a 2 + b 2 ) ​ [ ( a 2 − b 2 ) ​ u + 2 ​ a ​ b ​ v ] . \frac{1}{M}\operatorname{tr}|aA+bB|^{4}=(a^{2}+b^{2})^{2}+(a^{2}+b^{2})[(a^{2}-b^{2})u+2abv]. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 For nonzero nilpotent  N = A + i ​ B N=A+iB , write
 E N ​ ( θ ) = tr ⁡ | A ​ cos ⁡ θ + B ​ sin ⁡ θ | 4 E_{N}(\theta)=\operatorname{tr}|A\cos\theta+B\sin\theta|^{4} .
Expanding via  A ​ cos ⁡ θ + B ​ sin ⁡ θ = ( e − i ​ θ ​ N + e i ​ θ ​ N ∗ ) / 2 A\cos\theta+B\sin\theta=(e^{-i\theta}N+e^{i\theta}N^{*})/2 , the fourth harmonics vanish
because  tr ⁡ N 4 = 0 \operatorname{tr}N^{4}=0 . Thus, for some  M > 0 M>0 , 
 

 
 
 E N ​ ( θ ) = M ⁡ ( 1 + u ​ cos ⁡ 2 ​ θ + v ​ sin ⁡ 2 ​ θ ) . E_{N}(\theta)=M(1+u\cos 2\theta+v\sin 2\theta). 
 
 
 Every directional part  A ​ cos ⁡ θ + B ​ sin ⁡ θ A\cos\theta+B\sin\theta  is nonzero:
otherwise a rotation of  N N  would be skew-Hermitian and nilpotent,
hence zero. Thus  r = ( u 2 + v 2 ) 1 / 2 < 1 r=(u^{2}+v^{2})^{1/2}<1 .
Apply the sharp coordinate inequality to every rotation of  N N .
The maximum ratio of orthogonal directional fourth moments is
 ( 1 + r ) / ( 1 − r ) (1+r)/(1-r) , whence 
 

 
 
 r ≤ C 4 4 − 1 C 4 4 + 1 = 2 ​ 2 3 = : ρ . r\leq\frac{C_{4}^{4}-1}{C_{4}^{4}+1}=\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{3}=:\rho. 
 
 
 Conversely sharpness of  C 4 = 1 + 2 C_{4}=1+\sqrt{2}  among finite nilpotents
gives a sequence of moment pairs  ( u , v ) (u,v)  approaching the boundary
circle.
Rotating the nilpotents realizes every boundary direction in the
closure. Direct sums, with positive fourth-power rescaling, take
convex combinations of these moment pairs. Their closure
is therefore exactly the disk  u 2 + v 2 ≤ ρ 2 u^{2}+v^{2}\leq\rho^{2} . 
 
 
 ∎ 
 
 
 
 
 8.4.  A complete fourth-power criterion 

 
 The moment disk turns the signed classification at  p = 4 p=4  into an
explicit second-order cone condition. 
 
 
 Corollary 8.6 . 
 
 For finitely many real  c j , a j , b j c_{j},a_{j},b_{j} , put 
 

 
 
 Γ 0 \displaystyle\Gamma_{0} 
 = ∑ j c j ​ ( a j 2 + b j 2 ) 2 , \displaystyle=\sum_{j}c_{j}(a_{j}^{2}+b_{j}^{2})^{2}, 
 
 
 
 Γ 1 \displaystyle\Gamma_{1} 
 = ∑ j c j ​ ( a j 2 + b j 2 ) ​ ( a j 2 − b j 2 ) , \displaystyle=\sum_{j}c_{j}(a_{j}^{2}+b_{j}^{2})(a_{j}^{2}-b_{j}^{2}), 
 
 
 (8.13) 
 
 Γ 2 \displaystyle\Gamma_{2} 
 = 2 ​ ∑ j c j ​ a j ​ b j ​ ( a j 2 + b j 2 ) . \displaystyle=2\sum_{j}c_{j}a_{j}b_{j}(a_{j}^{2}+b_{j}^{2}). 
 
 
 Then 
 

 
 (8.14) 
 
 ∑ j c j ​ tr ⁡ | a j ​ A + b j ​ B | 4 ≤ 0 for every finite nilpotent  ​ A + i ​ B \sum_{j}c_{j}\operatorname{tr}|a_{j}A+b_{j}B|^{4}\leq 0\quad\text{for every finite nilpotent }A+iB 
 
 
 if and only if 
 

 
 (8.15) 
 
 Γ 0 + 2 ​ 2 3 ​ Γ 1 2 + Γ 2 2 ≤ 0 . \Gamma_{0}+\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{3}\sqrt{\Gamma_{1}^{2}+\Gamma_{2}^{2}}\leq 0. 
 
 
 Equivalently, the coefficient triple belongs to a scaled negative
Lorentz cone. This criterion also characterizes the existence of
an admissible superharmonic majorant of
 ∑ j c j ​ | a j ​ x + b j ​ y | 4 \sum_{j}c_{j}|a_{j}x+b_{j}y|^{4} , and the inequality then holds for every
compact quasinilpotent  A + i ​ B ∈ 𝒮 4 A+iB\in\mathcal{S}_{4} . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 For  N = A + i ​ B ≠ 0 N=A+iB\neq 0 , use the normalization ( 8.11 ).
Theorem  8.5  gives the closed moment disk
 u 2 + v 2 ≤ 8 / 9 u^{2}+v^{2}\leq 8/9 . Summing the
directional fourth moments with their signed coefficients gives 
 

 
 
 1 M ​ ∑ j c j ​ tr ⁡ | a j ​ A + b j ​ B | 4 = Γ 0 + Γ 1 ​ u + Γ 2 ​ v . \frac{1}{M}\sum_{j}c_{j}\operatorname{tr}|a_{j}A+b_{j}B|^{4}=\Gamma_{0}+\Gamma_{1}u+\Gamma_{2}v. 
 
 
 The supremum over the closed disk is the left side of
( 8.15 ). If it is positive, a nearby realized
moment pair gives a finite nilpotent counterexample; if it is
nonpositive, every pair satisfies ( 8.14 ).
The zero matrix is immediate. Theorem  1.3 
and Corollary  8.3  give the majorant and
compact-operator assertions.
∎ 
 
 
 
 The coordinate value  C 4 C_{4}  used in this derivation is classical
 [ 18 , Theorem 6.2] . The corollary makes the entire signed
fourth-power classification explicit; it requires no additional
estimate beyond the moment disk. 
 
 
 Corollary 8.7 . 
 
 Let  P , Q P,Q  be finite signed fourth-power ridge sums, with coefficient
triples  ( Γ 0 , Γ 1 , Γ 2 ) (\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2})  and  ( Q 0 , Q 1 , Q 2 ) (Q_{0},Q_{1},Q_{2})  as in
( 8.13 ). Assume
 Q 0 > ρ ​ Q 1 2 + Q 2 2 Q_{0}>\rho\sqrt{Q_{1}^{2}+Q_{2}^{2}} , and put 
 

 
 
 a \displaystyle a 
 = Q 0 2 − ρ 2 ​ ( Q 1 2 + Q 2 2 ) , \displaystyle=Q_{0}^{2}-\rho^{2}(Q_{1}^{2}+Q_{2}^{2}), 
 
 
 
 b \displaystyle b 
 = Q 0 ​ Γ 0 − ρ 2 ​ ( Q 1 ​ Γ 1 + Q 2 ​ Γ 2 ) , \displaystyle=Q_{0}\Gamma_{0}-\rho^{2}(Q_{1}\Gamma_{1}+Q_{2}\Gamma_{2}), 
 
 
 
 c \displaystyle c 
 = Γ 0 2 − ρ 2 ​ ( Γ 1 2 + Γ 2 2 ) . \displaystyle=\Gamma_{0}^{2}-\rho^{2}(\Gamma_{1}^{2}+\Gamma_{2}^{2}). 
 
 
 The optimal real coefficient in
 𝒫 4 ​ ( A , B ) ≤ λ ​ 𝒬 4 ​ ( A , B ) \mathcal{P}_{4}(A,B)\leq\lambda\mathcal{Q}_{4}(A,B)  over finite nilpotents is 
 

 
 (8.16) 
 
 λ ∗ = b + b 2 − a ​ c a . \lambda_{*}=\frac{b+\sqrt{b^{2}-ac}}{a}. 
 
 
 Equivalently, it is the least real number satisfying 
 

 
 (8.17) 
 
 λ ​ Q 0 − Γ 0 ≥ ρ ​ ( λ ​ Q 1 − Γ 1 ) 2 + ( λ ​ Q 2 − Γ 2 ) 2 . \lambda Q_{0}-\Gamma_{0}\geq\rho\sqrt{(\lambda Q_{1}-\Gamma_{1})^{2}+(\lambda Q_{2}-\Gamma_{2})^{2}}. 
 
 
 The same coefficient bounds every compact quasinilpotent
 A + i ​ B ∈ 𝒮 4 A+iB\in\mathcal{S}_{4} . A nonzero  Q Q  with nonnegative ridge coefficients
always satisfies the stated hypothesis. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The hypothesis is exactly positivity of the denominator on the
closed moment disk. Corollary  8.6 , applied to
 P − λ ​ Q P-\lambda Q , gives ( 8.17 ); its left side minus
its right side is strictly increasing from  − ∞ -\infty  to  + ∞ +\infty .
Squaring the boundary equation gives  a ​ λ 2 − 2 ​ b ​ λ + c = 0 a\lambda^{2}-2b\lambda+c=0 .
At  λ 0 = Γ 0 / Q 0 \lambda_{0}=\Gamma_{0}/Q_{0}  this quadratic is nonpositive, so
 b 2 − a ​ c ≥ 0 b^{2}-ac\geq 0  and its larger root is at least  λ 0 \lambda_{0} .
That root therefore satisfies the unsquared equation and is its
unique solution. In the proportional case the discriminant vanishes
and the formula gives  λ ∗ = λ 0 \lambda_{*}=\lambda_{0}  directly.
The compact assertion follows from Corollary  8.6 ;
nonnegative ridge coefficients give  Q 0 > 0 Q_{0}>0  and
 Q 1 2 + Q 2 2 ≤ Q 0 \sqrt{Q_{1}^{2}+Q_{2}^{2}}\leq Q_{0} , proving the final assertion.
∎ 
 
 
 
 For example,  Q ⁡ ( x , y ) = x 4 + y 4 − ( x + y ) 4 / 5 Q(x,y)=x^{4}+y^{4}-(x+y)^{4}/5  has coefficient triple
 ( 6 / 5 , 0 , − 4 / 5 ) (6/5,0,-4/5)  and satisfies the denominator condition.
Its trace is strictly positive on every nonzero finite nilpotent,
although  Q ( 1 , 1 ) = − 6 / 5 Q(1,1)=-6/5 . 
 
 
 
 8.5.  The full joint formula at  p = 4 p=4 

 
 Corollary 8.8 . 
 
 For the real pairs and weights in Theorem  1.4 , put 
 

 
 
 A 0 = ∑ j w j ​ a j 2 ​ ( a j 2 + b j 2 ) , B 0 = ∑ j w j ​ b j 2 ​ ( a j 2 + b j 2 ) , C 0 = ∑ j w j ​ a j ​ b j ​ ( a j 2 + b j 2 ) . A_{0}=\sum_{j}w_{j}a_{j}^{2}(a_{j}^{2}+b_{j}^{2}),\quad B_{0}=\sum_{j}w_{j}b_{j}^{2}(a_{j}^{2}+b_{j}^{2}),\quad C_{0}=\sum_{j}w_{j}a_{j}b_{j}(a_{j}^{2}+b_{j}^{2}). 
 
 
 Then every joint model in that theorem at  p = 4 p=4  has norm 
 

 
 (8.18) 
 
 Λ 4 ​ ( P ) 4 = A 0 + 17 ​ B 0 + 4 ​ 2 ​ 9 ​ B 0 2 + C 0 2 . \Lambda_{4}(P)^{4}=A_{0}+17B_{0}+4\sqrt{2}\sqrt{9B_{0}^{2}+C_{0}^{2}}. 
 
 
 Moreover 
 

 
 (8.19) 
 
 Λ 4 ​ ( P ) 4 = ∑ j w j ​ K 4 ​ ( a j , b j ) 4 \Lambda_{4}(P)^{4}=\sum_{j}w_{j}K_{4}(a_{j},b_{j})^{4}
