 if and only if all indices with  w j > 0 w_{j}>0  and  b j ≠ 0 b_{j}\neq 0  have the
same ratio  a j / b j a_{j}/b_{j} . Zero weights and pure identity outputs impose
no condition; the condition is vacuous with at most one such index. 
 
 
 For shifted pairs, let  W = ∑ j w j W=\sum_{j}w_{j} ,  Z = ∑ j w j ​ e 2 ​ π ​ i ​ α j Z=\sum_{j}w_{j}e^{2\pi i\alpha_{j}} ,
and  D = 9 ​ W − 8 ​ Re ⁡ Z D=9W-8\operatorname{Re}Z . Then 
 

 
 (8.20) 
 
 Λ 4 ​ ( P ) 4 = D + D 2 − 9 ​ W 2 + 8 ​ | Z | 2 . \Lambda_{4}(P)^{4}=D+\sqrt{D^{2}-9W^{2}+8|Z|^{2}}. 
 
 
 In particular, two distinct shifts in  ( 0 , 1 ) (0,1)  with positive weights
give strict improvement over the sum of the separate fourth powers.
For one output this specializes the classical scalar formula
 [ 9 ]  to 
 

 
 (8.21) 
 
 K 4 ​ ( a , b ) 2 = K 4 / 3 ​ ( a , b ) 2 = a 2 + b 2 ​ ( a 2 + 9 ​ b 2 + 2 ​ 2 ​ | b | ) . K_{4}(a,b)^{2}=K_{4/3}(a,b)^{2}=\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}\left(\sqrt{a^{2}+9b^{2}}+2\sqrt{2}\,|b|\right). 
 
 
 In particular, at  p = 4 p=4  and  p = 4 / 3 p=4/3  on both  ℓ p ​ ( ℤ ) \ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z})  and  ℓ p ​ ( ℤ , 𝒮 p ) \ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z};\mathcal{S}_{p}) , 
 

 
 (8.22) 
 
 ‖ R α ‖ 2 = 1 + 8 ​ sin 2 ⁡ ( π ​ α ) + 2 ​ 2 ​ sin ⁡ ( π ​ α ) . \|R_{\alpha}\|^{2}=\sqrt{1+8\sin^{2}(\pi\alpha)}+2\sqrt{2}\sin(\pi\alpha). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Apply Corollary  8.7  with  Q ⁡ ( x , y ) = | x | 4 Q(x,y)=|x|^{4} .
Then  ( Q 0 , Q 1 , Q 2 ) = ( 1 , 1 , 0 ) (Q_{0},Q_{1},Q_{2})=(1,1,0)  and
 ( Γ 0 , Γ 1 , Γ 2 ) = ( A 0 + B 0 , A 0 − B 0 , 2 ​ C 0 ) (\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2})=(A_{0}+B_{0},A_{0}-B_{0},2C_{0}) , giving 
 

 
 
 a = 1 9 , b = A 0 + 17 ​ B 0 9 , b 2 − a ​ c = 32 81 ​ ( 9 ​ B 0 2 + C 0 2 ) . a=\frac{1}{9},\qquad b=\frac{A_{0}+17B_{0}}{9},\qquad b^{2}-ac=\frac{32}{81}(9B_{0}^{2}+C_{0}^{2}). 
 
 
 Formula ( 8.16 ) gives ( 8.18 ),
including  B 0 = 0 B_{0}=0 , and Theorem  1.4  transfers it to
every joint operator model. 
 
 
 For the equality criterion put
 B j = w j ​ b j 2 ​ ( a j 2 + b j 2 ) B_{j}=w_{j}b_{j}^{2}(a_{j}^{2}+b_{j}^{2})  and
 C j = w j ​ a j ​ b j ​ ( a j 2 + b j 2 ) C_{j}=w_{j}a_{j}b_{j}(a_{j}^{2}+b_{j}^{2}) .
Applying the single-output case of the formula yields the exact gap 
 

 
 (8.23) 
 
 ∑ j w j ​ K 4 ​ ( a j , b j ) 4 − Λ 4 ​ ( P ) 4 = 4 ​ 2 ​ ( ∑ j ‖ ( 3 ​ B j , C j ) ‖ 2 − ‖ ∑ j ( 3 ​ B j , C j ) ‖ 2 ) . \sum_{j}w_{j}K_{4}(a_{j},b_{j})^{4}-\Lambda_{4}(P)^{4}=4\sqrt{2}\left(\sum_{j}\|(3B_{j},C_{j})\|_{2}-\Bigl\|\sum_{j}(3B_{j},C_{j})\Bigr\|_{2}\right). 
 
 
 Every nonzero vector has first coordinate positive. Equality in
the Euclidean triangle inequality holds exactly when these vectors
are positively collinear, equivalently when their ratios
 C j / B j = a j / b j C_{j}/B_{j}=a_{j}/b_{j}  agree. For shifted pairs,
 A 0 = ( W + Re ⁡ Z ) / 2 A_{0}=(W+\operatorname{Re}Z)/2 ,  B 0 = ( W − Re ⁡ Z ) / 2 B_{0}=(W-\operatorname{Re}Z)/2 ,  C 0 = Im ⁡ Z / 2 C_{0}=\operatorname{Im}Z/2 ,
which proves ( 8.20 ). Finally  cot ⁡ ( π ​ α ) \cot(\pi\alpha) 
is strictly decreasing on  ( 0 , 1 ) (0,1) , proving the strictness assertion.
The single-pencil formula factors as ( 8.21 ); scalar
duality gives the  4 / 3 4/3  value, and substitution gives
( 8.22 ). This duality argument applies to a single
pencil. For several pencils, the adjoint maps an  ℓ p ′ \ell^{p^{\prime}} 
direct sum into a single space, so it does not give the same joint
norm identity at  p ′ p^{\prime} .
∎ 
 
 
 
 For example, two unit-weight shifts  α 1 = 1 / 4 \alpha_{1}=1/4 ,  α 2 = 3 / 4 \alpha_{2}=3/4 
give 
 

 
 
 Λ 4 ​ ( P ) 4 = 18 + 12 ​ 2 < 18 + 8 ​ 5 = ‖ R 1 / 4 ‖ 4 + ‖ R 3 / 4 ‖ 4 . \Lambda_{4}(P)^{4}=18+12\sqrt{2}<18+8\sqrt{5}=\|R_{1/4}\|^{4}+\|R_{3/4}\|^{4}. 
 
 
 The compatibility condition concerns common approximate extremizers.
Indeed, a sequence saturating the joint sum must saturate every
positive-weight summand; conversely proportional nonidentity pencils
share a single-pencil extremizing sequence. 
 
 
 Comparisons of arbitrary real directions and products of joint
families acting in independent coordinates are recorded in
Appendix  E . 
 
 
 Remark 8.9 . 
 
 The planar majorant applies to real linear combinations  a ​ A + b ​ B aA+bB 
of self-adjoint operators. The norm formula does not extend to
arbitrary complex coefficients  a , b a,b .
For example, on  𝒮 2 \mathcal{S}_{2}  let  X = e 12 X=e_{12} . Then
 H ℰ ​ X = − i ​ X H_{\mathcal{E}}X=-iX , so
 ‖ ( I + i ​ H ℰ ) ​ X ‖ 2 = 2 ​ ‖ X ‖ 2 \|(I+iH_{\mathcal{E}})X\|_{2}=2\|X\|_{2} .
Substituting  ( a , b ) = ( 1 , i ) (a,b)=(1,i)  in the absolute-value angular
ratio in ( 1.18 ) instead gives  2 \sqrt{2}  at  p = 2 p=2 .
This explicit failure rules out that complex extension.
In particular, the sharp real-combination results, including the
completely bounded equalities of Corollary  1.5 , do not
establish a sharp Riesz or triangular projection bound. 
 
 
 
 
 8.6.  Riesz projections and matrix amplification 

 
 We apply Theorem  1.1  to matrix and Fourier realizations.
The amplification bound is used at every matrix level before it is
converted to a canonical completely bounded estimate. 
 
 
 Corollary 8.10 . 
 
 For every algebra in Theorem  1.1 , every  1 < p < ∞ 1<p<\infty , and every  n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 , 
 

 
 
 ‖ Id M n ⊗ P ‖ L p ​ ( M n ​ ⊗ ¯ ​ ℳ ) → L p ​ ( M n ​ ⊗ ¯ ​ ℳ ) , ‖ Id M n ⊗ P 0 ‖ L p ​ ( M n ​ ⊗ ¯ ​ ℳ ) → L p ​ ( M n ​ ⊗ ¯ ​ ℳ ) ≤ csc ⁡ ( π / p ) . \|\operatorname{Id}_{M_{n}}\otimes P\|_{L^{p}(M_{n}\overline{\otimes}\mathcal{M})\to L^{p}(M_{n}\overline{\otimes}\mathcal{M})},\quad\|\operatorname{Id}_{M_{n}}\otimes P_{0}\|_{L^{p}(M_{n}\overline{\otimes}\mathcal{M})\to L^{p}(M_{n}\overline{\otimes}\mathcal{M})}\leq\csc(\pi/p). 
 
 
 For the canonical operator space structure on  L p ​ ( ℳ ) L^{p}(\mathcal{M}) , also 
 

 
 
 ‖ P ‖ cb , ‖ P 0 ‖ cb ≤ csc ⁡ ( π / p ) . \|P\|_{\mathrm{cb}},\ \|P_{0}\|_{\mathrm{cb}}\leq\csc(\pi/p). 
 
 
 These are uniform upper bounds; a particular subdiagonal algebra need not attain them. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The algebra  M n ​ ( ℬ ) M_{n}(\mathcal{B})  is subdiagonal in  M n ​ ( ℳ ) M_{n}(\mathcal{M}) , with diagonal  M n ​ ( 𝒟 ) M_{n}(\mathcal{D})  and expectation  Id M n ⊗ Φ \operatorname{Id}_{M_{n}}\otimes\Phi . Multiplicativity, trace preservation, and weak-star density hold entrywise; Theorem  C.1 (3) gives maximality. Its retained and strict projections are the stated amplifications, so Theorem  1.1  proves the tracial bounds. The normalized matrix trace may be replaced by the ordinary trace, since this scales both norms by the same factor. 
 
 
 Lemma  4.6  now converts these uniform tracial amplification bounds to the stated canonical completely bounded bounds. This application uses no additional sharp estimate and keeps the tracial and operator-space matrix norms distinct.
∎ 
 
 
 
 8.6.1.  Triangular truncations 

 
 For  n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 , let  T n ( ( x i ​ j ) ) = ( 1 { i ≤ j } x i ​ j ) T_{n}((x_{ij}))=(1_{\{i\leq j\}}x_{ij})  be retained-diagonal upper triangular truncation;  S n p S_{n}^{p}  denotes the Schatten  p p -class on  ℂ n \mathbb{C}^{n} . 
 
 
 Corollary 8.11 . 
 
 Let  1 < p < ∞ 1<p<\infty  and let  ( ℳ , τ ) (\mathcal{M},\tau)  be any finite von Neumann algebra
with a faithful normal tracial state. Then 
 

 
 (8.24) 
 
 sup n ≥ 1 ∥ T n : S n p ⟶ S n p ∥ = csc ⁡ ( π / p ) , sup n ≥ 1 ∥ T n ⊗ Id ℳ : L p ( M n ⊗ ¯ ℳ ) ⟶ L p ( M n ⊗ ¯ ℳ ) ∥ = csc ⁡ ( π / p ) . \begin{split}\sup_{n\geq 1}\|T_{n}:S_{n}^{p}\longrightarrow S_{n}^{p}\|&=\csc(\pi/p),\\
\sup_{n\geq 1}\|T_{n}\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{M}}:L^{p}(M_{n}\,\overline{\otimes}\,\mathcal{M})\longrightarrow L^{p}(M_{n}\,\overline{\otimes}\,\mathcal{M})\|&=\csc(\pi/p).\end{split} 
 
 
 The tensor-product trace is  Tr n / n ⊗ τ \operatorname{Tr}_{n}/n\otimes\tau .
These equalities concern the supremum over matrix sizes; equality
is not asserted at a fixed size. 
 
 
 
 Proof of Corollary  8.11 . 
 
 The upper-triangular algebra over  ℳ \mathcal{M}  is a subdiagonal algebra in
 M n ​ ⊗ ¯ ​ ℳ M_{n}\,\overline{\otimes}\,\mathcal{M} , with diagonal algebra
 ⨁ j = 1 n ℳ \bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{M}  and expectation that deletes the off-diagonal
entries. Its maximality follows from Theorem  C.1 (3),
and its Riesz projection is  T n ⊗ Id ℳ T_{n}\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{M}} .
Theorem  1.1  therefore gives both upper bounds.
The use of normalized rather than ordinary matrix trace does not
change a projection norm. 
 
 
 For the lower bound, we give a finite Toeplitz argument. Let
 f ⁡ ( z ) = ∑ k = − m m a k ​ z k f(z)=\sum_{k=-m}^{m}a_{k}z^{k}  be a scalar trigonometric polynomial,
and, for  n > 2 ​ m n>2m , set 
 

 
 
 A n ​ ( f ) = ( a j − i ) 1 ≤ i , j ≤ n , ‖ A ‖ p , n = ( n − 1 ​ Tr n ​ | A | p ) 1 / p . A_{n}(f)=(a_{j-i})_{1\leq i,j\leq n},\qquad\|A\|_{p,n}=\bigl(n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}_{n}|A|^{p}\bigr)^{1/p}. 
 
 
 If  P + ​ f = ∑ k = 0 m a k ​ z k P_{+}f=\sum_{k=0}^{m}a_{k}z^{k} , then
 T n ​ A n ​ ( f ) = A n ​ ( P + ​ f ) T_{n}A_{n}(f)=A_{n}(P_{+}f) .
Apply the singular-value assertion of Lemma  3.1  with  d = 1 d=1  to  f f  and  P + ​ f P_{+}f . It gives 
 

 
 
 ‖ A n ​ ( f ) ‖ p , n ⟶ ‖ f ‖ p , ‖ T n ​ A n ​ ( f ) ‖ p , n ⟶ ‖ P + ​ f ‖ p . \|A_{n}(f)\|_{p,n}\longrightarrow\|f\|_{p},\qquad\|T_{n}A_{n}(f)\|_{p,n}\longrightarrow\|P_{+}f\|_{p}. 
 
 
 For every nonzero  f f  we therefore have 
 

 
 
 sup n ‖ T n ‖ ≥ ‖ P + ​ f ‖ p ‖ f ‖ p . \sup_{n}\|T_{n}\|\geq\frac{\|P_{+}f\|_{p}}{\|f\|_{p}}. 
 
 
 Trigonometric polynomials are dense in  L p ​ ( 𝕋 ) L^{p}(\mathbb{T}) .
The boundedness and explicit scalar sharpness already proved above
show that the supremum of the last ratio is  csc ⁡ ( π / p ) \csc(\pi/p) .
Finally, the embedding  A ↦ A ⊗ I A\mapsto A\otimes I  is isometric for
the normalized  L p L^{p}  norms and intertwines the two triangular
truncations. It supplies the same lower bound for every fixed
 ( ℳ , τ ) (\mathcal{M},\tau)  and proves ( 8.24 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark 8.12 . 
 
 This corollary is the exact infinite-size norm of triangular
truncation, including finite-algebra coefficients. The
Fourier–Schur transference relation proved by Neuwirth and Ricard
 [ 18 , Proposition 6.3]  places it in the corresponding completely
bounded scalar Fourier-multiplier framework; the proof here does
not require that transference result. 
 
 
 
 
 8.6.2.  Fourier projections with finite-algebra coefficients 

 
 Let  m m  be normalized Haar measure on  𝕋 \mathbb{T} , and let
 𝒩 = L ∞ ​ ( 𝕋 ) ​ ⊗ ¯ ​ ℳ \mathcal{N}=L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})\,\overline{\otimes}\,\mathcal{M} , with trace
 τ ~ = m ⊗ τ \widetilde{\tau}=m\otimes\tau .
For an  ℳ \mathcal{M} -valued trigonometric polynomial define 
 

 
 
 P + ℳ ​ ( ∑ k ∈ ℤ z k ​ x k ) = ∑ k ≥ 0 z k ​ x k . P_{+}^{\mathcal{M}}\left(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}z^{k}x_{k}\right)=\sum_{k\geq 0}z^{k}x_{k}. 
 
 
 Here the sum is finite and the coefficients lie in  ℳ \mathcal{M} ; these polynomials are dense in  L p ​ ( 𝒩 ) L^{p}(\mathcal{N}) . 
 
 
 Corollary 8.13 . 
 
 For every nonzero finite tracial von Neumann algebra  ( ℳ , τ ) (\mathcal{M},\tau)  and
 1 < p < ∞ 1<p<\infty , 
 

 
 
 ‖ P + ℳ ‖ L p ​ ( 𝒩 ) → L p ​ ( 𝒩 ) = csc ⁡ π p . \|P_{+}^{\mathcal{M}}\|_{L^{p}(\mathcal{N})\to L^{p}(\mathcal{N})}=\csc\frac{\pi}{p}. 
 
 
 Equivalently, this is the norm of
 P + ⊗ Id L p ​ ( ℳ ) P_{+}\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{L^{p}(\mathcal{M})}  on the Bochner space
 L p ​ ( 𝕋 , L p ​ ( ℳ ) ) L^{p}(\mathbb{T};L^{p}(\mathcal{M})) .
Moreover, for every  n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 , 
 

 
 (8.25) 
 
 ‖ Id M n ⊗ P + ℳ ‖ L p ​ ( M n ​ ⊗ ¯ ​ 𝒩 ) → L p ​ ( M n ​ ⊗ ¯ ​ 𝒩 ) = csc ⁡ π p . \|\operatorname{Id}_{M_{n}}\otimes P_{+}^{\mathcal{M}}\|_{L^{p}(M_{n}\,\overline{\otimes}\,\mathcal{N})\to L^{p}(M_{n}\,\overline{\otimes}\,\mathcal{N})}=\csc\frac{\pi}{p}. 
 
 
 With the canonical operator space structure on  L p ​ ( 𝒩 ) L^{p}(\mathcal{N}) , 
 

 
 
 ‖ P + ℳ ‖ cb = csc ⁡ π p . \|P_{+}^{\mathcal{M}}\|_{\mathrm{cb}}=\csc\frac{\pi}{p}. 
 
 
 The same equalities hold for the strict Fourier projection
 P > 0 ℳ P_{>0}^{\mathcal{M}} , which retains precisely the positive frequencies. 
 
 
 
 Proof of Corollary  8.13 . 
 
 For  F ∈ 𝒩 F\in\mathcal{N} , its Fourier coefficient
 F ^ ​ ( k ) = ( m ⊗ Id ℳ ) ​ ( z − k ​ F ) \widehat{F}(k)=(m\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{M}})(z^{-k}F)  lies in  ℳ \mathcal{M} .
Consider the weak-star closed algebra 
 

 
 
 𝒜 = { F ∈ 𝒩 : F ^ ​ ( k ) = 0 ​  for  ​ k < 0 } . \mathcal{A}=\{F\in\mathcal{N}:\widehat{F}(k)=0\text{ for }k<0\}. 
 
 
 This is the weak-star closure of the analytic polynomials
 ∑ k ≥ 0 z k ​ x k \sum_{k\geq 0}z^{k}x_{k} .
Indeed, the Fourier coefficient maps are normal, giving one inclusion;
for the other, the Fejér means of an element of  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  are
uniformly bounded analytic polynomials converging ultraweakly to it.
Separate ultraweak continuity of multiplication and these polynomial
approximations show that  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  is a unital algebra and that 
 

 
 
 F ​ G ^ ​ ( 0 ) = F ^ ​ ( 0 ) ​ G ^ ​ ( 0 ) , F , G ∈ 𝒜 . \widehat{FG}(0)=\widehat{F}(0)\widehat{G}(0),\qquad F,G\in\mathcal{A}. 
 
 
 To justify the last identity explicitly, first approximate  G G  while
holding an analytic polynomial  F F  fixed, then approximate  F F ;
for polynomials no two nonnegative frequencies add to zero except
 0 + 0 0+0 .
Fourier uniqueness gives
 𝒜 ∩ 𝒜 ∗ = I ⊗ ℳ \mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{A}^{*}=I\otimes\mathcal{M} .
The map  Ψ ​ F = I ⊗ F ^ ​ ( 0 ) \Psi F=I\otimes\widehat{F}(0)  is a normal
 τ ~ \widetilde{\tau} -preserving conditional expectation onto this diagonal,
and is multiplicative on  𝒜 \mathcal{A} .
Finally,  𝒜 + 𝒜 ∗ \mathcal{A}+\mathcal{A}^{*}  contains all  ℳ \mathcal{M} -valued
trigonometric polynomials, hence is weak-star dense in  𝒩 \mathcal{N} .
Thus  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  is subdiagonal and is maximal by
Theorem  C.1 (3).
Its retained-diagonal projection agrees with  P + ℳ P_{+}^{\mathcal{M}}  on
trigonometric polynomials. Theorem  1.1  gives the upper bound.
On the isometric scalar subspace  f ↦ f ​ I f\mapsto fI  it is the scalar
Riesz projection, whose sharp lower bound was proved above.
Hence its norm is exactly  csc ⁡ ( π / p ) \csc(\pi/p) .
The identity 
 

 
 
 ‖ F ‖ L p ​ ( 𝒩 ) p = ∫ 𝕋 ‖ F ⁡ ( z ) ‖ L p ​ ( ℳ ) p ​ 𝑑 m ​ ( z ) \|F\|_{L^{p}(\mathcal{N})}^{p}=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\|F(z)\|_{L^{p}(\mathcal{M})}^{p}\,dm(z) 
 
 
 first holds for simple functions and extends by completion, yielding
the stated Bochner-space interpretation.
Corollary  8.10 , applied to
 𝒜 ⊂ 𝒩 \mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{N} , gives the upper bound at every
tracial matrix level and for the complete norm. The scalar embedding
 f ↦ f ​ I f\mapsto fI  gives the matching lower bound at each level,
proving ( 8.25 ) and the complete norm equality. Finally,
 P > 0 ℳ = M z ​ P + ℳ ​ M z ¯ P_{>0}^{\mathcal{M}}=M_{z}P_{+}^{\mathcal{M}}M_{\bar{z}} .
Multiplication by  z z  or  z ¯ \bar{z}  is an isometry at every tracial matrix level and a complete isometry by ( 4.13 ). This proves all strict-projection assertions.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 9.  Limitations of the two-point criterion 

 
 The scalar two-term pencil is governed by an angular quotient involving
two points separated by  π / p \pi/p . For a general homogeneous obstacle, consider the necessary
conditions 
 

 
 (9.1) 
 
 f ⁡ ( θ ) + f ⁡ ( θ + π / p ) ≤ 0 for every  ​ θ . f(\theta)+f(\theta+\pi/p)\leq 0\qquad\text{for every }\theta. 
 
 
 These tests are necessary: extend
 sin ⁡ ( p ⁡ ( θ − α ) ) \sin(p(\theta-\alpha))  on  [ α , α + π / p ] [\alpha,\alpha+\pi/p]  by zero
periodically. It is nonnegative and its  ℒ p \mathcal{L}_{p}  is
 p ⁡ ( δ α + δ α + π / p ) p(\delta_{\alpha}+\delta_{\alpha+\pi/p}) ; pairing with a
superharmonic angular majorant proves ( 9.1 ).
They are not sufficient for general finite real ridge sums.
The two-point criterion of  [ 9 , Theorem 3.7]  requires additional
sector hypotheses on the homogeneous obstacle. The example below
shows why such restrictions cannot be dropped in the present class. 
 
 
 Proposition 9.1 . 
 
 Put  p = 8 / 3 p=8/3  and 
 

 
 (9.2) 
 
 F ⁡ ( x + i ​ y ) = ∑ j = 0 7 ( − 1 ) j ​ | x ​ cos ⁡ j ​ π 8 + y ​ sin ⁡ j ​ π 8 | p . F(x+iy)=\sum_{j=0}^{7}(-1)^{j}\left|x\cos\frac{j\pi}{8}+y\sin\frac{j\pi}{8}\right|^{p}. 
 
 
 Its angular restriction  f f  satisfies
 f ⁡ ( θ + 3 ​ π / 8 ) = − f ⁡ ( θ ) f(\theta+3\pi/8)=-f(\theta) , so every test
( 9.1 ) is an equality. Nevertheless  F F  has no
continuous homogeneous superharmonic majorant, and its nilpotent
trace inequality fails. More explicitly, for all sufficiently small
 ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 , the polynomial 
 

 
 
 q ε ​ ( z ) = z − ε ​ z 9 q_{\varepsilon}(z)=z-\varepsilon z^{9} 
 
 
 has  ∫ F ⁡ ( q ε ​ ( e i ​ t ) ) ​ 𝑑 t / ( 2 ​ π ) > 0 \int F(q_{\varepsilon}(e^{it}))\,\mathrm{d}t/(2\pi)>0 .
Thus  N n = S n − ε ​ S n 9 N_{n}=S_{n}-\varepsilon S_{n}^{9}  is a counterexample for all
sufficiently large  n n . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Shifting the eight directions by  3 ​ π / 8 3\pi/8  permutes them with a sign
change, using the  π \pi -periodicity of  | cos | p |\cos|^{p} , and
 ∫ f ​ 𝑑 θ = 0 \int f\,\mathrm{d}\theta=0 . To construct the analytic counterexample, define 
 

 
 
 d k = ∫ 0 2 ​ π | cos ⁡ t | p ​ cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ k ​ t ) ​ d ​ t 2 ​ π . d_{k}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}|\cos t|^{p}\cos(2kt)\frac{\,\mathrm{d}t}{2\pi}. 
 
 
 Integration by parts on  [ − π / 2 , π / 2 ] [-\pi/2,\pi/2]  gives 
 

 
 
 ( p + 2 ​ k ) ​ d k = ( p − 2 ​ k + 2 ) ​ d k − 1 . (p+2k)d_{k}=(p-2k+2)d_{k-1}. 
 
 
 For  p = 8 / 3 p=8/3  this yields  d 4 = d 0 / 364 > 0 d_{4}=d_{0}/364>0 . Therefore 
 

 
 
 ∫ 0 2 ​ π f ⁡ ( t ) ​ cos ⁡ ( 8 ​ t ) ​ d ​ t 2 ​ π = 8 ​ d 4 = 2 ​ d 0 91 > 0 . \int_{0}^{2\pi}f(t)\cos(8t)\frac{\,\mathrm{d}t}{2\pi}=8d_{4}=\frac{2d_{0}}{91}>0. 
 
 
 Since  F F  is  C 1 C^{1}  away from zero, differentiation under the compact
boundary integral is legitimate. At  ε = 0 \varepsilon=0 , the radial
variation of  q ε ​ ( e i ​ t ) q_{\varepsilon}(e^{it})  is  − cos ⁡ 8 ​ t -\cos 8t  and its angular
variation is  − sin ⁡ 8 ​ t -\sin 8t . Hence 
 

 
 
 d d ​ ε | 0 ​ ∫ F ⁡ ( q ε ​ ( e i ​ t ) ) ​ d ​ t 2 ​ π \displaystyle\left.\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}\varepsilon}\right|_{0}\int F(q_{\varepsilon}(e^{it}))\frac{\,\mathrm{d}t}{2\pi} 
 = − ∫ { p f ( t ) cos 8 t + f ′ ( t ) sin 8 t } d ​ t 2 ​ π \displaystyle=-\int\{pf(t)\cos 8t+f^{\prime}(t)\sin 8t\}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}t}{2\pi} 
 
 
 
 
 = ( 8 − p ) ​ ∫ f ⁡ ( t ) ​ cos ⁡ 8 ​ t ​ d ​ t 2 ​ π = 32 ​ d 0 273 > 0 . \displaystyle=(8-p)\int f(t)\cos 8t\frac{\,\mathrm{d}t}{2\pi}=\frac{32d_{0}}{273}>0. 
 
 
 The integral is zero at  ε = 0 \varepsilon=0 , so it is positive for
small positive  ε \varepsilon . A continuous homogeneous superharmonic
majorant  U U  would instead give  ∫ F ⁡ ( q ε ) ​ 𝑑 t / ( 2 ​ π ) ≤ U ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 \int F(q_{\varepsilon})\,\mathrm{d}t/(2\pi)\leq U(0)=0 , a contradiction. Equation ( 3.4 ) supplies
the finite nilpotent counterexamples.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 Appendix A  An explicit superharmonic majorant 

 
 The construction uses the classical method of joining an obstacle
to homogeneous harmonic functions on sectors; see the superharmonic
proof of Essén  [ 15 ]  and the homogeneous-minorant treatment
in  [ 9 , Sections 3–4] . We record an explicit coordinate
majorant, including its nonpositivity on the cone used for real
diagonal perturbations. Put  F p ​ ( x , y ) = | x | p − C p p ​ | y | p F_{p}(x,y)=|x|^{p}-C_{p}^{p}|y|^{p} .
Complex powers below use the branch continuous on the closed right
half-plane, normalized by  1 p = 1 1^{p}=1 , with value zero at the origin.
For  p > 2 p>2 , set  α = π / ( 2 ​ p ) \alpha=\pi/(2p) ,  c = cot ⁡ α = C p c=\cot\alpha=C_{p} ,
 L = cos p − 1 ⁡ α / sin ⁡ α L=\cos^{p-1}\alpha/\sin\alpha , and define 
 

 
 (A.1) 
 
 U p ​ ( x , y ) = { L ​ Re ⁡ ( | x | + i ​ y ) p , | y | ≤ | x | / c , F p ​ ( x , y ) , | y | ≥ | x | / c . U_{p}(x,y)=\begin{cases}L\operatorname{Re}(|x|+iy)^{p},&|y|\leq|x|/c,\\
F_{p}(x,y),&|y|\geq|x|/c.\end{cases} 
 
 
 For  1 < p < 2 1<p<2 , set  β = π / ( 2 ​ p ) \beta=\pi/(2p) ,  c = tan ⁡ β = C p c=\tan\beta=C_{p} ,
 M = sin p − 1 ⁡ β / cos ⁡ β M=\sin^{p-1}\beta/\cos\beta , and define 
 

 
 (A.2) 
 
 U p ​ ( x , y ) = { F p ​ ( x , y ) , | y | ≤ | x | / c , − M ​ Re ⁡ ( | y | + i ​ x ) p , | y | ≥ | x | / c . U_{p}(x,y)=\begin{cases}F_{p}(x,y),&|y|\leq|x|/c,\\
-M\operatorname{Re}(|y|+ix)^{p},&|y|\geq|x|/c.\end{cases} 
 
 
 At  p = 2 p=2 , take  U 2 = x 2 − y 2 U_{2}=x^{2}-y^{2} . 
 
 
 Proposition A.1 . 
 
 The function  U p U_{p}  is an admissible superharmonic majorant of  F p F_{p} , even in each
coordinate. Moreover  ∂ y 2 U p ≤ 0 \partial_{y}^{2}U_{p}\leq 0  distributionally and
 U p ​ ( z ) ≤ 0 U_{p}(z)\leq 0  exactly when  | Re ⁡ z | ≤ C p ​ | Im ⁡ z | |\operatorname{Re}z|\leq C_{p}|\operatorname{Im}z| .
Consequently, for every real  K ≥ C p K\geq C_{p} , 
 

 
 
 U p , K ​ ( x , y ) = U p ​ ( x , ( K / C p ) ​ y ) U_{p,K}(x,y)=U_{p}(x,(K/C_{p})y) 
 
 
 is an admissible superharmonic majorant of  | x | p − K p ​ | y | p |x|^{p}-K^{p}|y|^{p} ,
nonpositive exactly on  Σ p , K \Sigma_{p,K} . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 For  p ≠ 2 p\neq 2 , write  ϑ = π / ( 2 ​ p ) \vartheta=\pi/(2p) ,  b = cot ⁡ ϑ b=\cot\vartheta ,
 L = cos p − 1 ⁡ ϑ / sin ⁡ ϑ L=\cos^{p-1}\vartheta/\sin\vartheta , and
 E p ​ ( t ) = L ​ Re ⁡ ( 1 + i ​ t ) p − 1 + b p ​ t p E_{p}(t)=L\operatorname{Re}(1+it)^{p}-1+b^{p}t^{p}  on  0 ≤ t ≤ tan ⁡ ϑ 0\leq t\leq\tan\vartheta .
For  φ = arctan ⁡ t \varphi=\arctan t , 
 

 
 
 q ⁡ ( φ ) \displaystyle q(\varphi) 
 = sin ⁡ ( ( p − 1 ) ​ φ ) sin p − 1 ⁡ φ , \displaystyle=\frac{\sin((p-1)\varphi)}{\sin^{p-1}\varphi}, 
 q ′ ​ ( φ ) \displaystyle q^{\prime}(\varphi) 
 = − ( p − 1 ) ​ sin ⁡ ( ( p − 2 ) ​ φ ) sin p ⁡ φ , \displaystyle=-\frac{(p-1)\sin((p-2)\varphi)}{\sin^{p}\varphi}, 
 
 
 
 E p ′ ​ ( t ) \displaystyle E_{p}^{\prime}(t) 
 = p ​ t p − 1 ​ ( b p − L ​ q ​ ( φ ) ) , \displaystyle=pt^{p-1}(b^{p}-Lq(\varphi)), 
 q ⁡ ( ϑ ) \displaystyle q(\vartheta) 
 = b p / L . \displaystyle=b^{p}/L. 
 
 
 Since  | ( p − 2 ) ​ φ | < π / 2 |(p-2)\varphi|<\pi/2 ,
 sgn ⁡ ( p − 2 ) ​ E p ′ ≤ 0 \operatorname{sgn}(p-2)E_{p}^{\prime}\leq 0 , and the endpoint identities
 E p ​ ( tan ⁡ ϑ ) = E p ′ ​ ( tan ⁡ ϑ ) = 0 E_{p}(\tan\vartheta)=E_{p}^{\prime}(\tan\vartheta)=0  imply
 sgn ⁡ ( p − 2 ) ​ E p ≥ 0 \operatorname{sgn}(p-2)E_{p}\geq 0 .
On the harmonic sector the difference  U p − F p U_{p}-F_{p}  is
 | x | p ​ E p ​ ( | y | / | x | ) |x|^{p}E_{p}(|y|/|x|)  for  p > 2 p>2 , and
 − b − p ​ | y | p ​ E p ​ ( | x | / | y | ) -b^{-p}|y|^{p}E_{p}(|x|/|y|)  for  p < 2 p<2 .
Thus  U p ≥ F p U_{p}\geq F_{p} , with limiting axis values understood. 
 
 
 The endpoint identities give  h p = U p | 𝕋 ∈ C 1 ​ ( 𝕋 ) h_{p}=U_{p}|_{\mathbb{T}}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{T})  with no angular atoms;
its piecewise second derivative is  O ⁡ ( 1 + | θ − θ 0 | p − 2 ) O(1+|\theta-\theta_{0}|^{p-2})  at the axes.
Hence  h p ∈ W 2 , q ​ ( 𝕋 ) h_{p}\in W^{2,q}(\mathbb{T})  for  1 < q < 1 / ( 2 − p ) 1<q<1/(2-p)  when  p < 2 p<2 ,
and any finite  q > 1 q>1  otherwise. These choices satisfy  q ⁡ ( p − 2 ) + 2 > 0 q(p-2)+2>0 ;
Lemma  3.2  supplies the planar regularity.
On harmonic sectors  Δ ​ U p = 0 \Delta U_{p}=0 . Elsewhere 
 

 
 
 Δ ​ U p = p ⁡ ( p − 1 ) ​ ( | x | p − 2 − c p ​ | y | p − 2 ) ≤ 0 : \Delta U_{p}=p(p-1)(|x|^{p-2}-c^{p}|y|^{p-2})\leq 0: 
 
 
 for  p > 2 p>2  use  | y | ≥ | x | / c |y|\geq|x|/c , and for  p < 2 p<2  use  | y | ≤ | x | / c |y|\leq|x|/c 
with the negative exponent. In either case the second term is at
least  c 2 ​ | x | p − 2 c^{2}|x|^{p-2}  and  c ≥ 1 c\geq 1 .
At  p = 2 p=2 ,  U 2 U_{2}  is harmonic and  ∂ y 2 U 2 = − 2 \partial_{y}^{2}U_{2}=-2 . The sign description follows from the
cosine on the harmonic sector and from  F p F_{p}  on its complement.
On contact sectors,  ∂ y 2 U p = − C p p ​ p ​ ( p − 1 ) ​ | y | p − 2 ≤ 0 \partial_{y}^{2}U_{p}=-C_{p}^{p}p(p-1)|y|^{p-2}\leq 0 .
On harmonic sectors the same derivative is
 − L ​ p ​ ( p − 1 ) ​ Re ⁡ z p − 2 -Lp(p-1)\operatorname{Re}z^{p-2}  for  p > 2 p>2 , with  z = | x | + i ​ y z=|x|+iy , and
 − M ​ p ​ ( p − 1 ) ​ Re ⁡ z p − 2 -Mp(p-1)\operatorname{Re}z^{p-2}  for  p < 2 p<2 , with  z = | y | + i ​ x z=|y|+ix .
In both cases  | arg ⁡ z | ≤ π / ( 2 ​ p ) |\arg z|\leq\pi/(2p)  and
 | ( p − 2 ) ​ arg ⁡ z | < π / 2 |(p-2)\arg z|<\pi/2 , so the derivative is nonpositive.
The established  C 1 C^{1}  and Sobolev regularity excludes singular
interface terms. For  t = K / C p ≥ 1 t=K/C_{p}\geq 1 , almost everywhere, 
 

 
 
 Δ ⁡ [ U p ​ ( x , t ​ y ) ] = ( Δ ​ U p ) ​ ( x , t ​ y ) + ( t 2 − 1 ) ​ ( ∂ y 2 U p ) ​ ( x , t ​ y ) ≤ 0 . \Delta[U_{p}(x,ty)]=(\Delta U_{p})(x,ty)+(t^{2}-1)(\partial_{y}^{2}U_{p})(x,ty)\leq 0. 
 
 
 Linear change of variables preserves the regularity, and the
majorization and sign assertions follow directly from those of  U p U_{p} .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Consequently ( 8.7 ) has exactly the two remainder terms
in ( 2.23 ), with no further operator argument.
Its spectral correction can be dropped when  spec ⁡ T ⊂ Σ p \operatorname{spec}T\subset\Sigma_{p} ,
or whenever the signed eigenvalue sum is nonpositive.
The Laplacian vanishes on a full sector, so positivity of  Ψ T \Psi_{T} 
alone gives no uniform stability lower bound. 
 
 
 
 Appendix B  Weyl comparison and normalization 

 
 The spectral transfer theorem in Section  2  also identifies
a compactly supported Weyl distribution and quantifies its difference
from the eigenvalue measure. We record those consequences and the
normalizations used in their potential-theoretic interpretation. 
 
 
 Example B.1 . 
 
 For scalar  T = a + i ​ b T=a+ib  one can take
 μ a , b = ∫ 0 1 ( 1 − r ) ​ r − 1 ​ δ ( r ​ a , r ​ b ) ​ 𝑑 r \mu_{a,b}=\int_{0}^{1}(1-r)r^{-1}\delta_{(ra,rb)}\,\mathrm{d}r  on
 ℝ 2 ∖ { 0 } \mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\} . Its positive radial moments are finite, whereas
its mass is infinite if  ( a , b ) ≠ ( 0 , 0 ) (a,b)\neq(0,0) .
For  T = 1 T=1 , reflection of this measure preserves every absolute directional
moment but changes  ∫ x ​ 𝑑 μ \int x\,\mathrm{d}\mu  from  1 / 2 1/2  to  − 1 / 2 -1/2 .
Thus the full ridge identity, not just power moments, is necessary. 
 
 
 
 The distribution below is the scalar trace of Anderson’s Weyl
functional calculus  [ 4 ,  5 ] , characterized by
its Fourier transform  tr ⁡ e i ⁡ ( ξ 1 ​ A + ξ 2 ​ B ) \operatorname{tr}e^{i(\xi_{1}A+\xi_{2}B)} . Its relation to
tracial joint spectral measures is discussed in
 [ 3 , Sections 0.1.1–0.1.3] . The corollary records
its radial reconstruction from  μ A , B \mu_{A,B}  and its comparison with
the eigenvalue measure through  Ψ T \Psi_{T} . 
 
 
 Corollary B.2 . 
 
 With  𝒬 = ℰ ⁡ ( ℰ + 1 ) \mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{E}+1) , 
 

 
 (B.1) 
 
 ⟨ 𝔚 T , g ⟩ = d ​ g ​ ( 0 ) + ∫ 𝒬 ​ g ​ d ​ μ A , B \langle\mathfrak{W}_{T},g\rangle=dg(0)+\int\mathcal{Q}g\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{A,B} 
 
 
 defines a real compactly supported distribution of order at most two,
with mass  d d  and directional spectral marginals; in particular
 ⟨ 𝔚 T , e i ⁡ ( ξ 1 ​ x + ξ 2 ​ y ) ⟩ = tr ⁡ e i ⁡ ( ξ 1 ​ A + ξ 2 ​ B ) \langle\mathfrak{W}_{T},e^{i(\xi_{1}x+\xi_{2}y)}\rangle=\operatorname{tr}e^{i(\xi_{1}A+\xi_{2}B)} .
For smooth real  g g  near all the supports, 
 

 
 (B.2) 
 
 ⟨ 𝔚 T , g ⟩ − ∑ j g ⁡ ( λ j ​ ( T ) ) \displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{W}_{T},g\rangle-\sum_{j}g(\lambda_{j}(T)) 
 = 1 2 ​ π ​ ∫ Ψ T ​ Δ ​ g ​ d ​ m 2 , \displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\Psi_{T}\Delta g\,\mathrm{d}m_{2}, 
 
 
 (B.3) 
 
 Δ ​ Ψ T \displaystyle\Delta\Psi_{T} 
 = 2 π ( 𝔚 T − ∑ j δ λ j ​ ( T ) ) , ( log | ⋅ | ) ∗ 𝔚 T = L T . \displaystyle=2\pi\left(\mathfrak{W}_{T}-\sum_{j}\delta_{\lambda_{j}(T)}\right),\qquad(\log|\cdot|)*\mathfrak{W}_{T}=L_{T}. 
 
 
 Furthermore, 
 

 
 (B.4) 
 
 ∫ ℂ Ψ T ​ d ​ m 2 = π 2 ​ ( ‖ T ‖ 2 2 − ∑ j | λ j ​ ( T ) | 2 ) . \int_{\mathbb{C}}\Psi_{T}\,\mathrm{d}m_{2}=\frac{\pi}{2}\left(\|T\|_{2}^{2}-\sum_{j}|\lambda_{j}(T)|^{2}\right). 
 
 
 Thus  Ψ T = 0 \Psi_{T}=0  almost everywhere if and only if  T T  is normal. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Since  𝒬 ​ g = z 𝖳 ​ D 2 ​ g ​ z + 2 ​ z ⋅ ∇ g \mathcal{Q}g=z^{\mathsf{T}}D^{2}g\,z+2z\cdot\nabla g ,
the first two radial moments give the order and support of  𝔚 T \mathfrak{W}_{T} .
The right-inverse calculation in Lemma  2.3 
and the ridge identity give its mass and directional marginals.
Equation ( B.2 ) is exactly
( 2.16 ). Its distributional form gives the first
identity in ( B.3 ); convolution with
 log | ⋅ | \log|\cdot|  and compact support of  Ψ T \Psi_{T}  give the second. 
 
 
 For  g ⁡ ( z ) = | z | 2 g(z)=|z|^{2} , one has  𝒬 ​ g = 6 ​ g \mathcal{Q}g=6g  and
 ∫ 𝒬 ​ g ​ d ​ μ A , B = tr ⁡ ( A 2 + B 2 ) = ‖ T ‖ 2 2 \int\mathcal{Q}g\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{A,B}=\operatorname{tr}(A^{2}+B^{2})=\|T\|_{2}^{2} 
by ( 2.2 ). Since  Δ ​ g = 4 \Delta g=4 ,
( 2.16 ) proves ( B.4 ).
Schur triangularization identifies this spectral difference with the
squared Hilbert–Schmidt norm of the strictly upper-triangular part;
together with  Ψ T ≥ 0 \Psi_{T}\geq 0 , this proves the normality assertion.
∎ 
 
 
 
 For a finite matrix, write 
 

 
 (B.5) 
 
 δ 2 ​ ( T ) 2 = ‖ T ‖ 2 2 − ∑ j | λ j ​ ( T ) | 2 . \delta_{2}(T)^{2}=\|T\|_{2}^{2}-\sum_{j}|\lambda_{j}(T)|^{2}. 
 
 
 The number  δ 2 ​ ( T ) \delta_{2}(T)  is Henrici’s departure from normality
 [ 8 ] . Thus ( B.4 ) represents its square as
the mass of a nonnegative potential supported in the numerical range. 
 
 
 Corollary B.3 . 
 
 For a finite matrix  T T  and every real-valued
 g ∈ C ∞ ​ ( ℂ ) g\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}) , 
 

 
 (B.6) 
 
 | ⟨ 𝔚 T , g ⟩ − ∑ j g ⁡ ( λ j ​ ( T ) ) | ≤ δ 2 ​ ( T ) 2 4 ​ sup w ∈ W ⁡ ( T ) | Δ ​ g ​ ( w ) | . \left|\langle\mathfrak{W}_{T},g\rangle-\sum_{j}g(\lambda_{j}(T))\right|\leq\frac{\delta_{2}(T)^{2}}{4}\sup_{w\in W(T)}|\Delta g(w)|. 
 
 
 The numerical factor  1 / 4 1/4  is optimal. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 By ( B.2 ), nonnegativity of  Ψ T \Psi_{T} , and its
support property ( 2.10 ), the left side
is at most
 ( 2 ​ π ) − 1 ​ sup W ⁡ ( T ) | Δ ​ g | ​ ∫ Ψ T ​ d ​ m 2 (2\pi)^{-1}\sup_{W(T)}|\Delta g|\int\Psi_{T}\,\mathrm{d}m_{2} .
Equation ( B.4 ) gives ( B.6 ).
For  g ⁡ ( z ) = | z | 2 g(z)=|z|^{2} , one has  Δ ​ g = 4 \Delta g=4  and the difference is
 δ 2 ​ ( T ) 2 \delta_{2}(T)^{2} . Any nonnormal matrix therefore proves optimality.
∎ 
 
 
 
 The comparison concerns a distribution and the eigenvalue measure;
it does not assert positivity of  𝔚 T \mathfrak{W}_{T} . 
 
 
 Corollary B.4 . 
 
 For a finite matrix  T = A + i ​ B T=A+iB , put
 K T = { r w : 0 ≤ r ≤ 1 , w ∈ W ⁡ ( T ) ¯ } K_{T}=\{rw:0\leq r\leq 1,\ w\in\overline{W(T)}\} .
Let  D D  be as in
Theorem  2.4 . Every real-valued smooth  f f  on  D D 
with  f ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 f(0)=0  satisfies 
 

 
 (B.7) 
 
 | ∑ j ( ℋ ​ f ) ​ ( λ j ​ ( T ) ) − ∫ f ​ d ​ μ A , B | ≤ δ 2 ​ ( T ) 2 24 ​ sup w ∈ K T | Δ ​ f ​ ( w ) | . \left|\sum_{j}(\mathcal{H}f)(\lambda_{j}(T))-\int f\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{A,B}\right|\leq\frac{\delta_{2}(T)^{2}}{24}\sup_{w\in K_{T}}|\Delta f(w)|. 
 
 
 The numerical factor  1 / 24 1/24  is optimal. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Since  D D  is centered at zero and contains  supp ⁡ μ A , B \operatorname{supp}\mu_{A,B} ,
( 2.6 ) implies  K T ⊂ D K_{T}\subset D .
Apply Corollary  B.3  to  g = ℋ ​ f g=\mathcal{H}f , with an outer
cutoff if necessary. Since  g ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 g(0)=0  and  𝒬 ​ g = f \mathcal{Q}g=f ,
( B.1 ) identifies its defect with minus the radial defect.
The identity  Δ ​ g ​ ( w ) = ∫ 0 1 r ⁡ ( 1 − r ) ​ Δ ​ f ​ ( r ​ w ) ​ 𝑑 r \Delta g(w)=\int_{0}^{1}r(1-r)\Delta f(rw)\,\mathrm{d}r  gives
 sup W ⁡ ( T ) | Δ ​ g | ≤ 1 6 ​ sup K T | Δ ​ f | \sup_{W(T)}|\Delta g|\leq\frac{1}{6}\sup_{K_{T}}|\Delta f| .
Thus  1 / 24 = ( 1 / 4 ) ​ ( 1 / 6 ) 1/24=(1/4)(1/6) ; the quadratic test  f ⁡ ( z ) = | z | 2 f(z)=|z|^{2}  gives
equality for every nonnormal  T T  and proves optimality.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Example B.5 . 
 
 Let  N = ( 0 2 0 0 ) N=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&2\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right) .
For  E = N − w ​ I E=N-wI  the matrix  ( w / w ¯ ) ​ E − 1 ​ E ∗ (w/\bar{w})E^{-1}E^{*} 
has determinant one and trace  2 − 4 / | w | 2 2-4/|w|^{2} . The Jensen formula therefore
gives 
 

 
 
 Ψ N ​ ( w ) = { 2 ​ arcosh ⁡ ( 1 / | w | ) , 0 < | w | < 1 , 0 , | w | ≥ 1 . \Psi_{N}(w)=\begin{cases}2\operatorname{arcosh}(1/|w|),&0<|w|<1,\\
0,&|w|\geq 1.\end{cases} 
 
 
 Its mass is  2 ​ π 2\pi  by ( B.4 ), although the eigenvalue
correction is zero. Also
 ⟨ 𝔚 N , e i ​ ξ ⋅ z ⟩ = 2 cos | ξ | \langle\mathfrak{W}_{N},e^{i\xi\cdot z}\rangle=2\cos|\xi| , so 
 

 
 
 ⟨ 𝔚 N , 1 ⟩ = 2 , ⟨ 𝔚 N , | z | 2 ⟩ = 4 , ⟨ 𝔚 N , | z | 4 ⟩ = 16 / 3 . \langle\mathfrak{W}_{N},1\rangle=2,\qquad\langle\mathfrak{W}_{N},|z|^{2}\rangle=4,\qquad\langle\mathfrak{W}_{N},|z|^{4}\rangle=16/3. 
 
 
 Hence  ⟨ 𝔚 N , ( 1 − | z | 2 ) 2 ⟩ = − 2 / 3 \langle\mathfrak{W}_{N},(1-|z|^{2})^{2}\rangle=-2/3 .
A nonnegative cutoff equal to one on the support shows that  𝔚 N \mathfrak{W}_{N} 
is not positive. This nonpositivity test is the Pauli-matrix example
in  [ 3 , Section 0.1.2, Example 2] , after simultaneous
unitary conjugation and division of its quartic polynomial by six.
The calculation above also displays its nonzero positive defect
potential. Finally  ‖ N ‖ 2 = 2 \|N\|_{2}=2  but
 ‖ Re ⁡ N ‖ 2 = ‖ Im ⁡ N ‖ 2 = 2 \|\operatorname{Re}N\|_{2}=\|\operatorname{Im}N\|_{2}=\sqrt{2} :
the whole operator cannot replace its real part in ( 1.24 ). 
 
 
 
 Remark B.6 . 
 
 For this comparison only, normalize the trace by  τ = d − 1 ​ tr \tau=d^{-1}\operatorname{tr} .
The Fuglede–Kadison determinant  [ 6 ]  of an invertible matrix  S S 
is  Det τ ⁡ ( S ) = exp ⁡ τ ⁡ ( log ⁡ | S | ) = | det S | 1 / d \operatorname{Det}_{\tau}(S)=\exp\tau(\log|S|)=|\det S|^{1/d} .
The Brown measure of  T T   [ 7 ]  is the normalized eigenvalue measure
 ν T B = d − 1 ​ ∑ j δ λ j ​ ( T ) \nu_{T}^{\mathrm{B}}=d^{-1}\sum_{j}\delta_{\lambda_{j}(T)} . For
 w ∉ spec ⁡ T w\notin\operatorname{spec}T , 
 

 
 
 log ⁡ Det τ ⁡ ( T − w ​ I ) = ∫ log ⁡ | w − z | ​ d ​ ν T B ​ ( z ) . \log\operatorname{Det}_{\tau}(T-wI)=\int\log|w-z|\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{T}^{\mathrm{B}}(z). 
 
 
 By ( B.3 ), the normalized defect satisfies 
 

 
 (B.8) 
 
 1 d Ψ T = ( log | ⋅ | ) ∗ ( 1 d 𝔚 T − ν T B ) ≥ 0 almost everywhere . \frac{1}{d}\Psi_{T}=(\log|\cdot|)*\left(\frac{1}{d}\mathfrak{W}_{T}-\nu_{T}^{\mathrm{B}}\right)\geq 0\quad\text{almost everywhere}. 
 
 
 Thus the logarithmic potential of the trace Weyl distribution dominates
that of the Brown measure, although the Weyl distribution itself can
fail positivity. This gives an operator-algebraic interpretation of
the finite-dimensional spectral defect. Matsaev–Sodin already pointed
to Brown’s determinant theory as a setting for extensions of spectral
inequalities  [ 24 ] . Extending this real-directional mechanism to
a finite von Neumann algebra would require a compatible positive ridge
representation and the corresponding integrability and comparison
identities; it is a further problem beyond the real-directional matrix and compact
Schatten results proved here. 
 
 
 
 
 Appendix C  Hardy-space preliminaries and structural inputs 

 
 The conditional expectation extends contractively to every  L p L^{p} ; its trace adjoint is itself. The multiplicativity of  Φ \Phi  and  τ ∘ Φ = τ \tau\circ\Phi=\tau  show that  ℬ \mathcal{B}  is orthogonal to  ℬ 0 ∗ \mathcal{B}_{0}^{*}  in  L 2 L^{2} : if  U ∈ ℬ U\in\mathcal{B}  and  b ∈ ℬ 0 b\in\mathcal{B}_{0} , then  τ ​ b ​ U = τ ​ Φ ​ ( b ) ​ Φ ​ ( U ) = 0 \tau bU=\tau\Phi(b)\Phi(U)=0 . Consequently the decomposition of  𝒞 \mathcal{C}  is unique. 
 
 
 Moreover,  ℬ ∗ = ℬ 0 ∗ + 𝒟 \mathcal{B}^{*}=\mathcal{B}_{0}^{*}+\mathcal{D} , so  𝒞 = ℬ + ℬ ∗ \mathcal{C}=\mathcal{B}+\mathcal{B}^{*} . This core is norm dense in each  L p L^{p} ,  1 ≤ p < ∞ 1\leq p<\infty . Indeed, if a dual functional annihilates it, that functional is given by trace pairing with an element of  L p ′ ⊂ L 1 L^{p^{\prime}}\subset L^{1}  (or an element of  ℳ \mathcal{M}  for  p = 1 p=1 ), hence is normal on  ℳ \mathcal{M}  and vanishes by weak-star density. Hahn–Banach proves the claim. Taking closures gives the orthogonal decompositions 
 

 
 (C.1) 
 
 L 2 ​ ( ℳ ) = H 2 ⊕ ( H 0 2 ) ∗ , H 2 = H 0 2 ⊕ L 2 ​ ( 𝒟 ) . L^{2}(\mathcal{M})=H^{2}\oplus(H_{0}^{2})^{*},\qquad H^{2}=H_{0}^{2}\oplus L^{2}(\mathcal{D}). 
 
 
 Thus  P 2 P_{2}  is the orthogonal projection onto  H 2 H^{2} , agrees with  P P  on  𝒞 \mathcal{C} , and is self-adjoint for the trace pairing. If  g = g ∗ ∈ L 2 g=g^{*}\in L^{2} , write  g = a + a ∗ + d g=a+a^{*}+d , where  a ∈ H 0 2 a\in H_{0}^{2}  and  d = Φ ​ g = d ∗ d=\Phi g=d^{*} . Then 
 

 
 (C.2) 
 
 𝒯 ℬ ​ g := ( 2 ​ P 2 − Φ ) ​ g = 2 ​ a + d ∈ H 2 , Re ⁡ 𝒯 ℬ ​ g = g , Φ ​ 𝒯 ℬ ​ g = Φ ​ g . \mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}g:=(2P_{2}-\Phi)g=2a+d\in H^{2},\qquad\operatorname{Re}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}g=g,\qquad\Phi\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}g=\Phi g. 
 
 
 Multiplication on either side by a bounded element of  ℬ \mathcal{B}  preserves  H p H^{p} , by approximation and Hölder. 
 
 
 Products of one  H 0 2 H_{0}^{2}  element and one  H 2 H^{2}  element have zero trace, by bounded analytic approximation and Hölder. We next prove the bounded intersection identity. Put 
 

 
 
 𝒜 = H 2 ∩ ℳ = { x ∈ ℳ : τ ⁡ ( b ​ x ) = 0 ​  for every  ​ b ∈ ℬ 0 } , \mathcal{A}=H^{2}\cap\mathcal{M}=\{x\in\mathcal{M}:\tau(bx)=0\text{ for every }b\in\mathcal{B}_{0}\}, 
 
 
 where the second description follows from ( C.1 ).
These normal equations show that  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  is ultraweakly closed.
It is a unital algebra containing  ℬ \mathcal{B} : for  x , y ∈ 𝒜 x,y\in\mathcal{A} , take
 y j ∈ ℬ y_{j}\in\mathcal{B}  with  y j → y y_{j}\to y  in  L 2 L^{2} . The right  ℬ \mathcal{B}  module property
puts  x ​ y j ∈ H 2 xy_{j}\in H^{2} , and boundedness of  x x  gives  x ​ y j → x ​ y xy_{j}\to xy  in  L 2 L^{2} .
If  x ∈ 𝒜 ∩ 𝒜 ∗ x\in\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{A}^{*} , the orthogonal decomposition
forces  x ∈ L 2 ​ ( 𝒟 ) x\in L^{2}(\mathcal{D}) , hence  x = Φ ​ x ∈ 𝒟 x=\Phi x\in\mathcal{D} .
For  x , y ∈ 𝒜 x,y\in\mathcal{A} , put  x 0 = x − Φ ​ x ∈ H 0 2 x_{0}=x-\Phi x\in H_{0}^{2} . For  d ∈ 𝒟 d\in\mathcal{D} , 
 

 
 
 τ ⁡ ( d ∗ ​ x 0 ​ y ) = τ ⁡ ( x 0 ​ y ​ d ∗ ) = 0 , \tau(d^{*}x_{0}y)=\tau(x_{0}yd^{*})=0, 
 
 
 since  y ​ d ∗ ∈ H 2 yd^{*}\in H^{2} . Thus  Φ ⁡ ( x 0 ​ y ) = 0 \Phi(x_{0}y)=0 , and
 Φ ⁡ ( x ​ y ) = Φ ​ x ​ Φ ​ y \Phi(xy)=\Phi x\,\Phi y .
The inclusion  ℬ ⊂ 𝒜 \mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{A}  supplies weak-star density of
 𝒜 + 𝒜 ∗ \mathcal{A}+\mathcal{A}^{*} . Therefore  𝒜 \mathcal{A}  is a subdiagonal
algebra containing  ℬ \mathcal{B} , and maximality gives  𝒜 = ℬ \mathcal{A}=\mathcal{B} .
Finally, if  x ∈ H q ∩ ℳ x\in H^{q}\cap\mathcal{M}  with  q ≥ 1 q\geq 1 , then
 τ ⁡ ( b ​ x ) = 0 \tau(bx)=0  for every  b ∈ ℬ 0 b\in\mathcal{B}_{0} , by  L 1 L^{1}  convergence of its analytic
approximants. Since  x ∈ L 2 x\in L^{2} , orthogonality in
( C.1 ) gives  x ∈ H 2 x\in H^{2} .
We have proved 
 

 
 (C.3) 
 
 H q ∩ ℳ = ℬ , 1 ≤ q < ∞ . H^{q}\cap\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{B},\qquad 1\leq q<\infty. 
 
 
 
 
 For clarity we state the published structural inputs used in the proof. These are results about finite subdiagonal algebras, rather than assumptions about the particular ambient algebra. 
 
 
 Theorem C.1 . 
 
 
 (1)  
 
 If  w ∈ ℳ w\in\mathcal{M}  is positive and boundedly invertible, there exists  R ∈ ℬ R\in\mathcal{B}  with  R − 1 ∈ ℬ R^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}  and  R ∗ ​ R = w R^{*}R=w . 
 
 (2)  
 
 Define the Fuglede–Kadison determinant by 
 

 
 
 Det τ ⁡ ( x ) = lim ε ↓ 0 exp ⁡ ( 1 2 ​ τ ​ log ⁡ ( x ∗ ​ x + ε ​ I ) ) . \operatorname{Det}_{\tau}(x)=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\exp\bigl(\tfrac{1}{2}\tau\log(x^{*}x+\varepsilon I)\bigr). 
 
 
 It is multiplicative on bounded operators, and  Det τ ⁡ ( Φ ​ b ) ≤ Det τ ⁡ ( b ) \operatorname{Det}_{\tau}(\Phi b)\leq\operatorname{Det}_{\tau}(b)  for  b ∈ ℬ b\in\mathcal{B} . The same assertion holds in the adjoint algebra  ℬ ∗ \mathcal{B}^{*} . 
 
 (3)  
 
 Every finite trace-preserving subdiagonal algebra is maximal. 
 
 
 
 
 
 The positive factorization in (1) is Arveson’s
Corollary 4.2.4(ii)  [ 26 ] .
For (2), determinant multiplicativity and its analytic extension
to noninvertible bounded elements are recalled in
 [ 29 , Section 3] ; see also  [ 6 ] .
The general Jensen inequality, including noninvertible  b ∈ ℬ b\in\mathcal{B} ,
follows directly from  [ 32 , Corollary 3.3] ,
which applies to all  h ∈ H 1 h\in H^{1} .
Labuschagne’s Szegő theorem and Jensen formula
 [ 31 ] , together with
 [ 26 , Theorem 4.4.3] , also give the bounded Jensen
inequality. Applying it to the adjoint algebra gives the assertion
for  ℬ ∗ \mathcal{B}^{*} . Assertion (3) is Exel’s Theorem 7  [ 27 ] .
When  Det τ ⁡ ( x ) > 0 \operatorname{Det}_{\tau}(x)>0 ,
 τ ​ log ⁡ ( x ∗ ​ x ) = 2 ​ log ⁡ Det τ ⁡ ( x ) \tau\log(x^{*}x)=2\log\operatorname{Det}_{\tau}(x)  is a finite
spectral integral, even if  x x  is not boundedly invertible.
We use no inverse of  x x  in that case. 
 
 
 
 Appendix D  Analytic regularization and its nonsharp background 

 
 The operator  𝒯 ℬ \mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}  and its real-part identities are given in ( C.2 ). Write
 ℋ ℬ = − i ⁡ ( 𝒯 ℬ − I ) \mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}=-i(\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}-I)  for conjugation. The three orthogonal components in ( C.1 ) give 
 

 
 
 ℋ ℬ 2 = − I + Φ , Φ ​ ℋ ℬ = 0 , ‖ ℋ ℬ ​ x ‖ 2 ≤ ‖ x ‖ 2 . \mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{2}=-I+\Phi,\qquad\Phi\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}=0,\qquad\|\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}x\|_{2}\leq\|x\|_{2}. 
 
 
 For self-adjoint  g g ,  ℋ ℬ ​ g \mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}g  is self-adjoint and  𝒯 ℬ ​ g = g + i ​ ℋ ℬ ​ g \mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}g=g+i\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}g . The following auxiliary estimates justify analytic regularization
independently of the sharp theorem. The moment estimate first puts
the powers of the canonical element in  H 1 H^{1} . Yosida approximation
then identifies their exponential series with the contraction
semigroup, establishing analytic resolvent membership before any
sharp  L p L^{p}  estimate is used. 
 
 
 D.1.  A nonsharp moment estimate used only for regularization 

 
 Lemma D.1 . 
 
 For dyadic  q = 2 n q=2^{n} ,  n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 , the conjugation satisfies 
 

 
 
 ‖ ℋ ℬ ​ u ‖ q ≤ ( q − 1 ) ​ ‖ u ‖ q ( u = u ∗ ∈ L q ) . \|\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}u\|_{q}\leq(q-1)\|u\|_{q}\qquad(u=u^{*}\in L^{q}). 
 
 
 Consequently, for bounded  g = g ∗ ∈ ℳ g=g^{*}\in\mathcal{M}  and  h = 𝒯 ℬ ​ g h=\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}g ,
 h ∈ H q h\in H^{q}  at every dyadic exponent, and for every integer  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 , 
 

 
 (D.1) 
 
 h k ∈ H 1 , ‖ h k ‖ 1 ≤ ( 2 ​ k ​ ‖ g ‖ ∞ ) k . h^{k}\in H^{1},\qquad\|h^{k}\|_{1}\leq(2k\|g\|_{\infty})^{k}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The  q = 2 q=2  bound was proved above. Assume a bound with constant  C q C_{q} 
on self-adjoint core elements. For  u = u ∗ ∈ 𝒞 u=u^{*}\in\mathcal{C} , let
 v = ℋ ℬ ​ u v=\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}u ,  d = Φ ​ u d=\Phi u  and  f = u + i ​ v = 𝒯 ℬ ​ u ∈ ℬ f=u+iv=\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}u\in\mathcal{B} .
Then  Φ ​ f = d \Phi f=d  is self-adjoint. Since 
 

 
 
 f 2 = u 2 − v 2 + i ⁡ ( u ​ v + v ​ u ) , Φ ⁡ ( f 2 ) = d 2 , f^{2}=u^{2}-v^{2}+i(uv+vu),\qquad\Phi(f^{2})=d^{2}, 
 
 
 conjugation of its real part yields
 ℋ ℬ ​ ( u 2 − v 2 ) = u ​ v + v ​ u \mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}(u^{2}-v^{2})=uv+vu . Applying  ℋ ℬ \mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}  once more gives 
 

 
 (D.2) 
 
 v 2 = u 2 + ℋ ℬ ​ ( u ​ v + v ​ u ) − d 2 . v^{2}=u^{2}+\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}(uv+vu)-d^{2}. 
 
 
 Both  u 2 − v 2 u^{2}-v^{2}  and  u ​ v + v ​ u uv+vu  belong to the bounded core, as the real and
imaginary parts of the analytic element  f 2 f^{2} .
Contractivity of conditional expectation in  L 2 ​ q L^{2q} ,
( D.2 ), and Hölder give 
 

 
 
 ‖ v ‖ 2 ​ q 2 ≤ 2 ​ ‖ u ‖ 2 ​ q 2 + 2 ​ C q ​ ‖ u ‖ 2 ​ q ​ ‖ v ‖ 2 ​ q . \|v\|_{2q}^{2}\leq 2\|u\|_{2q}^{2}+2C_{q}\|u\|_{2q}\|v\|_{2q}. 
 
 
 Solving the quadratic gives  C 2 ​ q ≤ C q + C q 2 + 2 C_{2q}\leq C_{q}+\sqrt{C_{q}^{2}+2} .
If  C q ≤ q − 1 C_{q}\leq q-1  and  q ≥ 2 q\geq 2 , then 
 

 
 
 C 2 ​ q ≤ q − 1 + ( q − 1 ) 2 + 2 ≤ 2 ​ q − 1 . C_{2q}\leq q-1+\sqrt{(q-1)^{2}+2}\leq 2q-1. 
 
 
 Induction proves the stated bounds on the self-adjoint core.
Core density extends them to self-adjoint  L q L^{q} , consistently with
 ℋ ℬ \mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}  on  L 2 L^{2}  because  q ≥ 2 q\geq 2 .
Taking self-adjoint core approximants to  g g  in  L q L^{q}  proves
 h = 𝒯 ℬ ​ g ∈ H q h=\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}g\in H^{q} , with  ‖ h ‖ q ≤ q ​ ‖ g ‖ q \|h\|_{q}\leq q\|g\|_{q} .
Choose a dyadic  q ≥ k q\geq k  with  q ≤ 2 ​ k q\leq 2k , allowing  q = 2 q=2  for  k = 1 k=1 .
Then  ‖ h ‖ k ≤ 2 ​ k ​ ‖ g ‖ ∞ \|h\|_{k}\leq 2k\|g\|_{\infty} , and Hölder gives the norm estimate
in ( D.1 ). Products of bounded analytic
approximants converging to  h h  in  L q L^{q}  converge to  h k h^{k}  in
 L q / k ⊂ L 1 L^{q/k}\subset L^{1} , proving  h k ∈ H 1 h^{k}\in H^{1} .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 D.2.  Bounded analytic accretive approximants: a proof 

 
 We use the standard maximally accretive semigroup theorem in its usual
operator form: a maximally accretive operator  A A  generates a contraction
semigroup  e − t ​ A e^{-tA} , its resolvent is the Laplace integral of that
semigroup, and its bounded Yosida approximants
 A n = A ​ ( I + A / n ) − 1 A_{n}=A(I+A/n)^{-1}  have semigroups converging strongly to it.
These statements are the Lumer–Phillips theorem, the Laplace resolvent formula, and the Yosida approximation theorem; see  [ 30 , Theorems II.3.15, II.1.10 and Section III.4.10] . They are independent of Hardy or
Riesz bounds. The following proof establishes all the analytic
membership and approximation assertions needed here. 
 
 
 Lemma D.2 . 
 
 Let  g ∈ ℳ g\in\mathcal{M}  be bounded and positive and put  h = 𝒯 ℬ ​ g h=\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}g ,  d = Φ ​ g d=\Phi g .
For every  ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 , 
 

 
 
 R ε = ( I + ε ​ h ) − 1 ∈ ℬ , ‖ R ε ‖ ∞ ≤ 1 , R_{\varepsilon}=(I+\varepsilon h)^{-1}\in\mathcal{B},\qquad\|R_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}\leq 1, 
 
 
 and 
 

 
 
 b ε = h ​ R ε ∈ ℬ , Re ⁡ b ε ≥ 0 , Φ ​ b ε = d ​ ( I + ε ​ d ) − 1 ≥ 0 . b_{\varepsilon}=hR_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{B},\qquad\operatorname{Re}b_{\varepsilon}\geq 0,\qquad\Phi b_{\varepsilon}=d(I+\varepsilon d)^{-1}\geq 0. 
 
 
 Moreover  b ε → h b_{\varepsilon}\to h  in  L 2 L^{2} , and hence in  L s L^{s}  for
 1 < s < 2 1<s<2 , while  Re ⁡ b ε → g \operatorname{Re}b_{\varepsilon}\to g  in the same spaces. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Put  K = ℋ ℬ ​ g K=\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}g , viewed as a self-adjoint affiliated
operator, and realize  h = g + i ​ K h=g+iK  on  Dom ⁡ K \operatorname{Dom}K .
Since  g g  is bounded,  h h  is closed and  h ∗ = g − i ​ K h^{*}=g-iK  on the same
domain. Both operators are accretive. For
 λ > ‖ g ‖ ∞ \lambda>\|g\|_{\infty} , the factorization 
 

 
 
 λ + h = [ I + g ​ ( λ + i ​ K ) − 1 ] ​ ( λ + i ​ K ) \lambda+h=[I+g(\lambda+iK)^{-1}](\lambda+iK) 
 
 
 is onto by a Neumann series, since
 ‖ g ​ ( λ + i ​ K ) − 1 ‖ ≤ ‖ g ‖ ∞ / λ < 1 \|g(\lambda+iK)^{-1}\|\leq\|g\|_{\infty}/\lambda<1 .
Thus  h h , and likewise  h ∗ h^{*} , is maximally accretive.
Affiliation and uniqueness of the generated semigroup imply that
its resolvents and contraction semigroup commute with every
unitary in the commutant and hence belong to  ℳ \mathcal{M} .
In particular,  ‖ ( I + ε ​ h ) − 1 ‖ ≤ 1 \|(I+\varepsilon h)^{-1}\|\leq 1 . 
 
 
 We prove that its contraction semigroup  S t = e − t ​ h S_{t}=e^{-th}  lies in  ℬ \mathcal{B} .
By ( D.1 ), for
 0 < t < ( 2 ​ e ​ ‖ g ‖ ∞ ) − 1 0<t<(2e\|g\|_{\infty})^{-1}  the formal series 
 

 
 
 ∑ k = 0 ∞ ( − t ) k ​ h k k ! \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-t)^{k}h^{k}}{k!} 
 
 
 converges in  H 1 H^{1} : the estimate  k ! ≥ ( k / e ) k k!\geq(k/e)^{k}  makes its norm
majorant geometric. If  g = 0 g=0 , the assertion is immediate instead.
To identify the series, let  R n = ( I + h / n ) − 1 R_{n}=(I+h/n)^{-1}  and  h n = h ​ R n h_{n}=hR_{n} .
The bounded Yosida semigroups  e − t ​ h n e^{-th_{n}}  converge strongly to  S t S_{t} 
and are contractions. Resolvents commute with  h h  on its domain, so
in the algebra of measurable operators 
 

 
 
 h n k = h k ​ R n k , ‖ h n k ‖ 1 ≤ ‖ h k ‖ 1 . h_{n}^{k}=h^{k}R_{n}^{k},\qquad\|h_{n}^{k}\|_{1}\leq\|h^{k}\|_{1}. 
 
 
 Both  h h  and  h ∗ h^{*}  are maximally accretive, so  R n → I R_{n}\to I 
strongly and  R n ∗ → I R_{n}^{*}\to I  strongly. Therefore bounded
multiplier convergence gives  h n k → h k h_{n}^{k}\to h^{k}  in  L 1 L^{1}  for every fixed
 k k . This multiplier convergence follows directly by truncating a
fixed  L 1 L^{1}  element to bounded operators and using the finite trace.
Dominated convergence of the exponential series therefore gives
convergence in  L 1 L^{1}  to the displayed  H 1 H^{1}  series. Strong convergence
of the uniformly bounded semigroups, applied to the identity vector
in the standard  L 2 L^{2}  representation, also gives convergence in
 L 2 L^{2}  and hence  L 1 L^{1}  to  S t S_{t} . Consequently  S t ∈ H 1 ∩ ℳ = ℬ S_{t}\in H^{1}\cap\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{B} 
for small positive  t t . The semigroup law then yields  S t ∈ ℬ S_{t}\in\mathcal{B}  for
all  t > 0 t>0 . 
 
 
 The resolvent formula 
 

 
 
 R ε = ∫ 0 ∞ e − t ​ S ε ​ t ​ 𝑑 t R_{\varepsilon}=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t}S_{\varepsilon t}\,dt 
 
 
 now shows  R ε ∈ ℬ R_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{B} , since  ℬ \mathcal{B}  is ultraweakly closed and
the integrand has norm at most  e − t e^{-t} .
The identity  b ε = h ​ R ε = ( I − R ε ) / ε b_{\varepsilon}=hR_{\varepsilon}=(I-R_{\varepsilon})/\varepsilon 
shows that  b ε b_{\varepsilon}  is bounded and analytic.
Multiplicativity extends to a bounded analytic factor times an  H 2 H^{2} 
factor by  L 2 L^{2}  approximation. Applying it to
 R ε ​ ( I + ε ​ h ) = I R_{\varepsilon}(I+\varepsilon h)=I  gives 
 

 
 
 Φ ​ R ε = ( I + ε ​ d ) − 1 , Φ ​ b ε = d ​ ( I + ε ​ d ) − 1 ≥ 0 . \Phi R_{\varepsilon}=(I+\varepsilon d)^{-1},\qquad\Phi b_{\varepsilon}=d(I+\varepsilon d)^{-1}\geq 0. 
 
 
 Using  ( I + ε ​ h ) ​ R ε = I (I+\varepsilon h)R_{\varepsilon}=I  and  Re ⁡ h = g \operatorname{Re}h=g  gives the bounded quadratic-form identity 
 

 
 
 Re ⁡ b ε = R ε ∗ ​ g ​ R ε + ε ​ b ε ∗ ​ b ε ≥ 0 . \operatorname{Re}b_{\varepsilon}=R_{\varepsilon}^{*}gR_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon b_{\varepsilon}^{*}b_{\varepsilon}\geq 0. 
 
 
 All terms are bounded since  g g  and  b ε b_{\varepsilon}  are bounded.
Finally  R ε , R ε ∗ → I R_{\varepsilon},R_{\varepsilon}^{*}\to I  strongly. Uniform boundedness
and truncation of  h ∈ L 2 h\in L^{2}  give  b ε = h ​ R ε → h b_{\varepsilon}=hR_{\varepsilon}\to h 
in  L 2 L^{2} , and taking real parts gives the remaining convergence assertions.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 Appendix E  Rotations and independent coordinates 

 
 The real-pencil and joint realization theorems have further
consequences obtained by rotation and by iteration over independent
variables. We record the full statements, including one-component
Schatten membership and the canonical completely bounded norms. 
 
 
 Corollary E.1 . 
 
 Let  ( c , d ) ≠ ( 0 , 0 ) (c,d)\neq(0,0) . The optimal constant, uniform over finite
nilpotent  T = A + i ​ B T=A+iB , in
 ‖ a ​ A + b ​ B ‖ p ≤ L ​ ‖ c ​ A + d ​ B ‖ p \|aA+bB\|_{p}\leq L\|cA+dB\|_{p}  is 
 

 
 (E.1) 
 
 L = K p ​ ( a ​ c + b ​ d c 2 + d 2 , b ​ c − a ​ d c 2 + d 2 ) . L=K_{p}\left(\frac{ac+bd}{c^{2}+d^{2}},\frac{bc-ad}{c^{2}+d^{2}}\right). 
 
 
 The same bound holds for compact quasinilpotent  T ∈ 𝒮 p T\in\mathcal{S}_{p} .
It also holds if  T T  is compact and quasinilpotent and only
 c ​ A + d ​ B ∈ 𝒮 p cA+dB\in\mathcal{S}_{p}  is assumed. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Put  r = c 2 + d 2 r=\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}  and rotate  T T  so that its self-adjoint parts are
 A ′ = ( c ​ A + d ​ B ) / r A^{\prime}=(cA+dB)/r  and  B ′ = ( − d ​ A + c ​ B ) / r B^{\prime}=(-dA+cB)/r .
Then  a ​ A + b ​ B = ( ( a ​ c + b ​ d ) / r ) ​ A ′ + ( ( b ​ c − a ​ d ) / r ) ​ B ′ aA+bB=((ac+bd)/r)A^{\prime}+((bc-ad)/r)B^{\prime} .
Corollary  1.5  and homogeneity prove the formula.
The rotation bijects the class of finite nilpotents, so sharpness
transfers in both directions. If  c ​ A + d ​ B = 0 cA+dB=0  for a nilpotent  T T ,
the rotated operator is skew-Hermitian and nilpotent and hence zero.
The weaker membership assumption follows from the qualitative
Matsaev theorem  [ 21 ,  23 ,  24 ] , applied to  i i  times
the rotated operator.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Corollary E.2 . 
 
 For  1 ≤ j ≤ d 1\leq j\leq d , let  Ω j \Omega_{j}  be  ℝ \mathbb{R}  with Lebesgue measure or
 ℤ \mathbb{Z}  with counting measure. Let  𝐒 j = ( S j , k ) k = 1 m j \mathbf{S}_{j}=(S_{j,k})_{k=1}^{m_{j}} 
be respectively a joint Hilbert-pencil family or a shifted discrete
family from Theorem  1.4 , with common norm  L j L_{j} .
Put  Ω = ∏ j Ω j \Omega=\prod_{j}\Omega_{j} ,  m = ∏ j m j m=\prod_{j}m_{j} , and 
 

 
 
 𝐒 = ( ∏ j = 1 d S j , k j ( j ) ) k 1 , … , k d . \mathbf{S}=\left(\prod_{j=1}^{d}S_{j,k_{j}}^{(j)}\right)_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{d}}. 
 
 
 For every nonzero Hilbert space  𝒦 \mathcal{K} , this map from
 L p ​ ( Ω , 𝒮 p ​ ( 𝒦 ) ) L^{p}(\Omega;\mathcal{S}_{p}(\mathcal{K}))  to
 ℓ p m ​ ( L p ​ ( Ω , 𝒮 p ​ ( 𝒦 ) ) ) \ell_{p}^{m}(L^{p}(\Omega;\mathcal{S}_{p}(\mathcal{K})))  has norm  ∏ j L j \prod_{j}L_{j} .
Its scalar and canonical completely bounded scalar norms have the
same value. For unweighted single-output families,
 L j = K p ​ ( a j , b j ) L_{j}=K_{p}(a_{j},b_{j})  on  ℝ \mathbb{R}  and
 L j = K p ​ ( cos ⁡ ( π ​ α j ) , sin ⁡ ( π ​ α j ) ) L_{j}=K_{p}(\cos(\pi\alpha_{j}),\sin(\pi\alpha_{j}))  on  ℤ \mathbb{Z} . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Apply each one-coordinate estimate to the corresponding slices,
and sum the other coordinates and output indices by Fubini–Tonelli.
Iteration gives the upper product bound, equally for mixed discrete
and continuous coordinates. Products of scalar approximate extremizers,
multiplied by a fixed rank-one projection, give the lower bound because
both input and joint output norms factor. A zero factor is immediate.
The same estimates hold for every  𝒮 p n \mathcal{S}_{p}^{n}  coefficient space, so
Lemma  4.6  gives the complete norm equality.
∎
